- **What the fence does not stop, named rather than implied.** Two gaps, both explicit in `docs/fence-notes.md` rather than left for someone to discover: (1) the PATH fix only removes the two rungs `augmented_path()` itself computes — the *inherited* tail (whatever PATH the app's own process already had) is untouched, so a worker launched from a terminal whose shell profile already exported `~/.local/bin` can still reach it there; stripping the inherited tail is the sandboxing decision the spec rules out by name. (2) non-`HOME`-keyed ambient credentials (`SSH_AUTH_SOCK` chief among them) are untouched — `~/.ssh` stops resolving by path, but an agent socket still lets `git+ssh` authenticate as the operator. Both are named in the notes doc rather than fixed, because fixing either is the deferred Posture Ladder, not this package.
- **The falsification test and the WP-06 debt, both run live rather than reasoned about.** `ls ~/.ssh` under a worker's exact fenced env (`env -i HOME=<private dir> PATH=... sh -c ...`) fails by path resolution — ordinary "No such file or directory," not a permission error. Separately, a real `git commit` under the same fenced env with no `-c user.*` override succeeded and attributed correctly from the seed, and a second worker's `git diff HEAD...fleet/worker-1` from its own (also fenced) worktree reproduced `peer-review-notes.md`'s original clean three-dot diff with no fetch and no shared checkout — confirming the worktree mechanism `peer-review-notes.md` measured does not route through `HOME` at all, so fencing it does not disturb peer review. Zero live DeepSeek probes: the overnight brief allowed up to two if a real-`claude` confirmation would add information over the env-level proof, and it would not have — `claude`'s pty resolves `~` and `$HOME` through the same OS call a plain `sh` does, so the shell-level proof is the whole proof.
- **What would reverse it.** Live evidence that a worker legitimately needs `gh` or a version manager mid-task — the fix is scoping and seeding that tool's config for the worker specifically (a `decisions.md` entry naming what got added and why), not loosening `HOME` generally. Evidence that the inherited-PATH gap or the `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` gap is being exploited or relied on in practice is what would justify building the Posture Ladder this package deliberately left un-built.
- **Verified:** 186 workspace tests unchanged and green (WP-08 touched no core crate); 92 shell tests green — 81 lib (`cargo test` in `src-tauri`, including 8 new fence tests: private-HOME wiring, worker-vs-orch PATH, gitconfig seeding and its idempotency, `[fence] posture` parsing and its fallback) plus the 11 real-pty tests in `panes.rs`, all previously-passing tests untouched including the L1/L2 pins (`a_virgin_config_dir_gets_the_two_keys...`, `a_worker_carries_the_auth_token_and_never_the_api_key`). `tsc --noEmit` and `vite build` clean — no UI or prompt-template files touched, so `brief-cost` stayed at the spec's own `0`.

## D-053 — WP-09 (budget half): the ledger closes, a Tier 2 cap is set slightly above measured reality, and a conservative prune recovers what little was left

- **The ledger, reconciled.** Every `brief-cost` line the roadmap wrote before the prose existed, against what `examples/system-prompt-spike/count.py` actually measured (DeepSeek Flash tokenizer):

  | Package | Budgeted (brief-cost) | orch Δ | worker Δ | combined Δ | actual ÷ budgeted | orch running | worker running |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | WP-02 (baseline) | — | — | — | — | — | 1,637 | 1,115 |
  | WP-03 (D-045) | ~120 | +248 | +228 | +476 | ~4.0× | 1,885 | 1,343 |
  | WP-04 | ~30 | +25 | +0 | +25 | ~0.8× | 1,910 | 1,343 |
  | WP-05 (D-047) | ~150 | +318 | +248 | +566 | ~3.8× | 2,228 | 1,591 |
  | WP-06 (D-050) | ~150 | +314 | +376 | +690 | ~4.6× | 2,542 | 1,967 |
  | WP-07 (D-051) | ~60 (worker) | +126 | +135 | +261 | ~2.25× vs. its worker-only budget | 2,668 | 2,102 |
  | **Sum, 03–07** | **510** | **+1,031** | **+987** | **+2,018** | **~4.0× overall** | | |
  | WP-09 prune | (negative, unbudgeted) | −23 | −3 | −26 | — | **2,645** | **2,099** |

  The sum of the five `brief-cost` guesses (510 combined) against the sum of what those five packages actually spent (2,018 combined) reconciles to **~4.0× overall** — one number, not five separate overruns. WP-04, the one package that added no prose worth calling protocol (a counter Notice line), is the sole outlier under budget. The other four all added a verb or a protocol (cmd, task, done+review+merge, operator), and every one of those landed between 3.8× and 4.6×, confirming Q-2/Q-3's reading: **a `brief-cost` guess written before the prose is fiction; a verb costs what its rules cost, not what its syntax costs.**
- **Contextualized, not just counted.** Post-prune: orch **2,645** tokens, worker **2,099** tokens.
  - Against `WORKER_WINDOW_TOKENS = 128_000` (D-046, the number a worker's own context gauge is measured against): the worker's whole system prompt is **1.64%** of its context window before a single message arrives. Even the larger orch figure, held to the same yardstick for scale, is **2.07%**. Neither figure is close to a window that would visibly crowd out real work.
  - Against the **1,485-token** block of Claude Code's own guidance that `--system-prompt` stopped sending (D-043, the WP-02 baseline point): orch is **1,160 tokens past parity** (1.78×) and worker is **614 tokens past parity** (1.41×). This is the number Q-3 flagged as "orch ~1,050 past parity" back at WP-06 and asked whether it was a problem.
  - **Verdict, stated in measurable terms rather than vibes:** nobody has measured a behavioral cost from either figure — no worker has been observed truncating, ignoring, or mishandling any brief content because of its size, and the live shakedown (this package's other half, operator-gated) is the first place such a cost could show up. What *is* measurable is that the prompt occupies under 2.1% of the budget it has to share with an entire task, and the "past CC parity" comparison is a reference point, not a demonstrated regression — CC's own 1,485-token guidance was calibrated for a generic coding agent, not a fleet member with a verb list, a task board and a review protocol to learn. On the numbers alone: **not a problem today; worth capping so it cannot become one silently.**
- **The Tier 2 cap.** `PROMPT_BUDGET_ORCH_TOKENS = 2,800`, `PROMPT_BUDGET_WORKER_TOKENS = 2,200` (DeepSeek Flash tokenizer, measured by `count.py`; not yet wired into a runtime check — see the menu doc for that as a candidate). Headroom above today's measured, post-prune reality: **+155 orch (~5.9%)**, **+101 worker (~4.8%)**. Deliberately thin, not aspirational: Q-2 and Q-3 already established there is no meaningful fat left (WP-06's own pre-measurement tightening pass recovered only 6% of *its* addition; this package's conservative pass recovered 0.5% of the *entire* prompt — see below), and no package left on the roadmap adds a verb before the live shakedown, so the likeliest next change to these files is a shakedown finding, not a feature. A cap with real headroom the next edit immediately breaks teaches nothing; a cap this close to reality catches accidental growth (a rewritten paragraph that quietly grew, a fragment edited without checking the render) while still leaving room for one small, deliberate addition without an immediate rewrite fire drill.
  - **What would reverse it.** Either direction: (1) the operator picks one of `prompt-budget-menu.md`'s cut candidates, the prompt shrinks, and the cap should come down to track it — a cap that stays high after a real cut is measuring nothing; or (2) a genuine new protocol (a verb, a new fragment) needs to land after the shakedown, in which case its own `decisions.md` entry re-measures and states the new cap explicitly, the same discipline WP-03 through WP-07 already used. A cap is not raised by editing this line without a re-measurement — that is exactly the estimate-before-prose pattern this whole ledger exists to stop.
- **The prune — conservative, and it shows.** Wording-level tightening only: redundant modifiers cut (`"their own context and their own view"` → `"...and view"`), a few `"rather than X"` clauses shortened to `"not X"` where the clause was not a pinned literal, one filler clause dropped (`"and get on with it;"`), one paraphrase shortened without changing what it says (`"say how what you built serves the part of the vision the block names"` → `"say how it serves the block's part of the vision"`). Nothing removed: every verb, every rule, the anti-amplification clause, the full delivery contract (`accepted` / `recorded` / `not a promise`), and the authority bound (`**their word is final**`) are untouched, byte-identical to before this package. Result: orch 2,668 → **2,645** (−23, −0.86%), worker 2,102 → **2,099** (−3, −0.14%); combined −26 tokens, **−0.5% of the whole prompt budget**. This is the fourth tightening pass in the ledger (after D-045's −6/−27, D-047's pass folded into its number, D-050's −21/−25) and by far the smallest — the expected shape once a prose that has already been tightened three times is tightened a fourth. It is further confirmation, not a new finding: **there is no fat left; what remains is protocol, and cutting it is the operator's menu, not a builder's edit.**
- **What the sum answers from WP-09's own performance criteria.** "Every verb still validated" — `validate_orch`/`validate_worker` both still pass on `DEFAULT_ORCH`/`DEFAULT_WORKER` (`the_baked_in_templates_validate`), and every one of the ~45 pinned-literal tests in `crates/fleetor-core/src/brief.rs` is unchanged and green — none were loosened, shortened, or removed to make room. "Both prompts ≤ cap after pruning" — 2,645 ≤ 2,800 and 2,099 ≤ 2,200, both true with the stated headroom.
- **Verified:** 186 workspace tests, 92 shell tests green (unchanged counts — this package edited only `prompts/orch.md` and `prompts/worker.md`, no Rust source). `tsc --noEmit` and `vite build` clean. `examples/system-prompt-spike/count.py` re-run before and after the prune, numbers above are its output.

## D-054 — the worker window is 500k, stated once, and Claude Code is told rather than left to guess

- **What changed.** `WORKER_WINDOW_TOKENS` (D-046's 128,000 — its own text called it a guess pending a real number) becomes **500,000**, operator-set. `spawn::worker_command` now exports `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS` from that same constant, so CC's auto-compact bookkeeping and the context gauge's denominator are one spelling that cannot diverge. Orch is untouched — its model is recognized and its window is not ours to state. The 80%-notice test now derives its fixture from the constant instead of hardcoding 80% of the old one. Pinned by `a_worker_is_told_the_window_the_gauge_divides_by`.
- **Why the default lost.** The WP-02 finding surfaced live: CC 2.1.223 does not recognize `deepseek-v4-flash`, warned it would hold sessions to an assumed 200k, and named this exact env var as the remedy. The operator stated 500k as the window to operate to (2026-08-06).
- **What would reverse it.** A confirmed published window for the worker model that differs from 500k — change the one constant, everything follows. If a future CC recognizes the model name natively, drop the env export only after verifying CC's native number matches the constant; until then the export is the honest statement.

## D-055 — shakedown finding 1: purpose before implementation, said outright

- **What changed.** `prompts/orch.md` clause 1 ("Ask for the bigger picture") gains: a small, concrete request triggers the purpose question rather than being exempt from it — never ask *how* before you know what it is *for*. +46 tokens (orch 2,691 of the 2,800 cap, D-053).
- **Why the default lost.** First live probe of the shakedown: handed `Add a --version flag to the CLI.`, orch asked how to implement it. The clause gated on "before you decompose" and a one-liner didn't read as decomposition — the prompt asked the right question at the wrong altitude. `docs/blackboard-shakedown.md` finding 1 has the full read.
- **What would reverse it.** The re-test failing the same way — at which point the fix is not more prompt prose but reconsidering whether orch's inherited operator-level rules (plan-first posture) need a counterweight, which is an operator conversation, not an edit.

## D-056 — the tenets go in as the operator wrote them, and orch finally gets the three attitudes

- **What changed.** `prompts/vision-tenets.md` stops being WP-02's distillation (D-044) and carries the operator's essay verbatim — quotations, names and scripture included. Three kinds of line only were dropped: the source newsletter's plugs (a Vision Roadmap PDF, two podcast mentions, a YouTube pointer — materials orch cannot reach and would try to consult), and the trailing question to the builder became the directive it meant: "Make the workers want to be better — the vision is only attainable by a fleet that wants to be better." Separately, `prompts/orch.md` gains the three attitudes (be better / meaningful impact / ask for help) — the original spec said "amongst the Orchestrator and Worker" and until now only workers carried them. The distillation-asserting test is rewritten to guard the operator's decision instead; a new test pins orch's attitudes. Orch: 2,691 → **3,366 tokens**; cap restated **2,800 → 3,500** per D-053's reversal clause (re-measure and state, never silently edit). Worker unchanged at 2,099 / cap 2,200.
- **Why the default lost.** The operator read the distillation against the live prompt and judged it undermined the essay — the chase-bigger, push-to-be-better spirit was exactly what the condensation had sanded off, and orch's missing attitudes proved the point.
- **What would reverse it.** The operator's own judgment after live sessions — if the essay's framing shows up in orch's outputs in ways they dislike, or a measured behavioral cost appears. Reversal is one fragment file, one test, one re-measured cap.

## D-057 — the docs get four homes, an index, and a root CLAUDE.md

- **What changed.** The flat `docs/` tree is reorganized into four homes, and the layout becomes a documented system. Moves, so old paths in entries above resolve: `docs/{handoff,phase2-spikes,phase0-report,tui-pivot-plan}.md` + `phase0-report.json` and `docs/futureDesign/requirements/{MORNING-HANDOFF,OVERNIGHT-QUESTIONS,prompt-budget-menu}.md` → `docs/archive/` (superseded and one-off artifacts, banner-flagged, never edited below the banner); `docs/{tui-spawn,system-prompt,command-channel,context-gauge,peer-review,fence}-notes.md` and `docs/blackboard-shakedown.md` → `docs/notes/` (measurements, version-stamped evidence); `docs/futureDesign/requirements/` → `docs/roadmap/` (the WP system, now with an extracted `TEMPLATE.md`); `docs/futureDesign/vision_tenets.md` and `docs/futureDesign/plans/*.html` → `docs/roadmap/source/`. `docs/futureDesign/` is gone. New files: a root `CLAUDE.md` (dev-session orientation + the doc maintenance loop — this does not brush Tier 1.1, which forbids the *app* writing `CLAUDE.md` into the repo it operates on; panes never run with this repo as cwd), `docs/README.md` (the index every doc change updates), `docs/runtime-layout.md` (the `~/.fleetor` map), and WP-10 (the `ui/` architecture map, filed not written). This entry's preamble was also touched: the `BUILDING.md` filename corrected to `building.md`, and the pre-pivot "Standing defaults" paragraph marked historical — entries were not modified.
- **Why the default lost.** The organic layout mixed four kinds of document flat in `docs/`, hid the roadmap under `futureDesign/`, and had no index and no auto-loaded entry point — every fresh session paid an exploration pass before its first useful action, and stale copies of the same fact (budget numbers in four files) drifted independently.
- **What would reverse it.** The index rotting or the taxonomy going unfollowed within a few sessions — at which point the answer is fewer homes and a generated index, not more prose.

## D-058 — WP-11: a run is a database, rotated at start, and history is a directory of frozen files

- **What changed.** The event log stops being one file that every fleet start appends to. `~/.fleetor/_shell/state.db` is now always and only the **live** run; at each `fleet_bootstrap`, before the store is opened, the previous run is archived to `~/.fleetor/runs/<timestamp>/state.db` and a fresh database is opened. Archiving checkpoints the old WAL and moves a single self-contained file (strategy B in `docs/notes/run-rotation-notes.md`), falling back to moving `state.db`+`-wal`+`-shm` together, and finally to leaving them alone with a `Warn` — rotation may never fail a boot. `runs/index.json` holds each run's editable label, target and derived counts, and is a **cache**: reads scan `runs/*/` for existence and merge, so losing it costs labels only. A new History view lists past runs and opens one read-only into the existing Messages, Tasks and Activity components. The run id is the timestamp directory; the label is a separate mutable string, auto-suggested at archive time, so renaming never moves a file.
- **Why the default lost.** The single shared log was about to become actively wrong: the evaluation loop this unblocks compares one cycle against another, and cannot while cycle 4's read returns cycle 1's messages. Physical isolation beat the cheaper `run_id`-column design because run separation behind a `WHERE` clause on the one table that is this app's whole persistence story is the corruption being prevented, reimplemented as a predicate — and migrations here are appended, never edited, so a wrong schema would be permanent. The boundary is cut at **start** rather than at teardown because teardown is the thing that fails; `orphans.rs` exists for exactly that reason, and a crash under rotate-on-stop silently reopens the previous run.
- **What would reverse it.** Runs accumulating to the point that the directory needs a retention policy — at which point the answer is a size column and an operator-driven delete before it is automatic expiry. Or a measured need for cross-run queries frequent enough that opening N files hurts, which would argue for `ATTACH` over a schema change, not for the `run_id` column.

## D-059 — the archive is written for an agent to read, and it takes the workers' transcripts with it

- **What changed.** An archived run stops being one SQLite file and becomes a readable directory. Rotation now also writes `events.json` (the whole log as a JSON array, each row the event exactly as it crossed the wire plus its `seq` and `ts` columns) and `manifest.json` (the run's metadata, what each file in the directory is, and what the data does *not* cover), and **moves** each worker's Claude Code session transcripts from `pane-config/worker-N/projects/*/` into `runs/<id>/transcripts/worker-N/`. Moved rather than copied: transcripts live in a config dir that outlives a run, so copying would leave every archive carrying its predecessors. `RunRecord` gains a `transcripts` count, the History rows gain an Export button, and `run_export` saves `events.json` wherever the operator points via the Rust-side dialog — a copy of the file that already exists, never a second serialization, so "download the logs" and "what an agent reads" cannot drift into two formats.
- **Why the default lost.** The operator's stated purpose for History is that *agents* read and evaluate past runs, humans later. That inverts the design: the primary artifact is a directory readable with `cat` and `jq` — no SQLite, no running app, no GUI to drive to produce the reader's own input — and the download button becomes a convenience over it rather than the mechanism. The event log alone was also the wrong evidence base: it records what the panes said to each other, and an evaluator asking why a run went the way it did needs what each pane *did* between saying things, which only the transcripts hold. `events.json` is deliberately re-emitted from parsed JSON rather than re-serialized through `FleetEvent`, so a field a future build adds survives being read by an older one.
- **What would reverse it.** Disk: every run now stores its log twice plus its transcripts, and a long run's transcripts dwarf both. If that bites, the answer is generating `events.json` on demand and keeping `state.db` as the only stored copy — at the cost of the property that makes this useful, so measure the actual sizes first. **The known gap, unreversed:** `orch`'s transcript is *not* archived, because it lives in the operator's own `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` outside `~/.fleetor` and reaching in there crosses Tier 1.1's boundary. The pane doing the deciding is the one whose reasoning the evaluator cannot read. Closing it needs an operator decision, not a builder's.

## D-060 — the self-improving loop: an answer key the orchestrator cannot see, and a grader it cannot change

- **What changed.** The evaluation sandbox abandoned on 2026-08-06 is re-opened with a different shape, filed as the WP-12 arc. Settled this session: (1) **dev mode** is a real, operator-triggered product mode, not a test rig bolted on the side — the last attempt died pretending to be a rig while doing ~10 files of core surgery; (2) **the evaluator** is a real `claude` in its own Tauri window, wakes on a new done verb, coaches and never edits; (3) **the veil** — `orch` is not told the evaluator exists and cannot address it until it declares done; (4) **rewind missions** — a real repo pinned to an old tag, judged against the feature upstream actually shipped, so the retro rests on an information asymmetry rather than on two Claude instances agreeing; (5) **the operator is the only writer** in the loop, which is Tier 1.8 applied to the platform itself.
- **Why the default lost.** "Evaluate the fleet" with no answer key converges on congratulation. Upstream's real commit is the only cheap source of ground truth that is not itself a model's opinion. And the abandoned attempt's failure was scoping, not concept: gating this behind a visible mode makes it product from the first line, and putting the largest chunk (the rewind harness) in a separate repo that imports nothing makes core churn structurally impossible for that part.
- **The hiding model, stated once.** A Claude Code process running as the operator can read any file the operator can read; deny rules are friction, not enforcement. So only three things are made **absent** rather than fenced — the answer key (not cloned until after the done verb), the evaluator's brief (separate private repo, `include_str!` behind a `devmode` cargo feature, so nothing on disk holds it), and the ledger. Dev-mode code, the second window and the done verb are deliberately *not* hidden: they do not contaminate, and `orch` learns the evaluator exists at first contact anyway.
- **The grader is frozen across generations.** Compiling the evaluator's brief in from a repo the fleet cannot reach is not only about `orch` reading its rubric — if an improve run ever softened the evaluator, "did the fleet improve or did the grader get soft" becomes unanswerable. Changing the evaluator deliberately resets the comparison baseline and must be version-stamped on every retro.
- **Tier 1.4 held, not bent.** The operator asked for an orch-to-orch cutoff the CLI honours too. Built as a mute it would be exactly the refusal-in-the-message-path §9.3 records as argued and lost twice, and it would make the log lie (L3). The allowed shape is **addressing, not delivery**: link off means the other fleet's panes are not addresses, so `fleet send other:orch` fails at *resolution* like `fleet send worker-9` does today. Nothing enters the delivery path to be refused. WP-19 is built this way or filed as a Tier-1 question.
- **The fleet is a contributor, not a hot-patcher.** An improve run works in its own worktree with its own `target/`, never writes into the running install, and takes effect only at an operator-triggered rebuild-and-relaunch — that relaunch *is* the generation boundary. Five cases catalogued in WP-12; the one live today is that `npm run tauri dev` runs Vite, so HMR would hot-swap the running UI. **Standing rule: run a built binary during a cycle, never `tauri dev`.**
- **What would reverse it.** A first retro (WP-15) that produces nothing `orch` has to argue with — at which point the fault is the evaluator's brief or the answer key's usefulness, and the fix is prose or mission selection, not more machinery. Or the arc reaching ~10 files of core churn again, which is the original verdict repeating and should stop the arc rather than be worked around.
- **Not verified:** managed-settings precedence and `PreToolUse` interception of `Bash` for the installed CC version — deny layers 2 and 3 in WP-12 are written as unconfirmed on purpose.

## D-061 — WP-16: dev mode is a key in the operator's config, not a webview preference

- **What changed.** Dev mode is stored as `dev_mode` in `~/.fleetor/config.json` and read on the Rust side through exactly one function, `dev::is_enabled()`, which re-reads the file rather than caching a boot-time snapshot. The UI is a view of that flag, never a second copy: `dev_mode_get` / `dev_mode_set` are the whole surface, the settings switch does not move until the write has landed, and the coral band is rendered from what came back. Two tests hold the boundaries the mode must not cross — no file on the path from `fleet send` to a pty may mention it (Tier 1.4), and no prompt file, brief renderer or roster type may either (WP-12's open question 4, answered the cheap way).
- **Why the default lost.** The established pattern for an app-wide preference in this repo is `localStorage`, where the theme and the nav selection live — and dev mode looks like one more of those. It is not one: WP-15's evaluator window and WP-17's fence branch on the mode from code that has no webview, so a `localStorage` flag would have to be mirrored to Rust, and two copies of one fact means the copy that is wrong is the one somebody reads. A second file under `~/.fleetor` was the other candidate and lost to "a mode with its own file is a mode the operator has to be told where to find" — `config.json` is where they already set the target. Reading fresh rather than snapshotting at bootstrap is the inverse of the call made for `Fleet::target`: a target has an incoherent half-state (half a fleet in one repo), a mode has none today, and caching would have pre-empted WP-15's decision about its own window's lifecycle.
- **What would reverse it.** A reader on a hot path that cannot afford a file read per call — at which point the answer is a cached handle *at that call site*, not a second store. Or WP-15 finding that a mode which can change under a running evaluator is a real hazard, which would make the flag snapshot at bootstrap like the target does; the seam for that is one function, `is_enabled`. **Not verified:** the band was reviewed against building.md §7 rule by rule but never seen on screen — this repo has no frontend test framework, and adding one is a Tier 2 dependency decision left to the operator, so nothing about the React half of this is machine-checked.
## D-062 — WP-14: `orch` gets a config dir of its own, and D-059's named gap closes

- **What changed.** `orch` stops running on the operator's own `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`. It now spawns with `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR=~/.fleetor/_shell/pane-config/orch`, seeded at the spawn site with the same two onboarding keys a worker's dir gets — because once it is a fleet-owned directory, L1 applies to `orch` exactly as it applies to a worker. Its session transcript therefore lands under `~/.fleetor`, and `runs::harvest_transcripts` archives it into `runs/<id>/transcripts/orch/` with **no code change**: it already walked `_shell/pane-config/*`. Nothing is read, copied or linked out of the operator's directory; `orch` starts a new one and goes forward from there. `worker_config_dir(slot)` became `pane_config_dir(PaneId)`, one root for every pane. This also gives WP-17's deny rules and hooks the home they need — the second job WP-12 filed this package for.
- **The load-bearing detail, and it is not obvious.** `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` alone **logs `orch` out**, invisibly. Claude Code namespaces its macOS Keychain service name by `sha256(config dir)[..8]` whenever that variable is set, so a fleet-owned directory is a fresh, empty login. The pane still reaches its input box, so every `fleet send` into it reports `accepted` and only its turns fail — the risk-register failure that looks exactly like a healthy pane. `orch_command` therefore also sets `CLAUDE_SECURESTORAGE_CONFIG_DIR` to the **empty string**, which selects the unsuffixed service name: the entry the operator's own `/login` already wrote. That moves the credential *lookup* and nothing else — `claude` performs the identical keychain read it performs today, so this does not touch the operator's auth, it preserves it. `worker_command` `env_remove`s the same variable so a worker can never inherit the key to that entry. Measured against CC 2.1.224 in `docs/notes/orch-config-dir-notes.md`, four arms of a real interactive pane plus the derivation read out of the binary.
- **Why the default lost.** D-059 left this gap open and said closing it needed an operator decision, because reaching into the operator's personal Claude history to move it would cross Tier 1.1. The operator made the call: the pane that makes every decision cannot be the one pane an evaluator cannot read, and the event log alone gives it `orch`'s messages with none of its reasoning. The refusal that produced the gap is intact — nothing reaches into their directory. What changed is that `orch` gets a directory of its own going forward, which is a different move from the one D-059 refused.
- **What it costs, stated plainly.** `orch` no longer inherits the operator's user `CLAUDE.md`, skills, subagents, MCP servers, settings or hooks — all of which are read from `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (`docs/notes/system-prompt-notes.md` §3 measured that they load today). D-030's "orch is untouched" is narrowed here for the first time, and narrowed to exactly one thing: the config dir. The Fence stays worker-only, `HOME` is still the operator's, `augmented_path()` still reads it, and `orch` still spawns with no `--permission-mode` and no `ANTHROPIC_*` override. Three tests assert that, because the cheap way to break this package is to make `orch` slightly more like a worker.
- **What would reverse it.** An operator noticing that `orch` stopped being *their* Claude — no skills, no MCP servers, no memory. The fix is not to undo this: it is to let the operator populate `~/.fleetor/_shell/pane-config/orch/` themselves, which is a directory they own and this app only seeds two keys into. Or Claude Code dropping `CLAUDE_SECURESTORAGE_CONFIG_DIR`, which is an internal and not in `claude --help`. That failure is bounded and self-healing — `orch` shows `Not logged in · Run /login`, one `/login` inside the pane writes the credential into the fleet dir's own namespace, it persists, and it cannot disturb the operator's own entry. `spawn_pane` puts that sentence on the Activity feed at every `orch` launch rather than leaving the operator to diagnose a pane that looks fine.
- **Not verified:** `orch` reaching a prompt *inside the running app*. The exact environment was spawned by hand in a pty and reached `Claude Max` with an input box; no fleet has been started since the change landed.

## D-063 — the answer key is absent, not unobtainable, and a frozen grader is not enough

- **What changed.** Two claims in WP-12 are corrected by the rewind harness's own measurement (`~/harness/fleetor-eval/NOTES.md`, 2026-08-07). (1) The answer key was called "airtight and free." It is free and **not airtight**: the pinned tree names its own upstream in six files and states its version, and GitHub serves arbitrary SHAs to `git fetch`, so a deliberate clone or a fetch-by-SHA retrieves everything the shallow clone withheld. Scrubbing the upstream identity is not available as a fix — the information that makes the hole possible *is the project*. (2) Case 5 said the grader must be frozen across generations; that is necessary and **not sufficient**, because judging weights are per-mission, and the same mission run twice is contaminated by the previous generation's branches and archived reasoning.
- **Why the default lost.** Both were reasoned, not measured, and a spike measured otherwise. `building.md` §4: where a spike and a plan disagree, the spike wins. The first claim mattered because "airtight" would have justified skipping the audit that is now the actual mitigation; the second mattered because it silently invalidates every generation-over-generation number the loop would otherwise produce.
- **What follows.** The answer key's mitigation is a **post-hoc audit** — a clone of upstream or a fetch-by-SHA appearing in the run's archived transcripts voids that run — not network egress control. And a real generation comparison needs **pairs of missions of matched difficulty**, which nothing in the arc builds yet; until it does, generation-over-generation claims are qualitative and only within-generation findings rest on measurement.
- **What would reverse it.** For the first: a mission source where the target's identity is genuinely separable from its content, which would make absence enforce rather than merely deter. For the second: a demonstrated way to build matched mission pairs, or evidence that re-running one mission after a full `~/.fleetor` reset and a fresh clone is uncontaminated enough to compare.

## D-064 — WP-13: the done verb is spelled `handoff`, it answers `recorded`, and the orch cap moves to 3,800

- **What changed.** A ninth `fleet` verb: `fleet handoff --built "<what the fleet built>" --evidence "<how anyone could check it>" [--evidence …] [--open "<what is unfinished>"]`, by which `orch` declares the whole confirmed goal met. It appends one `FleetEvent::Handoff` and does nothing else — new `Op::Handoff`, new `crates/fleetor-core/src/handoff.rs`, a sixth event variant with its `ui/src/fleet/types.ts` mirror and a coral, railed Activity row. `VERBS` goes 8 → 9; the clap enum, the wire, both prompt files, the pinned-literal brief tests and `src-tauri/src/prompts.rs`'s override fixture all moved in the one commit the contention rule requires.
- **Why it is not called `done`, and why the default lost.** The obvious name was taken: `fleet done <task-id> "<check>"` is a *worker* closing one block with a receipt (D-050). Naming the mission-level act with a near-synonym of the block-level one is precisely the drift the `accepted`/`recorded`/`delivered` discipline exists to prevent — one name per concept, and `done` already has its concept. `handoff` also carries the honest framing: the fleet's work is over and the operator takes it back. The two verbs are the two a model could most plausibly confuse, so each brief names the one that is **not** its pane's (`both_briefs_separate_closing_a_block_from_handing_the_work_back`), and the CLI refuses a worker's `fleet handoff` locally with a sentence naming `fleet done` — the same contract-on-the-sender check, in the same place, as `done.rs`'s refusal of `orch`.
- **`recorded`, deliberately, and not a fourth `OpResult`.** There is no pty to reach, and D-051 settled that stretching `accepted` over a record-only op dilutes the one word Tier 1.5 keeps; `delivered` is never rendered by anything. So `recorded` — reusing `OpResult::Recorded { record_id }`, because D-051's whole argument is that a second variant meaning "entered the log, no pty exists" would be two spellings of one fact. Three callers now share it: a task claim, a message to `operator`, and a handoff.
- **A sixth `FleetEvent` variant rather than a message to `operator`.** `task.rs`'s tripwire list is the bar, and it is cleared the same way `Task` clears it: **nothing reads a handoff back to permit, order or refuse anything.** `crates/fleetor-server/tests/handoff.rs` counts that instead of asserting it — a handoff sends the app zero `AppCommand`s, and a `fleet send` is byte-identical at the pty and in the log before and after one. `Hub::handoff` is not `async` and never touches `self.app`, the signature-level tell `Hub::task` and `Hub::record` already carry. It is not a `Message` to the operator because a message is something a pane *said* to an addressee; a declaration rendered in the message record would put words in `orch`'s mouth, and prose would flatten evidence that is worth keeping as a list. The variant has **no `accepted` field** for the same reason `recorded` is the word.
- **The worker brief was touched, and that was forced rather than chosen.** `brief::require_verbs` validates the rendered *worker* brief against the same `VERBS` list, so a verb `worker.md` never names makes the shipped template fail its own validation. Three options: one line in `worker.md`, per-pane `VERBS` lists, or leaving `handoff` out of `VERBS` and breaking the clap pin. The one line won — it is useful prose rather than a formality (it is the altitude split, said where a worker will look), while the other two trade one stable CLI contract for a shape in which a verb can end up pinned by nothing.
- **The veil held, and the test that keeps it grew.** Nothing `orch` is told mentions an evaluator, an assessment, a retro or a rubric; the prose says the verb reports to the operator, which is the whole truth from the fleet's side. WP-16's `no_pane_is_briefed_about_dev_mode` covered the *mode*; `no_pane_is_briefed_about_the_evaluator` now covers the thing behind it, over the same seven files that become words a pane is told. `review` is deliberately not on that word list — peer review is real, taught and worker-facing (WP-06).
- **Cost, measured — the sixth verb-adding overrun, and the ledger held again.** `count.py` (DeepSeek Flash tokenizer): orch **3,366 → 3,668 (+302)**, worker **2,099 → 2,139 (+40)**; combined +342 against a pre-prose guess of ~80, i.e. **~4.3×** — inside D-053's 3.8–4.6× band for a package that adds a verb, and for the reason that ledger gives: a verb costs what its *rules* cost. The syntax is one line; the rules are the altitude split, the mandatory evidence, the check-before-you-claim instruction and the consequence attached to each.
- **The cap is restated, not silently edited: orch 3,500 → 3,800; worker unchanged at 2,200.** D-053's reversal clause requires a re-measurement and an explicit statement, and this is it. 3,800 keeps the same discipline D-056 used — thin headroom over measured reality (+132, ~3.6%) so accidental growth still trips it, rather than a round number chosen to stop the question being asked. The measured worker figure (2,139) sits 61 under its unchanged cap. Stated for context and not as licence: the operator's position is that the cap is a drift tripwire rather than a ceiling and that ~10k would be acceptable, so the prose was written to say what the verb needs and then measured — not contorted to fit.
- **What would reverse it.** A live run in which `orch` hands back on a full board rather than a met vision — the fix is the prose ("the finish line is the vision the operator confirmed, not the last block on the board" is already the clause), not the verb. Or WP-15 finding it needs something the event does not carry, which is a field on `Handoff`, not a second verb. If the two verbs are observed being confused in practice despite both briefs naming the other, the reversal is a rename of one of them — cheap, because `VERBS`, the clap enum, the wire tag and the pinned tests all move together by construction.
- **Verified:** 210 workspace tests green (49 CLI, 108 core, 26 pane-messaging, 7 task-board, 3 handoff, 4 event-bus, 12 db, 1 ipc) and 121 shell tests green (107 lib, 3 dev-mode, 11 real-pty), `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx vite build` clean, `count.py` re-run before and after. **Not verified:** nothing has run this verb inside a live fleet — the Activity row was written against building.md §7's rules and never seen on screen, which is the same gap D-061 records for the dev-mode band (this repo has no frontend test framework).

## D-065 — WP-17: the write guardrail is a `PreToolUse` hook, writes only, and its Bash half enforces stated intent

- **What changed.** Every pane now spawns with a `PreToolUse` hook installed into its own `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (`src-tauri/src/guardrail.rs`, called from `fleet::spawn_pane` beside `seed_config_dir`), refusing any `Bash` / `Write` / `Edit` / `MultiEdit` / `NotebookEdit` call that would write outside that pane's roots. The roots are the pane's **own cwd** — a worker's git worktree, `orch`'s target repo — plus `~/.fleetor/_shell/`, minus `_shell/pane-config/`. Reads are untouched: the hook is not registered for `Read`, `Grep` or `Glob`, so it is never consulted about one. A refusal names the path as written and as resolved, which rule fired, the full root list, and what to do instead; it also appends a line to `~/.fleetor/_shell/guardrail.jsonl`, which a task of the fleet's own drains onto the Activity feed once a second. `prompts/launch.conf`'s `[fence]` section gained a repeatable, absolute-only `allow` key.
- **Why this shape and not the two the operator rejected.** A read denylist and a full read+write allowlist were both considered. Reads stay open because a Bash command a hook allows can read anything internally — read-blocking is friction wearing enforcement's clothes — and because a read allowlist has to cover every toolchain path, which wedges a pane in the way that looks exactly like a healthy pane (the risk register's worst entry). Network blocking is not here either: D-063 already settled that the answer key's mitigation is a post-hoc transcript audit, not egress control. Stripping the inherited environment is the sandboxing decision `docs/notes/fence-notes.md` rules out by name.
- **The gate was verified before anything was built on it.** WP-12's deny-layer list called `PreToolUse` interception of `Bash` unverified for the installed version. `examples/write-guardrail-spike/probe.py` settled it against CC 2.1.224 with four real `claude -p` arms, checking the **filesystem** rather than the model's prose: a deny stops the tool call, the reason reaches the model as words it acts on (in one arm it redid the write inside the worktree), and — the strong form — **the hook's deny beats an explicit `--allowedTools` allow**, which is the posture a worker actually runs in.
- **Why the Bash half enforces *stated intent*, and why that is the honest scope.** `Write`/`Edit`/`MultiEdit`/`NotebookEdit` name their destination in the tool call, so those refusals are enforcement. `Bash` does not, and two measurements decided the rule rather than a plan (`building.md` §4). A worker's first `git commit` writes **eight files into the target repo's `.git`** — outside its allowlist, by design of `git worktree` — and `fleet done` runs it first, so a guardrail built as a real write sandbox would have wedged every worker on receipt one. A real `cargo build` writes into `~/.cargo/registry` and names nothing at all. Both pass because the scanner only sees paths a command *states*, and both are now pinned by a test; `git -C <somewhere outside> commit`, which does state one, is refused.
- **Where the setting went, and why not a third mechanism.** `prompts/launch.conf`'s `[fence]` section, not `~/.fleetor/config.json`. D-052 created that key saying it exists "so a future one has somewhere to land without a new setting surface", and this is that future one: `launch.conf` is the hand-edited, documented, read-once-at-bootstrap file, whereas `config.json` holds app state the app itself writes (the target, WP-16's `dev_mode`). `allow` is additive and repeatable — "one more directory the fleet may write in" is the shape of the request — and refuses a relative path with the reason, because a relative root would resolve against each pane's own cwd and mean five different directories from one line.
- **Tier 1.7 is narrowed here, not widened, and that is stated so it is not re-litigated.** Workers already spawn `--permission-mode auto` scoped to their worktree; this refuses a subset of what that allowed. §9.2's escalation trigger is *widening* auto-approve. **Tier 1.4 is held structurally, not promised:** the hook is consulted by Claude Code inside the pane's own process, and `src-tauri/tests/write_guardrail.rs` pins both directions — no delivery-path file mentions the guardrail, and `guardrail.rs` mentions no `Hub`, `AppCommand`, `PaneRegistry`, `Op` or `deliver::`. Its only outputs are a settings file and a journal line, and nothing on the message path ever waits on either. D-062 is what makes any of this possible for `orch`: before it, `orch` ran on the operator's own config dir, which is not somewhere this app may write policy.
- **What it does not stop, named rather than implied.** A write whose path the command does not name (`cargo build`, `python3 -c` with a runtime-assembled path) — structural, and closing it means the OS sandbox the arc rules out. A pane disabling its own hook via `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR/settings.json`, which is not a literal path — `_shell/pane-config/` is refused by spelling only. The journal is inside `_shell`, so a pane can mangle it; a bad line is skipped and the real ones around it still report, and losing a notice never costs a decision.
- **A pre-existing Fence breakage the spike found and this package deliberately did not fix.** A worker cannot `cargo build` at all: the Fence redirects `HOME`, rustup resolves `$HOME/.rustup`, and the private home has no toolchains — measured with the guardrail entirely uninvolved. `fence-notes.md`'s catalogue checked `gh` and reasoned about `nvm`; it never checked rustup. This is exactly the "live evidence that a worker legitimately needs a tool" D-052 reserved as its own reversal condition, and the fix is a Fence decision with its own entry, not a root on this allowlist. It is WP-17 open question 1, and an improve run is a Rust build, so it wants closing first.
- **What would reverse it.** Live evidence that the write-position scanner refuses something a pane legitimately needs — the fix is a case in `examples/write-guardrail-spike/cases.py` and a rule beside it, not a wider root. Evidence that panes are routinely denied for `/tmp` scratch files, which is one `allow = /tmp` line. Or Claude Code changing the `PreToolUse` contract, which is version-stamped at 2.1.224 in `docs/notes/write-guardrail-notes.md`; the degradation there is the loud kind only if someone re-runs the probe, which is why that file names the reproduce command.
- **Verified:** 210 workspace tests green (unchanged — WP-17 touched no core crate) and **143** shell tests green, up from 121: 121 lib (including 11 new `guardrail` unit tests and 3 new `[fence] allow` parser tests), 3 dev-mode, 11 real-pty unchanged, and 8 new in `tests/write_guardrail.rs` that drive the decision through the *installed* `settings.json` command with a real shell. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx vite build` clean; no UI or prompt-template file touched, so `brief-cost` stayed at the spec's own 0. **Not verified:** the guardrail on an interactive pane inside the running app — the same honest gap D-062 records for `orch`'s config dir; no fleet has been started since this landed. Live spend: four `haiku` print-mode turns, ~$0.13.

## D-066 — WP-15: the evaluator is a `PaneId` in no enumeration, its wake is a bus subscriber, and it reads the live run in place

- **What changed.** `orch` calling `fleet handoff` now wakes a real interactive `claude` in a second Tauri window: `PaneId` gains a sixth variant `Evaluator`, the app lays the *live* run out for reading at `~/.fleetor/dev/retro/<run-id>/`, fetches the answer key, and spawns the pane there. The brief is compiled in from `~/harness/fleetor-eval/evaluator/brief.md` behind a new **`devmode` cargo feature** (`src-tauri/build.rs` → `OUT_DIR`); a default build carries none of it and has no reachable spawn path. Three conditions gate it and each fails with its own sentence: the feature, `dev::is_enabled()`, and the target being a prepared rewind-mission workspace.
- **Why a `PaneId` variant, and why the default lost.** The obvious reading of the veil is that a name `orch` has never heard of should not be in `orch`'s own contract at all. It has to be: the retro is a two-way logged conversation, and every name in this system's record *is* a `PaneId` — the CLI parses one before the socket, `Hello` declares one, `FleetEvent::Message` carries one from and to, and `fleet reply` resolves through a `HashMap<PaneId, PaneId>`. The alternatives were a spelling chosen to dodge a grep (the workaround `building.md` §6 bans by name) or reusing `operator`, which would put a lie in the log about who spoke. So the veil moved from *the name does not exist* to **the name is in no enumeration**, expressed once as `PaneId::is_fleet_member()` and filtered once in `PaneRegistry::roster()` — the single answer that feeds both `fleet roster`'s listing and a broadcast's legs.
- **The shape, stated as the inverse of D-051, because that is what makes both legible.** The operator is **in the roster listing and has no terminal**; the evaluator **has a terminal and is in no listing**. `has_pty()` and `is_fleet_member()` disagree in both directions, which is exactly why neither is derived from the other. A message to the evaluator is `accepted` — there is a real pty and bytes reached it — and Tier 1.5 needs no stretching.
- **The wake is off the message path, and the proof is a count that did not move.** It is a second `EventFollower` in `src-tauri`, downstream of D-020's persist-then-publish bus: `Hub::handoff` is byte-identical, no `AppCommand` is sent, and `tests/handoff.rs::a_handoff_asks_the_app_for_nothing_and_changes_no_later_send` is untouched and green. **One doc sentence was wrong and was corrected rather than left:** `Hub::handoff` claimed *nothing anywhere* read a handoff back. It now says *nothing on the message path*. What `task.rs`'s tripwire bars is a read that goes on to permit, order or refuse something; this permits, orders and refuses nothing — it changes what *exists*, the shape WP-16 named as allowed and WP-19 is held to.
- **An existing test's letter changed, and the replacement is stricter.** `no_pane_is_briefed_about_the_evaluator` grepped seven files for `evaluat`, one of them `pane.rs`, which the variant fails on day one. `pane.rs` came off **that one test's** list (it stays on the dev-mode one) and `nothing_a_pane_can_read_ever_names_the_evaluator` replaced it: the *rendered* orch brief, the *rendered* worker brief, `PaneId::roster()`, and the parse error a mistyped name produces. That asserts the property the grep was a proxy for — not "the source lacks the word" but "no pane is ever shown it" — and `pane.rs` could always have rendered a name it never spelled. The prose list also gained `prompts/scaffolding.md`, a fragment composed into both briefs that nobody had been checking. Net: seven greps became seven greps plus four behavioural assertions.
- **The live run is read in place, read-only, by the function that already existed** (`docs/notes/live-run-snapshot-notes.md`, measured). Rotation archives the *previous* run at bootstrap, so the run the evaluator wants is `_shell/state.db` with this process's writer holding it open. A read-only connection sees every committed row through an uncheckpointed WAL and does not disturb the writer; copying `state.db` alone provably does not (4 KB with no schema — D-058's finding, confirmed for a live run). So `archive::to_json` is called on the live file, `archive::freeze` is never called on it, transcripts are **copied** rather than moved, and no `Store` method and no third seam were added. `events.json` mid-run and `events.json` in `runs/` come from one function and cannot drift.
- **Two placements are load-bearing and neither is obvious.** The retro directory is under `~/.fleetor/dev/`, a **sibling** of `_shell/` — every pane's write guardrail has `_shell` as a root (D-065), so a snapshot inside it would be writable by the fleet being judged. And the evaluator's `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is outside `_shell/pane-config/`, because `harvest_transcripts` walks that directory and would file the evaluator's own reasoning into `runs/<id>/transcripts/` — handing the *next* generation's fleet the past retros WP-12 requires to be absent, by a code path nobody wrote.
- **Two live bugs the second window exposed, both pre-existing.** `pty::channel_key` matched on `slot()`, so `PaneId::Operator` already resolved to `orch`'s channel — harmless only because nothing spawns the operator, and a silent cross-render for any second slotless name. And `on_window_event` tore the **whole fleet** down on any `CloseRequested`, which with two windows is a six-pty kill on closing the wrong one. Separately, `capabilities/default.json` scopes permissions by window label, and a window missing from it gets no `invoke` and no `listen` with no error at all.
- **Tier 1.7 is narrowed here, not widened, and it is the one call worth re-reading.** The evaluator spawns `--permission-mode auto`, which `orch` does not get; its guardrail roots are its own working directory alone — not `_shell`, not `[fence] allow` — so what auto-approve can change is a strict subset of what any worker's already could. §9.2's trigger is *widening*. `manual` was the alternative and would park the pane on its first `Read` of a run archive while looking exactly like a healthy pane.
- **What would reverse it.** A first retro that produces nothing `orch` has to argue with — at which point the fault is the brief's prose or the mission's usefulness, and the fix is text or mission selection, not more machinery (D-060 already says so). Evidence that `auto` lets the evaluator change something it should not, which is one word in `spawn::evaluator_command`. Or the retro directory's transcript copies growing enough to matter, whose answer is a size column and an operator-driven delete, as D-058 reserved for `runs/`.
- **Verified:** **213** workspace tests green, up from 210 — the only `crates/` diff outside `pane.rs`'s three new tests is one corrected doc comment in `hub.rs` — and **165** shell tests green, up from 143: 134 lib (including 2 new in `pty` and 3 in `runs`), 4 dev-mode, **8 new in `tests/evaluator.rs`**, 11 real-pty, 8 write-guardrail. `cargo test --features devmode` green at 165 too (the `evaluator.rs` suite swaps its default-build assertion for the compiled-in-brief one). `cargo build --features devmode` succeeds against the real brief; with `FLEETOR_EVAL_HARNESS` pointed at nothing it fails at `build.rs:68` naming the absolute path. `strings` finds the brief's version stamp **0 times** in a default binary and 2 times in a `devmode` one. `npx tsc --noEmit` and `npx vite build` clean. **Not verified:** no fleet has been started, so the window has never been on screen, no evaluator has reached a prompt, and no retro has happened — the same gap D-062 and D-065 record, one package larger. No live spend.

## D-067 — worktrees namespaced by target repo

- **What changed.** `worktree_dir(slot)` became `worktree_dir(target, slot)`. Worker worktrees now live at `~/.fleetor/_shell/worktrees/<target-slug>/worker-N` where `<target-slug>` is the target directory name plus a 4-character hex hash of the canonical path (e.g. `my-project-a3f2`). `target_slug()` in `fleet.rs` computes it.
- **The default that lost was a flat `worktrees/worker-N`.** Switching targets left stale worktrees from the old repo on disk. `ensure_worktree` saw `.git` and short-circuited, so workers ended up in checkouts of the wrong repo. The alternative — detect-and-recreate — would have torn down and rebuilt worktrees on every target switch, losing any uncommitted worker state and adding teardown logic. Namespacing preserves each target's worktrees independently, so switching back reuses them.
- **What would reverse it.** Evidence that the disk accumulation matters (each target's four worktrees persist until `rm -rf ~/.fleetor/_shell`), or a target whose directory name plus hash collides with another in practice — which would require two targets whose canonical paths produce the same 16-bit hash *and* share a directory name.

## D-068 — orch answers with `fleet reply`, including to names it does not know

- **What changed.** `prompts/orch.md` §"What arrives" gained a paragraph: a reply written as ordinary output reaches nobody, `fleet reply` is what gets back to the sender, and that holds for a sender whose name orch does not recognize — if a message arrived, the sender is a real pane, and `fleet reply` finds it whether or not `fleet roster` lists it.
- **The gap it closes.** orch's brief enumerated only workers and `operator`, so a message from any other pane got answered into orch's own transcript and reached nobody. Observed live: the evaluator's two interview questions (seq 15, 16) had no reply in the log. `worker.md:23` already carried the equivalent ("This is your usual move"); orch's did not.
- **Why it is worded without naming the sender.** `tests/dev_mode.rs::no_pane_is_briefed_about_the_evaluator` scans this file and fails on the word, and the veil (WP-12) requires it. The clause is general on purpose — it is true of any non-enumerated pane, which is the property `pty.rs::roster` documents ("not enumerated, and not unreachable").
- **What would reverse it.** Evidence that orch replies to unknown senders without being told, or a decision that non-members should not be able to reach orch at all — which would be a delivery-path refusal and collides with Tier 1.4.
- **Brief cost, measured not guessed (D-053).** `examples/system-prompt-spike/count.py`: orch **3,668 → 3,749**, worker unchanged at 2,139. The guess before writing the prose was ~65; actual 81, ~1.25× — well inside the band, and consistent with D-053's finding that a package adding *no verb and no protocol* lands near its estimate while verb-adding ones land 3.8–4.6× over. **This leaves 51 tokens under the 3,800 cap**; the next orch-brief change measures first or restates the cap.

## D-069 — the Fence learns about rustup: both toolchain homes are the fleet's own

- **What changed.** A worker is now launched with `CARGO_HOME=~/.fleetor/_shell/cargo` and `RUSTUP_HOME=~/.fleetor/_shell/rustup`, plus one PATH rung (`_shell/cargo/bin`) holding eight shims symlinked to the operator's real `rustup` binary. `_shell/rustup` is a **mirror**: `settings.toml` copied, one symlink per installed toolchain into the operator's real `~/.rustup/toolchains/`. 4 KB seeded, against the 1.9 GB it points at. Both are seeded at spawn beside the private HOME (`spawn::seed_fleet_toolchain`), shared by all four workers, and set only when this machine actually has a rustup. `orch` gets neither — it is the operator's own `claude` (D-030).
- **This is D-052's own reversal condition, exercised as written.** D-052 reserved "live evidence that a worker legitimately needs … a version manager mid-task — the fix is scoping and seeding that tool's config for the worker specifically, **not** loosening `HOME` generally." `HOME` is untouched. `docs/notes/fence-notes.md` gains the 8th catalogue row and thirteen arms behind it.
- **The recorded bug was smaller than the real one.** WP-17 open question 1 recorded `rustup could not choose a version of cargo to run`. That measurement, and every arm in `examples/write-guardrail-spike/allowlist.sh`, ran with `PATH="$PATH"` — the operator's *login* PATH, which carries `~/.cargo/bin` — so it never asked whether a worker can find `cargo` at all. It cannot: `/opt/homebrew/bin/cargo` and `/usr/local/bin/cargo` are both absent, and those are the only two non-fleet rungs `worker_augmented_path` supplies. Under any PATH but an inherited login one the failure is `cargo: command not found`, exit 127, *before* rustup is reached. That is why the fix is a PATH rung and not two environment variables.
- **The plan going in was wrong about `RUSTUP_HOME`, and the spike won (`building.md` §4).** It proposed pointing `RUSTUP_HOME` at the operator's real `~/.rustup` on the grounds that it is read-only in practice, with the residual gap named. Arm 6b falsifies that: a `rust-toolchain.toml` naming an **uninstalled** channel — an ordinary thing for a repository to carry — downloads and installs it with no prompt, **35,981 files and ~1.2 GB into `~/.rustup`**. Arm 9b runs the identical pin against the mirror and the identical 35,981 files land under `_shell/` instead, 0 in the operator's. The mirror was listed in the plan as optional; it turned out to be free, and it is the difference between Tier 1.1 holding and not.
- **Why not point `CARGO_HOME` at the operator's `~/.cargo`** (the shape the old spike proved): it hands four auto-approving workers write access to `~/.cargo/bin`, which is on the *operator's* login PATH. Measured rather than reasoned — arm 7b runs `cargo install` under that setting and `~/.cargo/bin/spikey` appears. The write guardrail cannot refuse it, because `cargo install X` names no path (D-065's own measured limit). This is accident-shaped, which is the threat model this repo committed to.
- **Why not add `~/.cargo/bin` to the worker PATH:** that is the category of rung D-052 removed on purpose, and worse than the two it removed — the directory holds real binaries, not only shims (`cargo-create-tauri-app`, 1 MB, verified), and its contents change whenever the operator installs anything. The seeded rung exposes exactly the eight names the fleet enumerated, in a diff.
- **All eight shims or none, and that is measured.** Seeding `cargo` alone fails at `could not execute process 'rustc -vV' (never executed)` (arm 5a): cargo resolves through the proxy, then looks for `rustc` by name and finds nothing. With `cargo`, `rustc`, `rustup`, `rustdoc`, `rustfmt`, `cargo-fmt`, `cargo-clippy` and `clippy-driver` all linked, the build is clean (arm 5b).
- **One shared pair of directories, not one per worker.** Arm 8 runs two workers building concurrently against one `CARGO_HOME`: both exit 0, serializing on cargo's own package-cache lock. Per-worker `target/` already lives in each worktree, so a split would buy only a duplicated registry. Footprint after eight builds: 616 KB.
- **Absent rather than pointing at nothing.** With no rustup on the machine, neither variable is set and the PATH is byte-for-byte what it was. A `RUSTUP_HOME` naming a directory that does not exist makes rustup try to *install* there, which is a worse failure than today's. The operator is told once, on the Activity feed, beside the missing-`fleet`-binary warning.
- **No write-guardrail root was added, and there is now a test saying so.** `_shell` is already every pane's second root, so `_shell/cargo` and `_shell/rustup` were never refused. WP-17's instruction was "do not widen the allowlist to work around this"; it is honoured structurally, and `guardrail.rs::the_fleets_toolchain_homes_are_inside_a_pane_root_so_a_build_cache_write_is_never_refused` asserts `roots.len() == 2` so a later reader who reaches for `[fence] allow` finds out here.
- **An existing pin changed letter, and it is stricter.** `worker_path_drops_the_operator_home_rungs_orch_keeps_them` asserted `!worker_path.contains("/Users/operator")` — a substring sweep that held by luck. The new rung *is* under the operator's HOME, because that is where `~/.fleetor` lives. `worker_path_drops_every_operator_tool_rung_and_adds_only_the_fleets_own_cargo_bin` replaces it: every operator tool rung forbidden **by name**, `.cargo/bin` among them (the old test never named it), at most one rung under the operator's HOME and it must be the fleet's own, and orch's half byte-identical.
- **What this does not stop, named rather than implied.** The shims reach the operator's `rustup` binary by absolute path and the mirrored toolchains are absolute symlinks into `~/.rustup/toolchains/` — the Fence never claimed to stop absolute paths, and that is what makes a 4 KB mirror possible instead of a 1.9 GB copy. `rustup` itself is on a worker's PATH and must be (the shim set is all-or-nothing), so `rustup toolchain install` still works — it now writes into `_shell/rustup`. A worker can delete or corrupt the mirror, since `_shell` is inside its roots by design; the cost is a re-seed at the next start, never the operator's toolchain.
- **One inference is left standing and is labelled as one.** What a Finder-launched app inherits was **not** confirmed. The probe — a minimal `.app` launched through `open` — came back inconclusive: a sentinel variable proved `open` forwards the calling shell's environment, so it measured a terminal. `launchctl getenv PATH` is empty, which supports the inference; `ps eww | tr ' ' '\n' | grep ^PATH=` is actively wrong on this machine, because a PATH rung contains a space and the splitter truncates there. The fix does not depend on the inference: arm 2b shows the login-PATH case is broken too.
- **What would reverse it.** Evidence that a worker needs a toolchain the mirror does not expose, which is a wider seed and not a wider `HOME`. A cargo or rustup release that stops honouring `CARGO_HOME`/`RUSTUP_HOME`, which is upstream's own documented contract and would be loud. Or `_shell/rustup` growing enough to matter, whose answer is D-058's: a size column and an operator-driven delete, since a delete costs a re-download and never correctness.
- **Verified:** **176** shell tests green, up from 165 — 11 new: 10 in `spawn` (the D-069 block plus the rewritten PATH pin) and 1 in `guardrail`. 213 workspace tests green, unchanged; no `crates/` file was touched. 4 dev-mode tripwires green. Thirteen spike arms in `examples/fence-toolchain-spike/toolchain.sh`, transcripts in `docs/notes/fence-notes.md`. No prompt template changed, so `brief-cost` is 0 and the 51-token headroom D-068 recorded is untouched. **Not verified:** no fleet has been started since this landed, so no interactive worker has run `cargo build` in a real pane — the same honest gap D-062, D-065 and D-066 each record. The spike's arms are real shells with real builds, not a pane. No live spend.

## D-070 — an improve run targets a clone, never the live checkout

- **What was settled.** When the fleet is pointed at FLEETOR's own source (the improve run), the target is a **clone** — `git clone <repo> ~/fleetor-improve` — not the working checkout the app is running from. Reviewed work still merges to `fleet/integration` inside that clone (D-050 unchanged); the operator pulls from the clone into trunk themselves, deliberately, after a restart.
- **The failure it avoids is the file watcher, not the build.** Workers cannot break the running app: each works in its own worktree with its own `target/`, and reviewed branches never reach trunk, so nothing the fleet compiles is ever what the operator is executing. `orch` is the exception — its cwd is the target repo itself, and `tauri dev` watches `src-tauri/` with no `--no-watch` anywhere in this repo's config. An `orch` that edits a `.rs` file directly rather than delegating restarts the app underneath itself.
- **And a restart ends the run.** Panes are ptys the app owns; they die with it and there is no reattach. What survives is bounded and already built: `orphans.rs` kills the leaked `claude` processes at the next launch, and the run log rotates into `runs/` (D-058). So the cost of the crash is the whole live fleet, which is exactly the cost worth designing out.
- **The alternative was `--no-watch` on the dev launch.** One flag, and it leaves `orch` writing into the tree the operator is developing in — the isolation would depend on a launch argument nobody re-checks. The clone also makes a bad generation `rm -rf` rather than a git recovery, and it is the same shape workers already have (their own checkout, merges reviewed before they travel).
- **Not implemented, and nothing in code enforces it.** This is an operator procedure today: point the target picker at the clone. If live running shows the wrong target chosen by accident, the fix is a refusal at the target picker when the target resolves to the running app's own source — cheap, and worth waiting for evidence rather than guessing at.

## D-071 — the target is fixed once a pane exists, and the backend is what says so

- **What changed, and it is a behaviour change.** `fleet_set_target` and `fleet_pick_target` now **refuse** when the pane registry holds any pane. Both take `State<Arc<PaneRegistry>>` and funnel through one new private `adopt_target`, which checks `ensure_target_settable` before `write_target` and `apply_target`. A command that used to succeed unconditionally now fails in one case. Nothing about the start gate moves: with an empty registry both commands do exactly what they did.
- **The rule is not new; only its address is.** `Fleet.target`'s own doc comment already said the target is "safe to update before panes exist (the start gate); once panes are running the UI hides the target input" — so the invariant was written down, believed, and enforced entirely by `App.tsx:221`'s `!started` latch. That is the interface deciding what the thing that owns the state may do. Half a fleet in one repository and half in another is incoherent, which is the same reasoning that makes the target resolved once at bootstrap rather than re-read (D14).
- **The condition is `PaneRegistry` non-empty — existence, not membership and not liveness.** `roster()` was rejected because it filters to `is_fleet_member()`, so a session holding only the evaluator would answer "no panes" and accept a target change under a pane reading a run. `accepts_input()` was rejected because a pane that has *exited* was still seeded — config dir, worktree, brief, guardrail roots — against the target that was current when it spawned, and `PaneRegistry::spawn` respawns it in place rather than treating it as new. The map is emptied only by `kill`/`kill_all`, which is exactly the moment nothing holds the old target: kill every pane and the target is settable again, by construction rather than by a second rule. A poisoned registry answers "a pane exists" — the caller is a guard and must fail closed.
- **The broader condition that lost: "the fleet is bootstrapped."** `FleetState` being `Some` is what the interface actually keys on (`started` flips when `fleet_bootstrap` resolves, before any pane spawns) and it refuses strictly more often. It was rejected because it is not a reading of "a pane exists" — it is a different rule, and the honest version of it belongs with WP-21's half of D14, where the target snapshot and the evaluator wake become one source. Between bootstrap and the first spawn, `apply_target`'s in-place update is still correct and still the documented behaviour; refusing there would have been a rule this entry did not argue for. **The window is checked and it is not a gap:** `PaneContext` holds the *unrendered* templates and the launch config (`prompts.rs:78`) — nothing target-dependent — and `fleet_bootstrap` reads `f.target` live at each spawn (`fleet.rs:362`), so a target set between bootstrap and the first spawn is honoured in full. That is why the narrower condition costs nothing here.
- **One funnel, not two guards.** The picker also calls `ensure_target_settable` up front, before the folder dialog opens — making an operator browse to a folder and only then refusing it is a worse way to deliver the same sentence — but it still goes through `adopt_target`, so the picker is not a second place the rule has to be right. The refusal precedes `write_target` deliberately: a `config.json` recording a target no pane will ever spawn against is worse than no change at all.
- **The sentence, held as a constant so the test asserts on what the operator reads.** "the target is fixed for a running fleet — every pane was configured against the current one when it spawned. Stop the fleet (close the window) and set the target again at the start gate." **An operator who relied on the old behaviour** — calling `fleet_set_target` mid-run and expecting the next spawn to land in a new repo — never actually got that: panes already up kept the old one, so the result was the split fleet this refuses. The replacement move is the one in the sentence: close the window (which is `teardown_fleet`, killing every pane and shutting the hub), relaunch, set the target at the start gate, start.
- **The interface guard stays, and no UI file was touched.** `!started` still unmounts `StartGate`, which is the only component that calls either wrapper. It is now belt and braces: the interface keeps the control out of reach earlier than the backend refuses, and the backend refuses whatever reaches it.
- **What would reverse it.** A pane kind that genuinely does not care which repository the fleet is in — the Critic reading an *archived* run is the near candidate (WP-23), and the fix would be a predicate on the spec rather than a count of the map. Or a supported target switch that tears the fleet down and brings it back up as one action, at which point the refusal becomes a confirmation prompt in front of that action instead of a dead end.
- **Verified:** **177** shell tests green, up from **176** at `f542888` — 146 lib (1 new: `fleet::tests::the_target_is_settable_until_a_pane_exists`, which drives a real `PaneRegistry` over a real pty and asserts accepted-before-spawn, refused-after with both the claim and the remedy present in the message, and settable again after `kill_all`), 4 dev-mode, 8 evaluator, 11 real-pty, 8 write-guardrail — all unchanged. **213** workspace tests green, unchanged; no `crates/` file was touched. `tsc --noEmit && vite build` clean. `cargo clippy --all-targets` introduces no new warning (the three it reports are at `fleet.rs:1039`, `pty.rs:355` and `spawn.rs:193`, all pre-existing). No prompt template changed, so `brief-cost` is 0 and D-068's 51-token headroom is untouched. **Not verified:** the refusal has never been seen in the running app — no fleet has been started since this landed, the same honest gap D-062, D-065, D-066 and D-069 each record. Nothing exercises the two `#[tauri::command]` wrappers themselves; a `State<'_, T>` is not constructible outside a Tauri runtime, so the test drives `ensure_target_settable`, the function both wrappers pass through, and the wiring between them is held by reading the diff. No live spend.

## D-072 — the layout and the host: a pane's bring-up is a function of values, not of the operator's machine

- **What changed.** A new `src-tauri/src/placement.rs` owns the order a pane is brought up in, and `orch` — and only `orch`, this ticket — comes up through it. Two values arrive with it. **`Layout`** says where this fleet lives on disk: it is the one definition of the `~/.fleetor` tree, and `fleet.rs`'s nine location helpers are now each the operator's layout plus one accessor rather than nine independent re-derivations from `$HOME`. **`Host`** says what this machine has — the built `fleet` binary, the stand-in pane override, the operator's `HOME` and `PATH`, the Rust toolchain, the worker API key, the mission harness roots. `place(spec, &layout, &host, target, &context) -> Result<Placed>` returns the command, the notices and the gauge source. Workers and the evaluator keep the old sequence until tickets 03 and 04.
- **Why this is logged at all.** `building.md` puts internal module structure in Tier 3, and as a refactor this needs no entry. The entry is for the two values landing on the fleet's live state, because the next package will look for one — WP-22 and WP-23 both place panes, and "where does the layout come from" should have an answer that is not archaeology.
- **The rule that makes it worth doing: placement performs no process-global reads.** No home directory, no environment variable, no configuration path resolved inside `place`. Everything arrives in the layout, the host, the target or the context. That is not tidiness — it is the entire difference between a sequence that can be tested and one that cannot. The old path bottomed out in functions that read the operator's real `$HOME` at call time and took no argument, so it could only ever run against the operator's own installation. Each value has **two real constructors** so neither seam is hypothetical: `Layout::for_operator` / `Layout::under`, and `Host::discover` / `Host::bare`.
- **The host is discovered per spawn, not held on `Fleet` (D13).** This is what the code already did — binary, toolchain and key were each resolved at every spawn — so preserving it is what keeps this a pure refactor. It is also the better semantics: installing a toolchain mid-session takes effect on the next pane restart rather than being invisible until relaunch. The cost is a handful of filesystem checks, six times a session.
- **Notices are returned as `(level, text)` data, not written to the log from inside placement.** The same shape `prompts` and `guardrail` already use, and the reason placement needs no store. It is also what made "what does the operator actually read when orch comes up" assertable for the first time — before this, the only way to see those four lines was to run the application and read the Activity feed.
- **`PaneSpec` has one variant today, deliberately.** Stubbing `Worker`, `Evaluator` and `Critic` as arms that error was rejected: the live path for those kinds is still the old sequence, and `building.md` §6 is explicit that dead code implementing a pattern the design now bans is a trap, because the obvious next move on finding one is to re-point it at something live. Every variant that exists, works. Ticket 03 adds `Worker(slot)` in the commit that moves workers.
- **One duplicate implementation was refused rather than accepted.** `orch_command`, `augmented_path` and `base_command` each read the process, so placement could not call them. Each gained a `*_with`/`*_from` sibling taking those reads as arguments, and the reading function became a one-line wrapper onto it — one implementation, two entry points. `fleet.rs`'s `install_guardrail` likewise became the emitting wrapper over `placement::guardrail_notices`, so the evaluator's narrower roots have one home. Ticket 05 deletes the wrappers with their last callers.
- **What would reverse it.** Evidence that per-spawn host discovery is measurably slow enough to matter (it is six calls a session, so this would be surprising), which points at caching on `Fleet` and costs D13's mid-session semantics. Or a decision to make the state root operator-configurable, which the arc puts out of scope — `Layout` is the shape that change would need, which is a reason to prefer it here and not a reason to have built for it.
- **Verified:** **185** shell tests green, up from **176** — `tests/placement.rs` is new with 6 driven entirely through the seam against a scratch directory (orch's guardrail roots, the operator's `[fence] allow` extras reaching them, the notices and their order, the missing-`fleet`-binary warning off a bare host, containment, and the L1 seed keyed to the target), plus 3 net new lib tests in `placement`; one existing test moved from `fleet` to `placement` with the notice it pins, none deleted or weakened. **213** workspace tests green, unchanged — no `crates/` file was touched. `npm run build` green. The containment test was mutation-checked: pointing the config seed one directory outside the layout fails it with `escaped is outside both the layout and the target placement was handed`, and takes four of the other five with it.
