No prompt template changed, so `brief-cost` is 0 and D-068's 51-token headroom is untouched.
- **Not verified:** **no fleet has been started since this landed**, so no live `orch` pane has come up through `place` — the same honest gap D-062, D-065, D-066 and D-069 each record. The claim of no behaviour change rests on reading: the notice order is the old arm's order, `layout().socket()` is the old `socket_path()`, and `Host::orch_path()` feeds `augmented_path`'s own implementation the three values it used to read itself. No live spend. The four sites that independently re-derive `pane-config` (`guardrail::policy_dir`, `runs::harvest_transcripts`, `runs::copy_transcripts`, and the layout's own accessor) are **not** converged — that is the interface shrink ticket 05 does, and doing it here would have widened a refactor that is already the most-changed file in the repository.

## D-073 — the evaluator is a view in the rail, not a second window (supersedes the window half of D-066)

- **What changed.** The evaluator's terminal moved out of its own OS window and into the main window's rail, beside Terminals, Messages, Tasks, Activity and History, present only in dev mode. Four things went with the window: the `?window=evaluator` branch in `main.tsx` that chose which React root to mount (and `ui/src/EvaluatorWindow.tsx`, the root it chose), the `CloseRequested` interception in `lib.rs` that turned a close into a hide, the `"evaluator"` label in `capabilities/default.json`, and `fleet::open_evaluator_window` together with `evaluator::WINDOW_LABEL`. `wake_evaluator` keeps every line that decides *whether* there should be an evaluator — readiness, the answer key, the notices — and its tail now emits `evaluator://wake` to the one webview instead of building a window.
- **Why the default lost, and it is the same argument D-066 made, finished.** D-066's stated justification was that mounting `App` twice would give the app two webviews racing for one fleet. That is a true claim about the *root* and never was an argument for a *window*: it says the second window must not be a second `App`, not that a second window must exist. A view inside the single root has no race to lose, because there is no second webview. What the window cost was real — a close handler that could not tell which window it was closing (and for a while tore down all six ptys either way), capability scoping whose failure mode is silent, and a second root that had to re-derive the theme.
- **§7 rule 5 did not move; the level it is enforced at did.** The window survived being closed because the close was intercepted. The view survives being switched away from because it is hidden with `.is-hidden`, which is the mechanism every other view has always used. The terminal is mounted from launch and hidden until the wake, rather than mounted at the wake — both survive a switch, but only this one has no mount-once latch for a later session to reason about wrongly, and `TerminalPane`'s existing `started` prop (the start gate's own spawn gate) is what keeps a pty from existing before the handoff. The evaluator view hides three things and unmounts none, and the test asserts that as a count.
- **The wake is a new event rather than the `Handoff` the webview already receives.** Most handoffs must wake nothing — the mode is off, no grader is compiled in, or the target is not a prepared mission — and only Rust knows which. Deriving the wake frontend-side from `FleetEvent::Handoff` would have spawned an evaluator the readiness check had just refused. `evaluator://wake` carries that decision and nothing else. It is emitted from the same place `open_evaluator_window` was called, so the wake stayed downstream of the log: `spawn_evaluator_wake` is untouched, and `the_wake_is_a_bus_subscriber_and_not_something_the_hub_calls` is untouched and green.
- **What of D-066 survives, and it is most of it.** `PaneId::Evaluator` as a sixth variant in no enumeration; `is_fleet_member()` and the roster filter; the veil and both its tripwires; the `devmode` cargo feature and the brief compiled in from a separate repo through `OUT_DIR` with no override path; the three readiness conditions and their sentences; laying the live run out read-only under `~/.fleetor/dev/`; the config dir kept outside `_shell/pane-config/`; the narrowed guardrail roots and `--permission-mode auto`. **Only the window half is superseded.** D-066 also recorded the two bugs the second window exposed: the `channel_key` fix stands, and the `on_window_event` fix is now moot rather than reverted, because a single-window app has one meaning for a close.
- **The rail's ordering is load-bearing and cost one line.** The evaluator sits last among the workspace rows, after History and before Settings, because it is the only row that appears and disappears — putting it anywhere else means turning dev mode on shifts every row beneath it and the rail the operator has learned moves under them. Turning dev mode *off* while the evaluator view is selected is corrected in `App.tsx` rather than prevented, and on `=== false` rather than `!== true`, so nothing bounces off the view while the backend has not yet answered.
- **The view-restore list was already wrong, and about a view that predates this ticket.** `usePersistedNav.ts`'s `VIEWS` omitted **`history`** from the day History shipped: selecting it and relaunching parsed `"history"` back out of localStorage, failed `isValidView`, and landed on Terminals having said nothing — the identical fallback a genuinely corrupt value gets, which is why it survived a release. Both `history` and `evaluator` are on the list now, and `src-tauri/tests/views.rs` reads the `View` union in `Sidebar.tsx` and the `VIEWS` array in `usePersistedNav.ts` and fails, naming the missing views in both directions, if they stop agreeing. A Rust source-reading tripwire rather than a frontend test because the arc rules a frontend runner out by name; this is the one exception it reserved.
- **Two existing tests changed their letter and are stricter for it** (the move D-066 itself made). `the_evaluator_windows_terminal_is_never_conditionally_rendered` read a file this ticket deletes; its successor reads the evaluator's stage-view block in `App.tsx` and additionally asserts there is **no start control** (no `<button>`, no `onClick`) and that the stale `review` label is gone — two claims the old test could not make. `closing_the_evaluator_window_hides_it_and_does_not_kill_the_fleet` asserted the label branch, the close interception and the capability scoping; its successor asserts all three are **absent**, plus that no `WebviewWindowBuilder` or `get_webview_window` survives in `fleet.rs`, that `WINDOW_LABEL` is deleted rather than kept, and that `main.tsx` has no root branch. Net for the file: 8 tests to 9, and no assertion was dropped without a stronger one taking its place.
- **The `review` label is retired (D7).** The terminal was labelled `review`, which collides with peer review — the one thing about review the briefs actually teach a worker. It reads `evaluator` now. This is UI chrome the operator sees and no part of any brief, so the veil is untouched; `no_pane_is_briefed_about_the_evaluator` scans prompts and `brief.rs`, not `ui/`.
- **What would reverse it.** A measurement showing the operator genuinely needs the retro and the fleet on screen at once — which is a second window again, but then for a stated reason rather than an inherited one, and it would need its own root for D-066's original argument, which remains correct. Or the evaluator growing chrome that does not fit a stage view. Neither is evidence anyone has.
- **Verified:** **213** workspace tests green, unchanged, and no file under `crates/` was touched. **180** shell tests green, up from 176: 145 lib (unchanged), 4 dev-mode (unchanged), **9** evaluator (was 8), 11 real-pty (unchanged), **3 new in `tests/views.rs`**, 8 write-guardrail (unchanged). `cargo test --features devmode` green at 180 as well. `cargo build` produces no warnings. `npm run build` (`tsc --noEmit && vite build`) clean. The new tripwire was checked against the bug it exists for rather than only asserted: deleting `"history"` from `VIEWS` fails `the_rail_and_the_restore_list_name_the_same_views` and `history_is_restorable_which_it_was_not_before_d073`, printing both lists and naming `history`, and the file was restored and re-run green.
- **Not verified:** the frontend has no test runner and this arc adds none, so **nothing about the view's runtime behaviour is tested**: that the rail entry appears and disappears with dev mode, that the terminal and its scrollback survive a switch away and back, that the pre-handoff prose gives way to a live terminal on `evaluator://wake`, and that turning dev mode off while on the view moves the operator to Terminals — all four are source-read at best and are in the operator's by-hand list. No fleet has been started, so no handoff has fired the new event, no evaluator has reached a prompt in a view, and no retro has happened — the same gap D-062, D-065, D-066 and D-069 each record. `rustfmt` is not installed on this machine, so formatting was not checked. No live spend.

## D-074 — the evaluator is placed, dev mode is read through the layout, and the target is one cell

- **What changed.** `PaneSpec::Evaluator` joins `Orch` and `Worker(slot)`, so every pane kind that can exist now comes up through `placement::place`. The evaluator arm re-checks dev mode itself, lays the live run out under `~/.fleetor/dev/retro/<run-id>/`, renders the brief against that directory, seeds the config dir, installs the guardrail and builds the command — in that order, in one function. `fleet::spawn_pane`'s tail match is gone with it, and so are the two helpers only that arm used, `install_guardrail` and `evaluator_brief`. Separately, `Fleet.target` became a `Target` — one `Arc<Mutex<PathBuf>>` that the spawn path and the handoff watch both hold, rather than a field and a bootstrap-time copy of it.
- **Dev mode is read through the layout, not through the process (D11).** `Layout::dev_enabled()` is `dev::read_at(self.config_file())` — the same parse `dev::is_enabled` performs, against the config file inside the layout placement was handed. This is the one file read `place` performs that is neither an argument nor a write, and it is the rule applied rather than a hole in it: pointing the layout at a scratch directory points the mode at a scratch directory. It buys three properties at once that no other shape buys together. The spawn site genuinely re-checks, so `wake_evaluator` cannot lie to it — the existing code argued for that explicitly and had no way to test it. The mode stays read fresh at every placement, which is what D-061 promised. And a test can flip the flag without touching `~/.fleetor`. **Passing the mode in as a `bool` was rejected** for losing the first of those; it is what the code effectively did before, and it is why "unreachable outside dev mode" was a property of one caller rather than of the spawn site.
- **`evaluator::readiness` stopped reading the environment.** It resolved the harness, workspace and answer-key roots itself, from `FLEETOR_EVAL_*` or the operator's home — which is precisely what made it uncallable from placement. It takes a `&MissionRoots` now, which `Host::discover` already carried (D12). One consequence worth naming: the same target is `Ready` under one machine's roots and `NoMission` under another's, and that is a case a test can now write.
- **Laying the run out is part of placing, not a step before it (D2).** The evaluator's working directory *is* a snapshot of the run, so there is no order in which a caller could usefully do this first — the brief cites the directory, and a brief citing a directory nobody wrote costs the pane its first turn asking about it. The alternative on the table (the caller lays it out and hands a rendered brief in) reintroduces exactly what this module removes: a piece of the sequence living in the caller, plus a parameter the other arms discard.
- **The wake and the spawn path read one target (D14), and the doc comment that said so is true for the first time.** `wake_evaluator`'s comment claimed its target was "the same one `spawn_pane` renders the brief against". It was not: the watch was handed a `PathBuf` at bootstrap and kept it, while the spawn path read a field `apply_target` rewrites. Both now read one cell. **This changes no behaviour, and there is a reason to expect none rather than a hope:** D-071 fixed the target the moment a pane exists, and a handoff cannot happen before `orch` exists to send one, so the value the watch used to snapshot and the value it now reads can only differ in a fleet that never reaches a handoff. The behaviour-change half of D14 was that freeze, and it landed as D-071.
- **The write-guardrail test has no copy of the sequence left, which is the point of the ticket.** It used to invent a `_shell` and a worktree, call `guardrail::roots_for` and `guardrail::install` itself, and read the settings file it had just written. That copy had already drifted: `roots_for` is unconditional, so the hand-built version could never have produced the evaluator's narrower pair — the one pane whose roots matter most was the one pane the file could not test. Every `settings.json` it now runs is written by the same `place` call the application makes, against a scratch layout. **The seam it exists for is untouched:** the hook still goes through `sh -c`, still reads a genuine `PreToolUse` payload on stdin, and is still believed only when it answers. This is not a unit test of a Rust function and must not become one. `placement::guardrail_notices` is private again as a result — it had exactly one caller outside the module and one hand-typed impostor, and both are gone.
- **The evaluator's roots are now a checked property rather than a comment.** Its guardrail gets its own working directory and nothing else: not `_shell`, which holds the live event log it is reading, and not the operator's `[fence] allow` extras, which exist for the panes doing the work. Two tests hold it from different sides. `tests/placement.rs` reads the policy file placement wrote and compares it against `orch`'s three roots placed from the same layout and the same context, so "narrower" is a comparison and not a remembered number. `tests/write_guardrail.rs` runs the installed hook and watches the evaluator be refused all three directories `orch` is allowed on the same bench. This is the filesystem half of the veil; `PaneId`'s enumeration half is unchanged and its tripwires are untouched and green.
- **One shared test fixture was added rather than a second copy.** `src-tauri/tests/common/mod.rs` builds a whole scratch installation — layout, host, a prepared mission workspace, a real repository, a live SQLite run — because placing an evaluator has four preconditions and two test binaries assert different things about the same placement. A module for one thing, not a helpers grab-bag; retyping it in both files is the move this ticket exists to stop making.
- **What would reverse it.** A relocatable state root, which the arc puts out of scope: `Layout` is the shape that change needs, and `dev_enabled` would follow it for free. Or the Critic turning out to need a mode of its own, at which point "read the flag through the layout" becomes "read *which* flag through the layout" and the accessor takes an argument. Neither is evidence anyone has.
- **Verified:** **200** shell tests green under `cargo test --features devmode`, up from **195** at `edecac8` — 151 lib (was 150; one new, `evaluator::tests::readiness_judges_the_target_against_the_roots_it_was_handed`), 4 dev-mode, 8 evaluator, 11 real-pty, **14** placement (was 11), 3 views, **9** write-guardrail (was 8). **195** green under plain `cargo test` as well, where the four `devmode`-gated additions compile out. **213** workspace tests green, unchanged; no file under `crates/` was touched. `cargo build` and `cargo build --features devmode` produce no warnings; `npm run build` clean. `cargo clippy --all-targets --features devmode` reports the same three pre-existing warnings and no new one. Both new veil assertions were mutation-checked rather than only asserted: replacing the evaluator's roots branch with the worker's fails `the_evaluators_write_roots_are_its_own_directory_alone_and_no_pane_is_narrower` and `the_evaluator_may_write_in_the_run_it_was_given_and_in_nothing_else`, and replacing `layout.dev_enabled()` with `dev::is_enabled()` fails `placing_an_evaluator_reads_the_mode_from_the_layouts_own_config` on this machine, whose real config has the mode on. No test was deleted, weakened or marked ignored; six in `tests/write_guardrail.rs` changed their setup and kept every assertion.
- **Not verified:** **no fleet has been started since this landed**, so no evaluator has woken through `place`, no handoff has fired, and no retro has happened — the same honest gap D-062, D-065, D-066, D-069, D-071, D-072 and D-073 each record. The claim that the evaluator's command is unchanged rests on reading: `spawn::evaluator_command` now delegates to `evaluator_command_with`, and the two values placement hands it — `host.pane_program` and `host.orch_path()` — are what `pane_program()` and `augmented_path()` would have read. Nothing asserts the two commands are byte-identical. The mutation check on the mode is only meaningful on a machine whose real `config.json` has dev mode **on**; on a machine with it off, that test would pass even if placement read the process, and nothing detects that. `rustfmt` is not installed on this machine, so formatting was not checked. No live spend.

## D-075 — the process-shaping module becomes an internal of placement, and the layout goes on the fleet

- **What changed.** `src-tauri/src/spawn.rs` is now `src-tauri/src/placement/spawn.rs`, a **child module of `placement`** rather than its sibling, and every seeding and command-building function in it is `pub(super)`. Nothing outside `placement/mod.rs` can call them at all — not `fleet`, not a test binary — so they cannot be called in the wrong order by anything that is not the module that owns the order. `lib.rs` no longer exports `spawn`. Alongside it: `Layout` is held on `Fleet`, resolved once at bootstrap; `fleet.rs`'s five remaining location helpers (`fleetor_dir`, `shell_dir`, `testbed_dir`, `config_path`, `socket_path`) are gone with the sequence that needed them, leaving `layout()` alone; the two machine-level warnings moved into `placement::machine_notices`; and `"pane-config"` is spelled once, in `placement::pane_config_root`.
- **Why this is logged at all.** `building.md` puts internal module structure in Tier 3 and a refactor needs no entry. Two things here are not internal structure. `Layout` landing on the fleet's live state is the answer to "where does a pane's layout come from" that WP-22 and WP-23 will both ask, and D-072 logged the value for exactly that reason. And the visibility rule is a claim about what a future ticket *cannot* do, which is worth more written down than discovered.
- **The seam `spawn.rs` presented was hypothetical, and the test for that is who calls it.** Every seeding and command-building function in it had exactly one production caller after ticket 04, and that caller was `placement`. A public function with one caller is not an interface, it is an invitation: the next agent needing a seeded config dir calls `seed_config_dir` and skips the guardrail install that has to follow it, and nothing fails until an operator's pane can rewrite its own hook policy. `pub(super)` inside a child module is what makes that a compile error rather than a code review. Verified as a compile error, not asserted: adding `placement::spawn::seed_config_dir(...)` to `fleet::spawn_pane` fails with `function seed_config_dir is private`.
- **The reading wrappers are deleted, and the tests that wanted them keep every assertion.** `orch_command`, `evaluator_command`, `worker_command`, `augmented_path` and `worker_augmented_path` were each one line: the `*_with` implementation plus the process reads `Host::discover` now performs. `evaluator_command` had no caller at all. The other four had none in production and sixteen in `spawn.rs`'s own unit tests, so what was left was scaffolding — and **scaffolding belongs in the test module**, which is where those four now are, `#[cfg(test)]`, unchanged in body. Every test in that file keeps its letter, including the one that sets `HOME=/Users/operator` and reads the rungs back: what moved is *where* the environment is read, and it is read in the test rather than in the shipped module. **This loses no coverage of production**, because production stopped calling those wrappers in ticket 02 — `place` has gone through `Host` since then. The alternative on the table (have each test call `augmented_path_from` with literals) would have dropped the `$HOME` read from the test's letter, which is a weakening, so it was refused.
- **`tests/panes.rs` drives `placement::place` now, and that is stricter rather than merely different.** It was the last thing outside `placement` that knew how a pane is shaped: it built five commands by hand and spawned them on real ptys. It builds them by placing them, against a scratch layout and a `Host` that carries the stand-in program as a field — the same override, handed in instead of read out of `FLEETOR_PANE_CMD`, so the file no longer sets a process-global at all. The seeding and the guardrail install now happen on the way to every pty this file opens, which they did not before. Its target is deliberately **not** a git repository: a worker placed against it takes the announced shared-checkout fallback, so running the test suite branches nothing and creates no worktree of the repository it is running inside.
- **`Layout` is on `Fleet`; `Host` is deliberately not, and getting that backwards is a bug no test would catch.** The state root cannot move under a running fleet, so resolving it once and holding it is both cheaper and more honest than six accessors re-deriving it from `$HOME`. What the machine *has* is the opposite: a rustup installed mid-session, an `.env` saved, a `fleet` binary built. Caching those at bootstrap makes them invisible until relaunch — the exact staleness D13 exists to avoid — and it fails silently, because a stale `Host` produces a perfectly well-formed command. So `spawn_pane` discovers one per spawn, and now discovers it **once** per spawn: `warn_about_the_machine` used to call `fleet_bin_path` and `operator_toolchain` itself, a second independent read of the same machine that could in principle disagree with the one the pane was placed against.
- **`machine_notices` is the one part of bringing a pane up that the caller performs, and the reason is ordering.** Those two lines have to reach the operator even when the placement that follows returns `Err` — a machine with no worker key would otherwise be told only about the key. What moved into `placement` is the decision: which sentences, under which conditions, off which `Host`. `fleet.rs` holds neither. `orch` is excluded there because `place_orch` emits the `fleet`-binary line from the same constant, and saying it twice is how an operator learns to stop reading the feed.
- **Four spellings of `"pane-config"` became one, and they were never four independent facts.** `Layout::pane_config`, `guardrail::policy_dir` and the two transcript scans in `runs` each wrote `shell.join("pane-config")` out again. The guardrail refuses writes to exactly the directory the layout puts configs in, and rotation archives a transcript by walking exactly that directory — so a rename reaching three of them leaves either a fleet whose panes can rewrite their own hook policy or one whose orchestrator's transcript stops being archived, and no test would say which. `placement::pane_config_root(shell)` takes the `_shell` path rather than the whole `Layout` because the two callers outside `placement` hold one and not the other; converging their signatures onto `Layout` would have meant changing `runs::rotate` and `runs::snapshot_live_run` and the twenty-five test call sites under them, which is a wider ticket than this one.
- **The missing-key refusal names the directory again (the deferred cleanup this ticket owed).** `MISSING_API_KEY` was a constant, and a constant cannot say where it looked; the sentence the old sequence emitted did. It is `missing_api_key(searched_from)` now, fed by a new `Host::api_key_searched_from` and by `fleet::api_key_search_start`, which is also what `load_api_key`'s own walk starts from — one spelling, so the message names the directory that was actually searched. This is the operator-actionable half: under `tauri dev` the working directory is `src-tauri/`, so a key at the repo root *is* found by the walk, and an operator whose key is elsewhere can only tell which case they are in if the message says where it looked.
- **What would reverse it.** A second consumer of the process-shaping functions that is genuinely not a placement — a `fleet doctor` that wants to print the PATH a worker would get, say. That is a reason to make `placement` answer the question, not a reason to re-export the internals. Or a relocatable state root, which the arc puts out of scope and which `Layout` is already the shape of.
- **Verified:** **202** shell tests green under `cargo test --features devmode`, up from **200** at `1f69427` — 151 lib (unchanged), 4 dev-mode, 8 evaluator, 11 real-pty, **16** placement (was 14), 3 views, 9 write-guardrail. **197** green under plain `cargo test`, up from 195, where the three `devmode`-gated placement tests compile out. **213** workspace tests green, unchanged; no file under `crates/` was touched. `cargo build` and `cargo build --features devmode` produce no warnings; `npm run build` clean. `cargo clippy --all-targets --features devmode` reports **two** warnings, down from three: the third was `this function has too many arguments (8/7)` on `spawn::worker_command`, which this ticket deletes. **No test was deleted, weakened or marked ignored.** Both new tests were mutation-checked rather than only asserted: dropping the path from `missing_api_key` fails `a_worker_with_no_key_is_told_where_the_key_was_looked_for`, and removing the `orch` early return from `machine_notices` fails `a_bare_machine_warns_about_the_fleet_binary_and_the_toolchain_but_never_for_orch`. The visibility claim was checked by making the compiler enforce it (above). No source-reading tripwire names `src/spawn.rs` or `src/placement.rs` by path, so none of them silently stopped reading a file when the two moved — checked by grep over `tests/` before the move and after.
- **Not verified:** **no fleet has been started since this landed**, so no pane of any kind has come up through the moved module — the same honest gap D-062, D-065, D-066, D-069, D-071, D-072, D-073 and D-074 each record. The claim of no behaviour change rests on reading and on the suite: nothing asserts that the five commands are byte-identical to the ones the deleted wrappers built, and `tests/panes.rs` now proves the panes spawn and talk rather than proving their commands are unchanged. **`fleet.rs` did not get materially smaller**, which is one of this ticket's stated goals: 844 code lines to 831, and 1474 total lines to 1482 because the doc comments grew. Ticket 04 had already taken the bulk — the bring-up match and the two helpers only its evaluator arm used — and what was left for this ticket was five one-line location helpers and two process reads. The *composition* claim holds and the line count does not, and it is recorded here rather than rounded up. `rustfmt` is not installed on this machine, so formatting was not checked. No prompt template changed, so `brief-cost` is 0. No live spend.

## D-076 — the Critic: a fourth pane identity, a fifth brief, and a sixth finding category the spike earned

- **What changed.** `PaneId::Critic` joins `Orch`, `Worker(n)`, `Operator` and `Evaluator`, and `PaneSpec::Critic { run: RunSource }` joins the three placement variants. A new `src-tauri/src/critic.rs` says what it is told and where it works; `prompts/critic.md` is its brief, baked in with `include_str!` and overridable from `~/.fleetor/prompts/critic.md` like every other brief; `PaneContext` carries a third template. In the interface it is a rail row and an always-mounted stage view with one Start button. Neither `prompts/orch.md` nor `prompts/worker.md` changed by a byte, and `crates/fleetor-core/src/brief.rs` — where `VERBS` lives — was not touched at all, so `brief-cost` for this ticket is **0**.
- **The one fact every asymmetry falls out of: the Critic is a reader, not a participant.** `Operator` has no pty; `Evaluator` has a pty and is hidden; this has a pty and is simply not *in* the fleet. From that one fact: nothing enumerates a non-participant, so `is_fleet_member()` is false and it is therefore in no `fleet roster` listing, is no leg of a `fleet broadcast`, and is in no brief's peer list — all three through the single filter in `PaneRegistry::roster`, which is what `Hub::roster` and `Hub::broadcast` both read. And it is given **no `FLEET_SOCKET` and no `FLEETOR_PANE`**, which is what "it cannot message the fleet" means in code rather than in prose: the `fleet` CLI resolves that variable before it does anything else, so a `fleet send` typed inside the Critic exits non-zero with the sentence naming the variable and no pty is written to. `spawn::apply_pane_env` split into `apply_terminal_env` (what makes a process one of this app's terminals) plus the two fleet variables, so the Critic can have the first and not the second.
- **It is openly named, and that is the load-bearing difference from the evaluator (D5, D7).** The evaluator is hidden because WP-12's veil is what makes a handoff evidence rather than performance. Nothing about the Critic depends on the fleet not knowing it exists, so its brief is a file the operator can read and rewrite, it exists whether or not the mode is on, and no tripwire greps the fleet's prose for the word `critic`. **The veil tripwires were not weakened to make room for it**, and one of them caught this change while it was being written: `no_pane_is_briefed_about_dev_mode` failed on doc comments in `pane.rs` that named the mode and its test file while explaining the Critic. The prose was rewritten; the grep was not.
- **`prompts/critic.md` joined `WHAT_A_PANE_IS_TOLD` and deliberately did not join `WHAT_A_PANE_IS_TOLD_IN_PROSE`, and the reason is one phrase.** It is on the mode list because it is prose a terminal is handed and it has no business naming the mode — adding it cost nothing, since it names no spelling of it. It is off the evaluator-vocabulary list because six of that list's seven needles never appear in it and the seventh, `answer key`, appears in *"You have no answer key"* — D10's own phrase for why this pane judges the run and never the work. Putting the file on that list leaves exactly two moves, editing the prompt the spike converged on or widening a needle so it stops catching what it was written for, and **neither is worth a grep on a file no fleet pane reads.** What replaced it is `the_critics_brief_is_openly_named_and_names_no_evaluator_vocabulary`, which asserts the other six on that file, plus `the_evaluators_needles_still_mean_the_evaluator_and_never_the_critic`, which pins the six needles as literals and checks that none of them overlaps this pane's name — the collision D7 renamed it to avoid.
- **The brief is the spike's v3 prompt, and it ships with six categories rather than D10's five — a deliberate departure, on the spike's evidence.** `docs/notes/critic-spike-notes.md` measured two runs under three prompts. D10's categories 3 (blocks with no criteria) and 4 (two workers editing one file) produced **nothing on either run**, and the sixth category the spike added — *"Anything else the fleet did that cost the operator"* — produced the largest finding on **both**: run A's reviewed work never integrated because the integration worktree belonged to a different repository, and run B's `orch` abandoning its own workers to edit the target in place, uncommitted, until the operator interrupted it. Without a sixth slot the Critic reports a tidy run and misses what actually cost the money. The operator approved the addition. **It is a bounded slot, not an opinion slot**, and the spike is explicit that this is why it did not reopen the mush door: it carries the same citation rule, bans inference and prescription by name, and sends any moment that cannot be cited to UNCITED. That wording is not to be loosened.
- **The failure mode the brief is shaped against is not the one the arc predicted.** The spike's control — the same archive, an unconstrained prompt — did not produce "solid work, maybe more tests". It produced fluent, substantive, largely **true** prose with not one citation in it, judgements of the *work* ("wasn't a rubber stamp"), and prescriptions ("should be namespaced per-target"). Mush would be obvious and harmless; this is neither, because an operator cannot separate its true claims from its unfalsifiable ones. The two clauses doing the work are the **citation rule** and the **ban on prescription**, and `prompts/README.md` now says so with the measurement behind it, because story 35 makes the brief the operator's to edit and nothing defends against them being trimmed.
- **Its write-guardrail roots are its own working directory alone, like the evaluator's.** `placement::guardrail_notices` branched on `pane.is_evaluator()`; it is `pane.is_evaluator() || pane.is_critic()` now — **two names on one branch rather than a shared `PaneId::reads_a_run()` predicate**, because D5 refused one identity with a mode flag and a shared predicate would read as one. Not `_shell`, which holds the live event log it is reading, and not the operator's `[fence] allow` extras, which exist for the panes doing the work. Asserted the way ticket 04 asserted the evaluator's: `orch` is placed from the same layout with the same context in the same test, so "narrower" is a comparison and not a remembered number. Its config dir is `~/.fleetor/critic/config`, outside `_shell/pane-config/` — otherwise `runs::copy_transcripts` would copy the Critic's own transcript into the directory it is reading, as it works, and `harvest_transcripts` would file it into the run archive where the next run's Critic would read it as if it were the fleet's.
- **`RunSource` is declared in full and only `Live` is built, which bends this module's own rule on purpose.** `placement`'s doc says every variant that exists, works — a variant that errored is the `building.md` §6 trap, because the obvious next move on finding one is to point it at something live. `RunSource::Archived(id)` is declared because the design prototype's shape is what a Critic *is* and the type should say so, it is **constructed nowhere in the application**, and `place_critic` answers it with `ARCHIVED_NOT_BUILT` naming ticket 09. The §6 trap does not apply because there is no other live path to re-point it at: reading a past run does not exist anywhere yet. The alternative — falling through to the live run — was refused as the worst of the three, since the operator would get a pane that looked right and was reading a different run than the one they asked for.
- **Two match arms answer questions rather than filling holes, and both follow from the identity.** `machine_notices` now returns nothing for the Critic, for the opposite reason it returns nothing for `orch`: `orch` emits the `fleet`-binary line itself, and for the Critic that line would be **false** — it has no socket, so building a `fleet` binary would change nothing about it, and "panes cannot message each other" would send the operator to fix something irrelevant. And `place_critic` takes no `target`: the Critic reads a run, the repository the fleet was pointed at is named inside `manifest.json` where it reads it like every other fact, and a pane that reads a run has no business holding the live checkout. Every other arm — `slot()`, `Display`, `FromStr`, `channel_key`, `spawn_pane`'s spec match — is a one-liner that follows directly.
- **`FromStr` accepts `critic` for exactly one reason, and it is not that a model types it.** `pty_spawn` takes a `PaneId` and Tauri deserializes it out of the webview's JSON, so the name has to parse. No pane can learn it: it is in no brief, no roster and not in `ParsePaneIdError`'s list, which `pane.rs` still documents as the fleet's own names and nothing else.
- **What would reverse it.** A measurement showing the brief produces the control's failure mode under a cheaper model — the spike ran on Sonnet and explicitly does not establish that a smaller model holds the discipline, so putting one behind this pane means repeating that measurement first. Or a third run-reading pane, at which point two names on the guardrail branch becomes three and a predicate on `PaneId` starts being cheaper than the ambiguity it costs. Or `fleet task post` changing so criteria become optional, which is the one change that would make D10's category 3 start earning its keep and would weaken the case for category 6 carrying the run.
- **Verified:** **219** shell tests green under `cargo test --features devmode`, up from **202** at `bf52164` — 157 lib (was 151; six new, in `critic::tests` and `prompts::tests`), **7** dev-mode (was 4), 8 evaluator, 11 real-pty, **22** placement (was 16), **5** views (was 3), 9 write-guardrail. **214** green under plain `cargo test`, up from **197**: every one of the six Critic placement tests runs without the `devmode` feature, which is the assertion that it is a product feature. **215** workspace tests green from the repo root, up from **213** — two new `PaneId` tests in `crates/fleetor-core`. `cargo build`, `cargo build --features devmode` and `npm run build` all clean. `cargo clippy --all-targets --features devmode` reports the same **two** pre-existing warnings and no new one. A diff of `prompts/orch.md` and `prompts/worker.md` against `bf52164` is empty, and `crates/fleetor-core/src/brief.rs` is untouched, so the verb list is unchanged by construction. **No test was deleted, weakened or marked ignored**; one existing test (`pty.rs`'s roster test) gained the Critic beside the evaluator, and `tests/placement.rs`'s `mod common` lost its `devmode` gate so a default build can use the fixture. Four claims were mutation-checked rather than only asserted: giving the Critic `apply_pane_env` fails `the_critic_is_given_no_socket_so_fleet_send_inside_it_reaches_nothing`; narrowing the guardrail branch back to `is_evaluator()` alone fails `the_critics_write_roots_are_its_own_directory_alone_and_orch_is_wider`; dropping `Critic` from `is_fleet_member` fails `a_running_evaluator_is_not_on_the_fleets_roster`, which is the one filter `fleet roster` and `fleet broadcast` both read; and adding `prompts/critic.md` to `WHAT_A_PANE_IS_TOLD_IN_PROSE` fails `no_pane_is_briefed_about_the_evaluator` on `prompts/critic.md:14` alone, which is the evidence for the paragraph above rather than an inference.
- **Not verified:** **no fleet has been started since this landed, and no Critic has ever run** — the same honest gap D-062, D-065, D-066, D-069, D-071, D-072, D-073, D-074 and D-075 each record. Nothing here measures what a Critic pointed at a live snapshot actually produces; the spike measured *archived* runs read by an ordinary session with the prompt pasted in, never this pane, never this placement, never `snapshot_live_run`'s output. **One wrinkle in the shipped brief is known and was left verbatim:** its archive listing names `state.db`, which a live snapshot does not contain — `runs::snapshot_live_run` writes `events.json`, `manifest.json` and `transcripts/` only, and that manifest says "There is no state.db here" in so many words. The brief was shipped as the spike wrote it because the ticket asks for the prompt the spike converged on, and ticket 09's archived runs *do* have a `state.db`; whether a Critic burns a turn on the missing file is unmeasured. **The one-directional claim is the one that is tested.** `fleet send` from inside the Critic reaches nothing, structurally. The reverse — a pane sending *to* `critic` — is not blocked in the hub, and would write into the Critic's terminal if a pane ever typed that string; it is protected the way the evaluator's is, by the name being absent from every brief, every roster and the parse error, and nothing more. Closing it would mean a branch in the delivery path, which Tier 1.4 forbids. The Critic's cwd is a snapshot of the live run, so in a session where the evaluator has already messaged `orch`, the word `evaluator` is in the `events.json` the Critic reads — that is run data rather than prose and nothing in this ticket changes it, but it is the one place the two panes can meet. `rustfmt` is not installed on this machine, so formatting was not checked. No live spend.

## D-077 — `fleet reply` refuses a recipient at parse, and the discriminator is the argv element rather than the first word

- **What changed.** One match arm in `crates/fleetor-cli/src/main.rs` — `Command::Reply { text } => Op::Reply { text: reply_text(text)? }` — and the `reply_text` function it calls, which refuses when `text.first()` parses as a `PaneId` and otherwise returns `join(text)` exactly as before. **No verb was added, renamed or removed.** `crates/fleetor-core/src/brief.rs` (where `VERBS` lives), `crates/fleetor-core/src/wire.rs` (where `Op` lives), the clap `Command` enum, `prompts/orch.md` and `prompts/worker.md` are all byte-unchanged — `git diff` on the four files is empty and the only hunks in `main.rs` are at the match arm, at the new function, and in `mod tests`. `brief-cost` is **0**.
- **The defect is measured, and its shape is the reason it went unnoticed.** `Op::Reply { text }` has no recipient field, unlike `Op::Send { to, text }`, and `Command::Reply`'s `text` is `trailing_var_arg`. So `fleet reply worker-3 "Agreed — Rooftop Garden"` parses cleanly, prepends `worker-3` to the *message body*, and delivers the whole thing to whoever messaged the sender last. Nothing failed, so nothing was corrected. A 5m 40s run produced **nine** misdeliveries; two panes spent turns diagnosing "tangled cross-replies" and one re-sent three confirmations it had already sent. The panes were not being careless: `prompts/worker.md:23` teaches ``fleet reply "<text>"`` on the line *below* ``fleet send orch "<text>"``, and a name argument pattern-matched across. **The brief is correct and was not changed** — a verb whose only safe use requires the reader not to generalise from the line above it is the thing to fix, not the line.
- **The discriminator is the first argv element, not the first word of the joined string, and that is the whole of the care this needed.** `fleet reply worker-3 "Agreed"` arrives as `["worker-3", "Agreed"]` and is refused; `fleet reply "worker-3 said the annex is out"` arrives as one element that is not a pane name and goes through untouched. A pane must be able to open a message with a peer's name — quoting one, reporting on one, arguing with one — and **replacing a silent misroute with a silent refusal would be the worse trade of the two.** The first-word reading was mutation-checked, not merely avoided: swapping `text.first()` for `join(text).split_whitespace().next()` fails two tests.
- **It asks `PaneId::from_str` rather than matching spellings of its own**, so it is exactly as wide as `fleet send`'s recipient and cannot drift from it: `orch`, `orchestrator`, `lead`, `o`, `operator`, `critic`, the out-of-fleet grader's own name, and every worker as `3` / `w3` / `worker3` / `worker-3`, all case-insensitive and trimmed. `self` is deliberately *not* caught, because `fleet send self` is not a thing — only `fleet cmd` resolves that word, in `target()` — so a reply may start with it. A first token beginning with `-` never reaches the check at all; clap rejects it first, `-1` included.
- **Tier 1.4 holds because it refuses at parse, and that is not a matter of degree.** `reply_text` runs inside `run()`'s `let op = match cli.command`, in the same position as `Command::Send`'s `pane.parse::<PaneId>()` — **before** the tokio runtime is built, before `UnixTransport::new(socket()?)`, before `Client::connect`, before an `Op` exists. Nothing between a `fleet send` and a pty gained the ability to delay, refuse, reorder, drop or alter anything; this is the same shape of failure `fleet send worker-9` produces today. A check downstream of the socket would have been the wrong implementation of the same sentence.
- **WP-15's Tier 1.4 grep caught this ticket while it was being written**, which is worth recording because it is the second time (D-076 records the first): `the_delivery_path_cannot_see_the_evaluator` failed on two test-data literals in `main.rs`. The literals moved to `crates/fleetor-cli/tests/reply_recipient.rs`, which is not a file a message passes through; the grep was not touched. **The refusal message echoes back only the word the pane itself typed**, so it teaches no name to a pane that did not already have one — the same property `ParsePaneIdError` keeps by listing the fleet's names and nothing else.
- **What would reverse it.** A measured case of a pane wanting to send a one-token message that is exactly a pane name (`fleet reply orch`, `fleet reply 3`) — those are refused today and the refusal is the cost of the rule, unmeasured. Or a recipient field arriving on `Op::Reply`, at which point the refusal becomes a redirect and this entry is superseded rather than amended.
- **Verified:** **222** workspace tests green from the repo root, up from **215** at `bc9ef09` — four new unit tests in `crates/fleetor-cli/src/main.rs` beside the existing parse tests, three new integration tests in `crates/fleetor-cli/tests/reply_recipient.rs`. **219** shell tests green under `cargo test --features devmode` and **214** under plain `cargo test`, both unchanged: the shell does not test the CLI. `cargo build --features devmode` and `npm run build` clean. `cargo clippy --all-targets --features devmode` reports the same **two** pre-existing warnings and no new one; `cargo clippy --all-targets --workspace` from the root reports the same **three** pre-existing `fleetor-core` warnings and no new one. **No test was deleted, weakened or marked ignored.** The exit code and stderr are asserted against the real binary rather than against a bool: `naming_a_recipient_exits_non_zero_and_says_so_on_stderr` runs `env!("CARGO_BIN_EXE_fleet")` with `FLEETOR_PANE` and `FLEET_SOCKET` removed and pins `status.code() == Some(1)`, the exact sentence, and an empty stdout. `a_message_that_begins_with_a_peers_name_is_not_refused_here` proves the narrow half from the other side — that argv shape gets past every local check and fails on the missing socket instead, which is as far as a test without a hub can push it.
- **Not verified:** **no fleet has been started since this landed** — the same honest gap D-062, D-065, D-066, D-069, D-071, D-072, D-073, D-074, D-075 and D-076 each record. **The nine misdeliveries are the operator's measurement of a run this session did not observe**, and neither the two panes' diagnosis turns nor the three re-sent confirmations were re-derived from the archive here; they are the stated reason for the change and are taken as given. Nothing measures whether a pane that hits this refusal actually re-types `fleet send <pane> "…"` on its next turn rather than dropping the message or arguing with the tool — the sentence is shaped for that and the shape is untested against a live model. **`fleet broadcast` has the identical argv shape and was deliberately not touched**: `fleet broadcast worker-3 "…"` also glues the name onto the body, but broadcast has no recipient to get wrong, so the cost is a stray word rather than a misroute, and no measurement says it happens. `rustfmt` is not installed on this machine, so formatting was not checked. No live spend.

## D-078 — `fleet broadcast` refuses a recipient too, and the two message verbs move together

- **What changed.** `Command::Broadcast` routes through a new `broadcast_text`, the twin of D-077's `reply_text`: if the first argv element parses as a `PaneId`, it refuses at parse rather than gluing the name onto the body. Both verbs ask `PaneId::from_str` rather than matching spellings, so neither can drift from `fleet send`'s recipient or from each other — pinned by `broadcast_and_reply_refuse_exactly_the_same_set_of_names`, which asserts the two verbs agree on twenty candidates.
- **The severity is *not* D-077's, and the entry says so rather than borrowing its evidence.** A misaddressed `reply` is a misroute — the wrong pane gets the message and the right one never does, which is the measured defect: nine silent misdeliveries in one 5m 40s run. A `broadcast` has no recipient to get wrong; everyone receives it either way, so the unfixed cost is a stray leading word. **No measurement says it has ever happened.** It is caught because the *other* reading is expensive: `fleet broadcast worker-3 "…"` from a pane that meant to reach one peer tells all four, which `prompts/worker.md` already calls "almost never the right call".
- **A false positive is created here that D-077 does not have, and it was accepted deliberately.** A broadcast plausibly opens with a peer's name — "worker-3 is blocked on the schema" is an ordinary announcement, and a pane typing it unquoted is now refused for writing correct prose. A reply has no such shape in practice. **So the refusal names both remedies** — send to one pane, or quote the message — instead of assuming which was meant, and either correction is one line in the turn the pane is already in. Rejected: refusing with only `fleet send`'s spelling, which would tell a pane making a legitimate announcement to do the one thing it did not want.
- **Tier 1.4 holds for the same reason D-077's does.** The check runs in `run()`'s `let op = match cli.command` block, where `Command::Send`'s own `pane.parse::<PaneId>()` runs — before the runtime is built, before the socket is dialled, before any `Op` exists. Nothing between a `fleet send` and a pty gained the ability to refuse anything.
- **No verb moved.** `VERBS`, the clap `Command` enum, `Op`, `prompts/orch.md` and `prompts/worker.md` are byte-unchanged; the briefs already teach `fleet broadcast "<text>"` with quotes, so no pane is taught anything new.
- **What would reverse it.** A measured case of a pane wanting to broadcast a message whose first word is a bare pane name often enough that quoting is a real cost. Nothing observed suggests it.
- **Verified.** 226 workspace tests green, up from 222 at `97bfd43` — four added, none changed or deleted. Refusal and pass-through both exercised against the built binary through `cargo run`, not only in-process: `broadcast worker-3 is blocked`, `broadcast 3 hi` and `broadcast orch done` refuse; `broadcast "worker-3 is blocked on the schema"` and `broadcast heads up worker-3` go through. `cargo clippy --all-targets --workspace` output is byte-identical before and after (the same three pre-existing `fleetor-core` lib-test warnings). `cargo build` clean.
- **Not verified.** No fleet has been started since this landed, so no pane has met the refusal in a live run. The claim that broadcasting to four panes what belonged in one message is the expensive error is an argument from the brief's own wording, not a measurement — unlike D-077's nine misdeliveries, which were counted.

## D-079 — the Critic gets a socket and a switch, and the switch is in the hub

- **What changed.** `spawn::critic_command_with` now calls `apply_pane_env(&mut cmd, PaneId::Critic, socket, path)` exactly as `evaluator_command_with` does, so the Critic holds `FLEETOR_PANE` and `FLEET_SOCKET` unconditionally. The operator's control over whether it may *use* them is a `bool` on the hub (`hub::Interview`, a handle on one cell shared with `Fleet.interview`), read at the top of `Hub::handle`: while the interview is closed, an op whose `from` is `PaneId::Critic` returns `OpResult::Error` before the `match op`. Two Tauri commands move and read it — `critic_interview_open(open) -> bool` and `critic_interview_is_open() -> bool`, both registered in `lib.rs` — and the setter writes a `Notice` on each edge naming which it was. Default: closed.
- **Why the switch is not at spawn, which is where the arc doc's first reading puts it.** `FLEET_SOCKET` is fixed on the `CommandBuilder` when the pty is created. "Hand it a socket when the operator opens the interview" is therefore not buildable without respawning the pane — destroying the operator's conversation with the pane they just decided to let speak. The socket had to become unconditional and the gate had to move. Rejected: an operator control that respawns the Critic, for that reason; and gating on `fleet handoff` instead of on the operator, which WP-21 already rejected because some lapses are only visible while they are happening.
- **Tier 1.4 — refused at accept time, never accepted-then-dropped.** `building.md` §9.3 records this argument as had and lost twice, so the compliance claim is stated as what the early return *skips*: no `Message` is constructed, no `AppCommand` is sent, `self.app` is not touched, `self.store` is not touched, and `last_inbound_from` is not written — so no pane's next `fleet reply` is silently redirected either. This is the class `Hub::cmd`'s allowlist check already is (its own comment: "nothing enters the delivery path"), not a mute on a live route: open, the line is not reached and the Critic's ops take the identical path every other sender's do. The property is tested rather than asserted — `panes.rs::a_closed_interview_refuses_the_critic_and_leaves_the_event_log_untouched` reads the whole event log either side of a refused send and requires it not to have moved, on a real pty with a real store.
- **The refusal names the switch, not the addressee.** The arc doc's shorthand is that a closed interview fails "with 'no such pane'", copying WP-19's cross-fleet cutoff. Taken literally here it would be false: `orch` exists, and blaming it for a state of the operator's control is the log-lie L3 is about, one layer up. The sentence is instead *"the interview is closed, so no pane is an address from here and nothing was sent — the operator opens it with the control in the Critic view"*: the same fact — the Critic has no addresses right now — said without a false claim about the pane it named. The `fleet` CLI prints it to stderr and exits 1.
- **`is_fleet_member` did not move, and the test now asserts that with the socket present.** `tests/placement.rs::the_critic_is_given_no_socket_so_fleet_send_inside_it_reaches_nothing` was inverted rather than deleted, as `the_critic_is_given_a_socket_and_is_still_not_a_member_of_the_fleet`: it keeps the `orch` comparison (a bench that stopped setting sockets for everyone would now fail rather than pass), asserts both variables are present and equal to `orch`'s socket, and then asserts `!PaneId::Critic.is_fleet_member()` and its absence from `PaneId::roster` — no roster row, no broadcast leg, no peer list, with a socket in hand. That is the design: addressable, never enumerated. The write guardrail is untouched: the Critic's roots are still its own working directory alone. It gained a voice, not a pen.
- **One consequential side effect, taken deliberately.** `machine_notices` excluded the Critic because the `fleet`-binary warning "would be false for it" (D-076's own words). It is true of it now, so the exclusion is gone and a Critic on a machine with no `fleet` binary warns like any other pane. `orch` stays excluded, because `place_orch` emits that line itself.
- **No verb moved.** `VERBS`, the clap enum, `Op`, `prompts/orch.md` and `prompts/worker.md` are byte-unchanged — `git diff --exit-code` on all of them, plus `prompts/critic.md`, exits 0. No brief teaches the Critic to anyone: a pane meets the name when a message arrives and answers with `fleet reply`, which it already knows (WP-21 guardrail 4).
- **What would reverse it.** A measurement that the interview's answers do not earn their turns — the arc doc sets the bar as converting fewer than three of the first report's six UNCITED items. Or evidence that a pane, having been messaged by `critic`, starts writing for it: that is the failure the veil prevents for the evaluator and cannot prevent here, because the Critic is openly named.
- **Verified.** 226 workspace tests green (unchanged — nothing was added to the workspace crates); `src-tauri` 222 with `--features devmode`, up from 219, and 217 without, up from 214 — three added in `tests/panes.rs`, one renamed in `tests/placement.rs`, none deleted. The three new ones are real processes on real ptys through `placement::place`: a closed interview refuses and moves neither the log nor orch's terminal; an open one is `accepted`, arrives framed `[fleet · critic]`, and closes again with no respawn; and orch's `fleet send critic` reaches the Critic's pty and is logged with it as the recipient. **The refusal was mutation-checked**: adding a single `Notice` append beside the early return makes the log test fail with the two rows against one, so the assertion is load-bearing rather than decorative. The four veil tripwires pass and still match the evaluator only; `hub.rs` contains no needle of either tripwire. `cargo clippy --all-targets --workspace` shows the same three pre-existing `fleetor-core` warnings and `--features devmode` from `src-tauri` the same two — no new warning. `cargo build --features devmode` and `npm run build` clean.
- **Not verified.** **No fleet has been started since this landed** — the same gap D-062 through D-078 each record. So: no live `claude` has met the refusal sentence, and nothing measures whether a model reading it opens a `fleet roster` and argues rather than waiting. **The interview has never been run.** Whether asking a pane about its own conduct produces anything the archive did not already contain is the arc's central bet and is entirely unmeasured here; the three-of-six bar is a criterion, not a result. The `Notice` on each edge is asserted by no test — I wrote the two commands and read the `note` helper they call, but nothing pins that an open and a close leave two distinguishable rows, and I did not verify how the Activity feed renders them. **The dev-mode leak the arc doc sharpens is untouched and is now reachable**: a Critic that has read a dev-mode run's `events.json` can, with the interview open, ask `orch` about what it found there. WP-21's open question 2 recommends refusing to open an interview on a dev-mode run; that refusal is *not* built, and this entry is where a future session should find that out. `rustfmt` is not installed on this machine, so formatting was not checked. No live spend.
## D-080 — the Critic's brief learns the interview: a remit line, a third citation form, and testimony that can corroborate but never anchor

- **What changed.** `prompts/critic.md` gained three passages and one output bullet, and lost nothing: a paragraph drawing the in-remit / out-of-remit line inside `## Your remit`; a new `## The interview` section between the citation rules and the two traps; and an `INTERVIEW` bullet between `FINDINGS` and `UNCITED`. 119 lines / 7,222 bytes → 157 lines / 9,957 bytes. **No existing sentence was edited.** The six categories, the two traps, the archive citation rules, the anti-prescription paragraph and the `{archive}` placeholder are byte-identical. `prompts/orch.md`, `prompts/worker.md` and `crates/fleetor-core/src/brief.rs` (where `VERBS` lives) are byte-unchanged — `git diff --exit-code` on all three is empty — so `brief-cost` for this ticket is **0**, which is what WP-21 guardrail 4 asks for.
- **"Find mistakes and lapses of judgement" is not a reversal of the ban on judging, and the brief now says which side of the line a thing is on rather than leaving it to be inferred.** The arc doc calls this its most important sentence. A decision about **the run** — routing, decomposition, verification, what a pane concluded and on what basis — is in remit; a judgement about **the code** — whether it works, whether the design is right, whether the tests suffice — is out. Both worked examples in the brief are real findings from the Critic's first live run, and they are in the brief rather than only in the arc doc because the abstract rule is the half a model can satisfy while breaching: *"orch reported the run verified by receipts; no receipt existed"* is in, *"the retry logic should have been extracted"* is out. The paragraph is placed **between** the answer-key paragraph and the anti-prescription paragraph, touching neither, because the spike measured that removing the second produces "solid work, maybe more tests" — the exact failure this pane exists to avoid — and prescription is stage C's business.
- **Testimony is not record, and that is a rule about what the citation rule already forbids.** The brief's standing rule is *"you can cite what a pane wrote, not why it wrote it."* An interview answer is technically written by the pane, but afterwards, to someone assessing it, about its own conduct — so admitting it as archive evidence would quietly delete the rule while appearing to extend it. Three clauses hold the line: a third citation form `interview <pane> <ts>`, deliberately not in the shape of `events.json seq N` or a transcript line, so a reader can see at a glance which claims rest on the record; a finding may be **corroborated** by testimony and is **never established** by it, with the archive anchor kept on every finding; and **an answer that contradicts the archive is itself a finding** — of the archive-anchored kind, because the contradiction is visible in the record. That last clause is the one doing the work: it is where a lapse of judgement actually surfaces, and without it the interview can only confirm what the archive already showed. The brief tells the Critic to report the gap without deciding which side is true, which is the same refusal to grade the work stated one level up.
- **UNCITED survives the interview, and it records the answer.** A question asked and not settled goes there **with the answer recorded**, rather than the question alone or the answer alone. The arc's bar for stage A is a conversion rate out of that section — the first live report left six items there, three of them answerable by asking — so an UNCITED entry that drops what asking produced destroys the only measurement the stage has.
- **The brief teaches `fleet reply "<text>"` with no recipient, and that is D-077 showing up in prose rather than a style choice.** `fleet reply worker-3 "…"` is refused at parse; a brief that taught the recipient shape would teach a shape the CLI rejects, in a pane that reads its own stderr. The closed interview is described as failing at resolution with "no such pane" and named explicitly as *not* a finding — a Critic that filed the operator's own switch as a defect in the run would be reporting on the interface rather than on the fleet.
- **The veil was not weakened.** `prompts/critic.md` still contains none of the five needles `the_critics_brief_is_openly_named_and_names_no_evaluator_vocabulary` greps for, and the `answer key` exemption at line 14 is intact and still pinned by exact phrase. The word "judge" is used where "grade" would have read more naturally, because `grader` is one of the six needles and a sentence one letter away from tripping a tripwire is a sentence that will trip it during the next edit. No test, needle or exemption was touched.
- **What would reverse it.** A measured interview in which the corroboration rule produces a report an operator finds *less* useful than one without testimony — the arc's own bar is that an interview stage converting fewer than three of six UNCITED items is not earning its complexity, and nothing here measures that. Or stage C arriving, at which point the remit paragraph's "only the evidence available inside it" widens and this entry is amended rather than kept.
- **Verified.** **223** shell tests green under `cargo test --features devmode`, up from **219** at `99fa30f`; **218** under plain `cargo test`, up from **214**; **226** from the repo root under `cargo test --workspace`, unchanged, since the shell crate is not in that workspace. Four new tests in `critic::tests`, pinning the new rules by exact phrase the way `the_shipped_brief_keeps_the_citation_rule_and_the_ban_on_prescription` pins the old ones. All seven dev-mode tests green, the four veil tripwires included. **No test was deleted, weakened or marked ignored.** One assertion was mutation-checked rather than only written: replacing *"A finding may be corroborated by testimony and is never established by it"* with *"Testimony counts as evidence"* fails `the_brief_admits_testimony_as_corroboration_and_never_as_the_anchor` and nothing else, which is the evidence that the test pins the rule rather than the section.
- **Not verified.** **No Critic has ever conducted an interview, and none has ever run at all** — the same honest gap D-076 records and this entry does not close. Nothing here measures whether a model handed this brief actually keeps testimony out of its anchors under the pressure of a pane that answers confidently; the spike that shaped every other clause in this file measured *archived* runs read with no socket in hand, and its control run is evidence about prescription and citations, not about interviews. The three-of-six conversion bar is stated in the arc doc and is untested. The socket and the operator's control are a sibling's work and were not exercised from here, so the sentence *"a send fails with 'no such pane'"* describes a gate this session did not observe — I did not verify it. Whether the brief's added 2,735 bytes cost the Critic anything is unmeasured; the Critic's brief is outside the orch/worker budget (D-053), which is an argument that it *may* grow, not evidence that this growth was free. `rustfmt` is not installed on this machine, so formatting was not checked. No live spend.

## D-081 — the vendor-binary tier is wired into the conformance suite, runs by default, and shouts when it skips

- **What changed.** `src-tauri/tests/vendor_binary_tier.rs` — a new, separate test binary that runs `examples/codex-spike/probe.py` and gates on its exit code (the count of failed arms), plus two tripwires: the probe's vendor build id must match `docs/notes/codex-spike-notes.md`, and every URL in the probe must be loopback. The probe was **not** rewritten and no production code was touched. It is a third tier between the two issue #12 named, exactly as C13 specifies.
- **It runs by default rather than behind an opt-in env var, and that is the default that moved.** The obvious alternative — `FLEETOR_VENDOR_TIER=1 cargo test` — costs a machine with the vendor binary nothing, which is precisely what is wrong with it: an opt-in flag is a quiet skip with extra steps, and C13's whole argument is that this tier rots into decoration the moment its absence stops being visible. Cost, stated: **~47 s** added to `cargo test` wherever `codex` is on `PATH` (two non-interactive turns, three sandboxed commands, two real pty sessions), all bounded by the probe's own per-subprocess timeouts.
- **Loud is harder than it sounds, and the mechanism was verified rather than assumed.** `libtest` captures `println!` *and* `eprintln!` on a **passing** test, which is the state every skip is in — so the obvious announcement is invisible in a plain `cargo test`. The skip banner is written to file descriptor 2 directly, which the capture does not intercept; measured on a scratch test binary before the file was written, and then demonstrated end to end with `codex` off `PATH`.
- **Three things are deliberately not skips.** A missing probe (a *deleted tier*, not an absent vendor), a probe that ran and reported no arms (exit 0 with nothing measured — the silent pass this tier exists to make impossible), and a probe that printed its own `SKIP:` while the test resolved the binary (the two disagree about the machine). All three fail.
- **No harness was registered and nothing in `src/` learned the vendor's name.** Phase 1's exit condition is a green suite with exactly one registered harness; the binary is named in `tests/` as the subject of a measurement, the way `tests/write_guardrail.rs` names `sh`. No verb moved; the two existing tiers are byte-unchanged.
- **What would reverse it.** A CI machine where the ~47 s or the vendor binary's own side effects (it writes `/tmp/codex-spike` and briefly binds `127.0.0.1:8731`) make a default-on tier untenable — in which case the answer is a *default-on-with-a-documented-off-switch*, not a default-off flag, because the announcement is the load-bearing part.
- **Verified.** Both paths, on this machine. Running path: `codex-cli 0.153.4` on `PATH`, 8/8 arms PASS in 45 s, zero tokens (the model provider is the probe's own loopback capture server). Skip path: `PATH=~/.cargo/bin:/usr/bin:/bin cargo test --test vendor_binary_tier` — banner printed with no `--nocapture`, no failure, no silent pass. `src-tauri`: 166 lib + 75 integration across 10 binaries, up from 72 across 9. Root workspace: **226**, unchanged — the Tier 1.4 evidence. `cargo clippy --all-targets` adds no warning to the two pre-existing ones.
- **Not verified.** No machine *without* the vendor binary has run the suite for real — the skip path was demonstrated by removing `codex` from `PATH`, not by a machine that never had it. And nothing here measures whether an operator who sees the banner acts on it, which is the assumption C13's "or it rots into decoration" actually rests on.

## WP-22 — multi-TUI (Claude Code + Cursor), interview in progress

- **M1. The operator picks harness *and* model, per pane.** The selection unit is a pair — `claude` or `cursor`, then a model from that harness's own list — not a harness alone and not a named profile. Profiles are where this ends up if the pairs turn out to repeat; nothing measures that yet.
- **M2. The orchestrator gets a model picker too, and D-052's asymmetry is amended rather than quietly kept.** "The operator's own `claude`" stops being an invariant the moment that seat can be a different vendor; a Claude Code orch keeps a `default (your login)` sentinel so today's behaviour stays reachable.
- **M3. The last choice is remembered per harness, not per pane-seat alone.** Switching a pane from cursor back to claude restores the claude model it had, rather than resetting to a default.
- **M4. Every harness is told the same words.** One `orch.md`, one `worker.md` — `prompts/` stays the single source (D-042). What varies is the *delivery mechanism* for those words, which lives behind a harness seam.
- **M5. A cursor pane is briefed by a rules file in its own cwd, and this is measured, not assumed.** `.cursor/rules/fleetor.mdc` with `alwaysApply: true`, rendered from the same `orch.md`/`worker.md`. Verified in a scratch workspace: the pane answered with the rule's sentinel and printed nothing on screen, which is the `--system-prompt` property M4 needs. **`--add-dir` was measured and rejected** — the identical rule in an added root did not reach the model, so the brief cannot live under `~/.fleetor` and the file lands in the pane's worktree with a `.git/info/exclude` line to keep `git status` honest. Not verified: that the rule survives `/clear`.
- **M6. Typing is a per-harness profile, never assumed parity.** Paste mode, submit key, post-paste delay and newline policy are declared per harness and tuned against a real pty. The failure this refuses is a green `accepted` for a message left sitting unsubmitted in an input box — the one lie D-034 exists to prevent.
- **M7. The command channel stays harness-wide.** Cursor has `/clear`, so `fleet cmd self /clear` means the same thing in both. `/compact` parity is open.
- **M8. Cursor panes run on the operator's logged-in plan. No API key, ever.** Measured: cursor's auth follows `HOME`, so a fenced worker with a private `HOME` reports "Not logged in" — `seed_worker_home` therefore plants the operator's cursor credential too. Stated plainly rather than buried: **a cursor worker holds a credential a Claude Code worker is explicitly denied** (D-062 unsets the securestorage dir precisely so it cannot), it can spend the operator's Cursor plan without limit, and no per-pane budget exists anywhere in this codebase. The filesystem half of the Fence — `~/.ssh`, shell profiles, the operator's real config unreachable by name — is kept intact and is what WP-08 actually measured.
- **M9. A cursor pane inherits the operator's own cursor-agent preferences.** Each fenced pane's `~/.cursor` is a **layered snapshot** taken at bootstrap and never written back: the preference-bearing files are copied (`cli-config.json`, `skills-cursor/`, `plugins/`, `mcp.json`, user rules), the session and telemetry ones are not (`chats/`, `projects/`, `ai-tracking/`, `statsig-cache.json`, `extensions/`, `ide_state.json`). Snapshot rather than symlink because cursor *writes* to `cli-config.json` itself — a symlink is four workers mutating the operator's preferences mid-run. The orch pane has no private HOME and reads the operator's real `~/.cursor` untouched.
- **M10. Fleetor's needs are layered on top additively, and `deny` always wins.** The operator's `permissions.deny` entries are preserved and still refuse; fleetor unions in the allow coverage a worker needs so it cannot wedge on an approval prompt at its first `cargo test` — the failure that looks exactly like a healthy pane. The model comes from the picker (M1). Every key fleetor overlays gets an Activity feed line at spawn, the way `prompts.rs` announces a loaded override, so "my setting didn't apply" is never a silent mystery.
- **M11. A cursor worker runs `--sandbox enabled`, and never `--force`. Measured, and it corrected a wrong answer given minutes earlier in the same interview.** `-f`/`--yolo` *disables* the sandbox: the identical probe wrote to `$HOME` with exit 0 under `--force` and was refused `operation not permitted` without it. `--sandbox enabled` **is** cursor's auto mode — commands auto-run inside the sandbox with no approval prompt, and approval is sought only to escape it, which is a better shape than `--permission-mode auto` because the boundary is the filesystem rather than a model's judgement.
- **M12. The sandbox covers the agent's own file-writing tool, not only shell — so the missing `beforeFileEdit` hook does not matter.** A non-shell write to `$HOME` came back `Rejected`. A cursor worker is therefore genuinely fenced rather than deterred, which is the parity Q6 was asking about and did not expect to get.
- **M13. Network is refused by default inside the sandbox, and that is the wedge to design against.** `curl https://example.com` was rejected without executing. Under `-p` a rejection auto-denies; in a live TUI it prompts a human, and a worker has none — so `cargo fetch`, `npm install` and `git push` each park a pane that looks perfectly healthy.
- **M14. `networkAccess: allow_all` inside the sandbox.** A Claude Code worker has unrestricted network today; anything narrower makes cursor workers strictly more fragile than their peers with no measured threat behind it. Network exfiltration was never in scope for either harness, and a curated allowlist would be new policy smuggled in as a compatibility detail — with M13's silent wedge as its failure mode.
- **M15. The pickers live on the start gate, beside the target.** One row for the orchestrator and one for the workers, each carrying harness + model, with a disclosure that expands the workers row into four for a mixed fleet — so the common case costs one selection, not four. The gate keeps its single-screen cost statement, and its summary line reads from the same state the dropdowns write, so it cannot promise a fleet that is not what will spawn.
- **M16. Changing a model mid-run is the harness's own `/model`, not a fleetor control.** Fleetor picks the model a pane *starts* with and has no opinion after that, which matches "don't worry about switching harnesses in the middle" and keeps the fleet's own surface at nine verbs (D-034's spirit). Two consequences: fleetor's remembered choice (M3) goes stale the moment someone uses `/model`, and it is remembering *what it last launched*, not what a pane is running; and because a cursor pane's `/model` writes into its snapshot config (M9), the change cannot leak back into the operator's real preferences.
- **M17. Neither harness may start unlogged-in, and the gate says so.** The start gate checks login for cursor (`cursor-agent about` — login, email, Subscription Tier, default model in one call) and for Claude Code (`~/.claude.json`'s `oauthAccount` — email, `organizationType`, rate-limit tier), shows the plan as a fact beside the model, and offers a **re-check** button so logging in from another terminal does not need an app restart. A harness that is not logged in appears **disabled with the reason inline**, never hidden and never spawned — the same principle as today's "no API key found, the orchestrator runs alone". Caveat recorded: `~/.claude.json` is Claude Code's internal state, not a supported API, and its presence proves a login *happened*, not that the token still works. The plan tier is **displayed, never a gate** — `cursor-agent models` already returns what the account can actually run.
- **M18. Fleetor owns `sandbox.mode` on every cursor pane, the orchestrator included — this corrects M10.** The sandbox key lives in the operator's own `cli-config.json`, so honouring it wholesale means an operator who enabled the sandbox months ago for unrelated reasons gets a fleet whose orchestrator cannot talk. Preferences are inherited; this one key is not.
- **M19. A sandboxed pane cannot dial the fleet socket, and moving the socket does not help.** Measured twice: `connect()` refused with `PermissionError: Operation not permitted` both at `~/.fleetor/_shell/fleet.sock` and at a socket **inside the pane's own worktree**, because seatbelt blocks outbound unix sockets as a class. `CURSOR_FORCED_SHELL_EGRESS_WRITABLE_PATHS` did not lift it. **Only the outbound leg is affected** — inbound delivery is a bracketed paste into the pty and never touches the socket, so a sandboxed pane can be talked to; without a bridge it simply cannot talk back.
- **M20. The bridge is a `beforeShellExecution` hook, and it is measured, not hoped for.** Hook processes run **outside** the seatbelt: a project `.cursor/hooks.json` hook connected to the socket the pane itself had just been refused, and the listener received its bytes, while the pane's own `echo` ran normally inside the jail. The hook intercepts `fleet …`, performs it out-of-band, and returns the result to the pane via the response's `agent_message` — so `accepted` keeps its exact meaning (D-034) and there is still **one transport, one CLI**. The rejected alternatives: a second file-drop transport through the `fleetor-ipc` seam (a week, and it complicates the one path this codebase refuses to complicate), and unsandboxed cursor panes (gives up M11/M12's measured fence).
- **M21. The bridge ships as its own testable phase.** It is the one piece whose failure silently removes eight of the nine verbs from a pane that otherwise looks healthy, so it gets its own phase with its own verification rather than riding along inside the harness seam.
- **M22.
