The context gauge learns to read cursor's transcripts rather than going blank.** A second reader behind `TranscriptSource`, so the gauge means the same thing on every pane and D-045's compaction loop keeps working for cursor panes. Gated on a spike into cursor's `chats/` format (a `better_sqlite3.node` ships in the install, so plausibly SQLite rather than JSONL); if that format proves undocumented or unstable the fallback is an **explicit "unavailable"** in the rail, never an estimate synthesized from what we sent — a number that looks right and is wrong is worse than an absent one, which is the same rule D-054 already encodes.
- **M23. The seam is a `Harness` trait with a flat `HarnessSpec` for the data half, living inside `placement`, and it is enforced by a conformance suite rather than by the compiler alone.** 61 of the 79 Claude-Code-specific references already sit in `placement/spawn.rs` and 18 more in `guardrail.rs`, so this is where the knowledge is; a separate crate would invert the dependency direction and drag `guardrail`, `deliver`, `orphans` and `context_gauge` into it. The trait gives compile-time completeness across the fourteen checkpoints, and the suite — one pass per registered harness, each checkpoint asserted end to end — is what stops a method being implemented as a stub that compiles and lies (`building.md` §6). "Add a harness" becomes "make fourteen tests pass."
- **The fourteen checkpoints:** program + base args; brief carrier; model flag and permission/sandbox posture; config dir and its seeding; credential wiring and env scrub; private HOME seeding; write-guardrail install; outbound bridge; typing profile; command-channel spellings; context-gauge source and window constant; orphan-sweep process names; project-key canonicalization; login/plan probe and model list. Two are live bugs the moment a cursor pane exists: `orphans.rs:51` sweeps on the literal `"claude"` and would leak every crashed cursor pane as a terminal-less orphan, and `spawn::project_key` is *Claude Code's own* canonicalization shared by the trust flag and the gauge, which cursor keys differently — so it can no longer be one function.
- **M24. Transcripts are archived raw, per harness, with the format declared in the manifest.** `manifest.json` gains `harness`, `model` and `transcript_format` per pane, and the spawn event carries the same pair so the live feed is not poorer than the archive. Nothing is converted: a normalizer sitting between two vendors' undocumented formats and the permanent record is wrong forever once it drifts, and the raw evidence would already be gone. Two consequences to fix rather than inherit: `harvest_transcripts` must learn each harness's transcript location (a cursor pane contributes **zero** today, and `runs.rs:64` documents `0` as ordinary — a real gap wearing a normal run's clothes), and the manifest's agent-facing sentence *"that pane's Claude Code session .jsonl files"* becomes a false statement to any Critic reading a mixed run cold.
- **OPEN — does the fleet's own record disclose a peer's harness?** `fleet roster` is generated rather than brief text, so naming each pane's harness there would not violate M4. Recommendation, not yet settled: **keep the roster harness-free and show it only in the operator's rail** — a worker that knows worker-3 runs a different model starts reasoning about which peer is "weaker", which is a behaviour nothing in this codebase wants to introduce by accident. The operator needs the fact; the panes do not.
- **M25. The roster is harness-free: workers see each other as equals.** `fleet roster` names panes, never the harness or model behind them. The operator's rail shows it; the panes are not told. A worker that knows which peer is "weaker" starts routing work on that belief, and nothing in this design wants that behaviour introduced as a side effect of a compatibility feature.
- **M26. Cursor accepts fleetor's existing delivery path unchanged — measured on a real pty.** A bracketed paste followed by CR into a live `cursor-agent`, with fleetor's own `TERM`/`COLORTERM` env, submitted the turn and produced a reply carrying a token that appeared nowhere in the pasted bytes. M6's per-harness typing profile stays as the *seam* (a future harness will need it) but cursor's profile is identical to Claude Code's, so no tuning is owed.
- **M27. The CLI's identity and authorization holes are recorded and deliberately deferred.** `FLEETOR_PANE` is self-asserted (`main.rs:523`) and `hub.rs:202` trusts `hello.pane`, so any pane can speak as any other; `fleet handoff`'s orch-only rule is enforced in the client (`main.rs:314`) while the hub writes whatever `from` it is handed, so a worker can forge the orchestrator's "the goal is met" into the permanent record — the one record nothing else verifies. `fleet cmd`'s allowlist *is* hub-side (`hub.rs:403`), which is what makes the other two an oversight rather than a policy. **The operator's call: not blocking multi-TUI.** Both are survivable under the current "every pane is our own agent" trust model and neither is reachable by accident. They become urgent only if the M20 bridge ships, because a hook re-executing `fleet …` out-of-band would also re-execute `fleet done`'s `sh -c` (`done.rs:109`) outside the jail.
- **M28. `networkAccess: allow_all` lifts the socket refusal, so there is no bridge — measured.** Under `--sandbox enabled` with that one key, a sandboxed pane connected to a unix socket and the listener received its bytes (`CONNECTED`, exit 0), where the identical probe under the default policy was refused. The seatbelt profile has exactly two forms of the rule — `(allow network-outbound (remote ip "localhost:*"))` and the unrestricted `(allow network-outbound)` — and this key selects between them; a unix-socket connect is a `network-outbound` operation with a path filter, which is why the restricted form explained the refusal precisely. **Three things follow.** The `beforeShellExecution` bridge (M20) is not built: a fenced cursor pane runs the real `fleet` CLI directly, one transport, one CLI, nothing new on the message path. The receipt-verb split is unnecessary — `fleet done`'s `sh -c` (`done.rs:109`) runs *inside* the sandbox, which is the containment it should always have had, so M27's deferral of the CLI's identity and authorization holes is now unconditional rather than "unless the bridge ships". And `sandbox.networkAccess` joins `sandbox.mode` as a key **fleetor owns rather than inherits** (M18): an operator who set `user_config_only` for their own use would otherwise get a fleet whose panes cannot talk. The cost is stated rather than hidden — a network-restricted cursor worker and a talking cursor worker are mutually exclusive under this policy, and M14 already chose unrestricted network on parity grounds before it was load-bearing.

## WP-25 — codex TUI (third harness), interview in progress

- **C1. Codex is its own work package, its investigation runs *before* WP-24's seam is written, and its findings amend the fourteen checkpoints rather than being fitted to them.** Parallel structure to WP-24 — own issue, own roadmap doc (`25-codex-tui.md`), own decision series — but not parallel *timing*: WP-24 is `not-started`, so the `Harness` trait and its conformance suite are still a hypothesis derived from exactly one non-Claude vendor. `CODEX_HOME` already shows checkpoint 6 ("private HOME seeding") is mis-shaped — codex relocates its whole config dir without touching `HOME`, so the credential-follows-`HOME` fact that forced M8/M9 is Cursor's, not a harness universal. Discovering that now is a design edit; discovering it after phase 1 lands is a refactor of tested code. Rejected: folding codex into issue #12 (enlarges an already-L arc so nothing ships until three harnesses do), and sequencing codex strictly after WP-24 (honest, but bends a landed seam).
- **C2. A codex seat is still `harness + model`; the model *provider* is inherited and displayed, never picked.** M1 survives unamended. Codex can point at a ChatGPT plan, an OpenAI API key or any third-party endpoint through `[model_providers.*]`, and the operator's is currently a DeepSeek base URL — but `models.json` carries no provider field, so the catalog is already provider-implicit and there is no provider-scoped list to populate a picker from without inventing one. The provider appears on the gate as a fact beside the model, the way M17 shows plan tier. Cost stated rather than hidden: story 6's "compare vendors side by side" cannot mean `codex@deepseek` beside `codex@openai` in one fleet. Rejected: a per-seat provider control (amends M1, adds a third gate control for one harness, and per-seat providers mean per-seat credentials, multiplying checkpoint 5), and pinning codex to OpenAI (refuses the operator's own working setup).
- **C3. The codex brief is `model_instructions_file`, and it *replaces* the system prompt — measured against the wire, not inferred.** A fake `[model_providers.probe]` pointed at a local capture server rendered the exact request body codex sends. With the key set, the request's `instructions` field went from the built-in 17,730-character "You are Codex, an agent based on GPT-5…" to the 55-character sentinel file; "You are Codex" was absent. That is D-043's property exactly — *replace*, not append — and it is a plain config key, so the brief file can live in the pane's config dir rather than its worktree. **M5's two costs do not transfer:** no `.mdc` inside the checkout, no `.git/info/exclude` line, and story 26 is answered by construction. Two carriers were measured and rejected: `base_instructions` in a custom `model_catalog_json` is **not honoured** (the built-in prompt was sent anyway — the field is a descriptor, not an override), and `AGENTS.md` in the pane's cwd reaches the model as a **`user` message**, in-band, spending the pane's own context and reading as though the operator typed it — the failure story 25 names. `experimental_instructions_file` does not exist in `0.153.4`.
- **C4. Codex's write guardrail ports directly: it has a real `pre_tool_use` hook.** The hook event set is `pre_tool_use`, `permission_request`, `post_tool_use`, `pre_compact`, `post_compact`, `session_start`, `session_end`, `user_prompt_submit`, `subagent_start`, `subagent_stop`, `interrupt`. Checkpoint 7 is therefore a port of D-065 rather than the "the sandbox stands in for the missing pre-edit event" argument M12 had to make for Cursor. Unverified: hook *trust* — `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust` implies a persisted trust record a fleet-spawned pane will not have, which is a spike, not a design question.
- **C5. `sandbox_workspace_write.network_access = true` is codex's only socket lever, and the narrow alternative was measured and does not work.** Under the default `workspace-write` sandbox a unix-socket connect is refused (`PermissionError [Errno 1] Operation not permitted`); with `network_access = true` the identical probe printed `CONNECTED` and the listener received its bytes. `network.dangerously_allow_all_unix_sockets` and `network.unix_sockets` — which read like a narrow allowance and would have been strictly better than M14's all-or-nothing — left the refusal in place under three variations including `--enable network_proxy`, because they configure the network-*proxy* layer, not the seatbelt profile. So codex inherits M28's shape unchanged: no bridge, one transport, one CLI, and a network-restricted codex worker and a talking codex worker are mutually exclusive. The fence itself is real: `echo x > $HOME/probe` under the sandbox was refused and no file was created.
- **C6. A codex pane gets a private `CODEX_HOME`, seeded as a layered snapshot — and checkpoints 4 and 6 come apart.** Measured: `CODEX_HOME` alone determines codex's config *and* its login. A fake `HOME` with the real `CODEX_HOME` reported `Logged in using an API key`; an empty `CODEX_HOME` reported `Not logged in` regardless of `HOME`. So M8's central fact — "cursor's auth follows `HOME`, therefore the Fence logs a pane out" — is **Cursor's, not a harness universal**, and codex needs no credential planted in a private home. Each pane's `CODEX_HOME` is a bootstrap snapshot of the operator's `~/.codex` (auth, preference-bearing config, skills, plugins, MCP) that is never written back, which satisfies story 18 by mechanism rather than promise. The decisive secondary reason: codex writes its thread history into `CODEX_HOME`, so per-pane directories make checkpoint 12's harvest a per-directory read with pane attribution for free — where a shared `CODEX_HOME` would make it a query against one SQLite file. The Fence's private `HOME` is kept, but for its real job (`~/.ssh`, shell profiles) rather than as the load-bearing auth mechanism it is for cursor. **One measured trap the seeder must handle:** a `~` inside a copied config value (the operator's own `model_catalog_json = "~/.codex/models.json"`) resolves against the pane's private `HOME` and the pane dies at spawn with `Error loading configuration: No such file or directory` — loud, but only if the seeder rewrites or the suite asserts it. Rejected: a shared `CODEX_HOME` at the operator's real `~/.codex` with per-pane `-c` overlays (four panes mutating the operator's own config through `/model`, exactly what M9 refused, and transcript attribution becomes a query), and a clean `CODEX_HOME` with no inheritance (silently drops the operator's MCP servers, skills and model catalog — and with them the provider C2 says is inherited).
- **C7. A codex worker's containment is the legacy trio, measured on `0.153.4`: `sandbox_mode = "workspace-write"`, `approval_policy = "never"`, `sandbox_workspace_write.network_access = true`.** `never` rather than `on-request` for M13's reason: `on-request` lets the model decide when to ask, and a worker has no human to ask, so it parks looking healthy. Measured together: a write to `$HOME` was refused with no file created, and the fleet socket was reachable. Rejected for now: the newer `[permissions.<name>]` + `default_permissions` model, which is where codex is visibly heading (`~/.codex/.sandbox_migration` exists) but which carries an explicit *"derived permission profile cannot be represented as a legacy sandbox policy; falling back to read-only"* path in the binary — a worker that spawns clean and silently cannot write, the failure class M10 and M13 exist to prevent.
- **C8. The gate verifies the posture codex actually resolved, not the keys FLEETOR wrote — and it costs one call it was already making.** `codex doctor --json` returns in **1.36 s** with `schemaVersion`, `codexVersion` and a `checks` array carrying filesystem sandbox, network sandbox and approval policy. M17 already puts a login/plan probe on `Host`, and `Host::discover` (`placement/mod.rs:325`) already shells out per spawn for rustup, the fleet binary and the worker key, so this is a field read on a planned call rather than new machinery. It lives on the **gate**, not the spawn path, because `doctor` makes a live provider reachability request. This is what makes C7's legacy-keys risk survivable: a codex release that retires them fails the gate loudly instead of producing a mute fleet. Story 45's "verify the sandbox's network policy as part of startup" is satisfied for codex by the same call.
- **C9. A codex worker holds the fleet's credential, never the operator's — and C2 narrows to the orchestrator seat.** FLEETOR's Claude Code workers already do this: `spawn.rs:331` sets `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` to `https://api.deepseek.com/anthropic` and `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` to `.env`'s `DEEPSEEK_API_KEY`, and `spawn.rs:344` *unsets* `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` so the operator's shell key cannot leak in. The codex mirror is a FLEETOR-written `[model_providers.fleetor]` in the pane's seeded `CODEX_HOME` — same key, `base_url = "https://api.deepseek.com/"`, `wire_api = "responses"` — and the operator's `auth.json` and provider bearer tokens are **not** copied. **C2 is amended**, one turn after being settled: the provider is inherited and displayed on the **orchestrator** seat, where the operator's own login is the entire point (D-030/D-052); on a worker seat FLEETOR owns it. This dissolves M8's exception rather than importing it — a codex worker holds strictly less than a cursor worker and exactly what a Claude Code worker holds, so D-062's stance survives a third harness intact. Noted as fact, not inference: the operator's codex key and `.env`'s worker key are two different keys to the same vendor. Cost: a codex worker cannot spend an operator's ChatGPT plan even when that is what they wanted; the escalation path is the same one M1 named for profiles.
- **C10. The command channel needs no codex-specific spelling, and M7's open question closes.** `codex` carries `/clear`, `/compact`, `/model` and `/review`, so `fleet cmd self /clear` means the same thing on all three harnesses and `/compact` — which M7 left open for cursor — exists here. Checkpoint 10 is a table with identical rows for codex.
- **C11. The orphan sweep is a one-word change for codex, and the danger M23 named does not arise.** `orphans.rs` does not search by name: `sweep_at_named` reads pids **FLEETOR itself recorded** and calls `reap_if_named` to confirm the comm suffix before signalling (`orphans.rs:51`, `:77`). A running codex is a native binary whose comm ends in `codex`, not a Node tree, so checkpoint 13 for codex is `"codex"` beside `"claude"` — and the operator's own codex is never at risk because its pid was never in the registry. The "must catch a Node tree without ever matching a bare interpreter" problem is Cursor's alone.
- **C12. Codex's transcript is a versioned SQLite thread store, and M22's spike is already done for this harness.** A codex pane writes `thread_history_1.sqlite` inside its `CODEX_HOME` with `thread_items` (`thread_id`, `turn_id`, `item_id`, `rollout_ordinal`, `created_at_ms`, `item_type`, `item_json`) and `thread_turns` (turn boundaries, status, timings) — an `_sqlx_migrations`-managed schema in a **generation-numbered filename** (`state_5`, `logs_2`, `thread_history_1`), which is codex's own compatibility signal and the thing the manifest's `transcript_format` should record. Verified by running a pane against a capture server: the rows appear, `item_type = 'userMessage'`, `item_json` is the item verbatim. Two consequences. **M24's "archive raw" needs a mechanism note:** a WAL-mode SQLite file cannot be `cp`'d live without risking a torn copy, so the harvest uses `VACUUM INTO` or the backup API, not a file copy. **Unverified, and it is the real open spike:** whether per-turn token usage is persisted in the store at all — my probe never completed an assistant turn, so `TokenUsageInfo`/`TokenCountEvent` were never written. If it is not persisted, M22's stated fallback applies unchanged: the rail says **unavailable**, never a synthesized number. Note that codex publishes `context_window` per model in its catalog (`1048576` for the operator's), so checkpoint 11's window constant is a fact codex states rather than one FLEETOR asserts as D-054 has to.
- **C13. Codex earns a third test tier: vendor-binary behaviour tests that spend no tokens.** Issue #12's rule — "no test spends tokens, and no test requires either vendor's binary" — was written when the only alternatives were an artifact assertion or a token-spending prototype. Codex ships three instruments that are neither: `codex debug prompt-input` renders the exact model-visible input list, `codex sandbox <cmd>` runs the real seatbelt with no model, and a fake `[model_providers.*]` pointed at a local capture server yields the literal request body. Every measured claim in C3, C5, C6 and C7 came from those, in about a minute, for nothing. The tier sits between the two existing ones and **skips cleanly when the binary is absent**, so issue #12's two tiers survive verbatim. It exists because C3's carrier and C5's socket lever are precisely the checkpoints that fail by looking healthy, and an artifact assertion proves only that FLEETOR wrote a key — never that codex honoured it. Cost, stated: a machine without codex runs a strictly weaker suite, so the tier must announce its skips loudly or it rots into decoration (`building.md` §6).
- **C14. Any of codex's three auth shapes counts as logged in, and the shape is displayed.** `codex doctor --json` answers checkpoint 14 in one 1.36 s call: auth mode, provider, model and active-provider reachability. The gate shows "ChatGPT <plan>", "OpenAI API key" or "<name> (custom provider)" beside the model the way M17 shows cursor's Subscription Tier, and refuses to start only when codex resolves to no usable credential. C9 is what makes this proportionate: the orchestrator is the only codex seat that spends the operator's credential at all. **Caveat recorded, as M17 recorded its own:** doctor reports *reachability*, not authorization — its probe returned HTTP 401 against the operator's provider and still passed — so a revoked key clears the gate and fails on the first turn. Rejected: a live authenticated probe at gate time (real, but on a ChatGPT plan it spends quota before the operator has agreed to spend anything, which is what the gate's single-screen cost statement exists to prevent), and refusing plan-based login (story 9's "a supported feature looks unimplemented", at the gate).
- **C15. Codex is offered on the orchestrator and worker seats, not on the judges.** Issue #12's reasoning transfers unchanged: both the evaluator and the Critic judge the fleet's work, and a judge running the same harness as the judged is a variable worth not introducing in a first mixed run. Consequence of C2-as-amended and C9 together: a codex **orchestrator** runs on the operator's own login and inherited provider; a codex **worker** runs on FLEETOR's provider. That is the same asymmetry D-030/D-052 already established for Claude Code, now stated once and applying to three harnesses.
- **C16. Workspace trust is not a missing checkpoint — it already lives in checkpoints 4 and 14, and codex keys it differently.** `seed_config_dir` (`spawn.rs:543`) already writes `hasTrustDialogAccepted` and `hasCompletedProjectOnboarding` per project into a Claude Code pane's config, precisely so a pane never boots into a trust dialog — M13's wedge, solved for one harness three work packages ago. The codex equivalent is `[projects."<path>"] trust_level = "trusted"` in the pane's seeded `CODEX_HOME`, and it must be written per worktree or the pane parks unanswerable. The difference is the key itself: `spawn::project_key` canonicalizes (`std::fs::canonicalize`, for macOS's `/tmp` → `/private/tmp`) and matches exactly, while the operator's own codex config carries `[projects."/Users/bubblyducks/harness"]` — a **parent** of the repository — which suggests codex resolves trust by root rather than by exact path. Observed, not verified; it is the concrete form of M23's "it can no longer be one function" and a named spike. **FALSIFIED by C34 (#25):** codex resolves trust by a two-candidate *exact* lookup — canonicalized cwd **or** the git root it resolves to — with no ancestor walk. The `~/harness` entry exists only because codex was once run in that directory itself; `~/harness` is not a git repository, and a key for a parent does not trust the repository inside it. Read C34 before implementing checkpoint 14 for codex.
- **C17. The fourteen checkpoints stand; two are generalized before phase 1 is written.** Checkpoint 6 stops being *"private HOME seeding"* and becomes **"config and credential isolation mechanism"** — `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` for Claude Code, a private `HOME` for cursor, `CODEX_HOME` for codex — because C6 measured that the HOME coupling is cursor's alone and a checkpoint named after one vendor's mechanism is the stale name story 42 exists to prevent. Checkpoint 14 stops being *"project-key canonicalization"* and becomes **"project identity and trust seeding"**, covering both the key's shape and the first-run gate each harness parks a pane on (C16). Nothing added, nothing removed, the count stays honest. Rejected: leaving the names verbatim (checkpoint 6 would then assert something measurably false about a registered harness), and restructuring the fourteen into observable properties over per-harness mechanisms (architecturally better, but it trades a checklist a contributor can tick for an abstraction they must first understand, and rewrites a spec that is ready to implement).
- **C18. Codex lands first — it is the first harness added through the seam, and cursor follows.** On every checkpoint measured today codex is strictly easier: the brief needs no file in the worktree (C3 against M5), a real `pre_tool_use` hook means the write guardrail ports rather than being argued around (C4 against M12), the worker holds no credential exception (C9 against M8), the sweep is a native binary name rather than a Node tree (C11), the transcript store is a versioned schema rather than a gated unknown (C12 against M22), and it brings a token-free behaviour tier the vendor of WP-24 cannot offer (C13). The seam's claim is that adding a harness is a checklist rather than an archaeology project; that claim is tested honestly by the harness whose fourteen answers are already green, not by the one whose unknowns — does an `alwaysApply` `.mdc` survive `/clear`, what is cursor's transcript format — are still open. Cost: the operator-with-a-cursor-subscription story that motivates issue #12 waits.
- **C19. WP-25 owns the `Harness` seam; WP-24 shrinks to "register cursor through it".** WP-25 is now: the `Harness` trait and `HarnessSpec` inside `placement`; the conformance suite over the fourteen checkpoints as generalized by C17; Claude Code registered first and green; codex added through it; the gate's harness + model pickers with C14's probe; and the integration tail (gauge, archive, orphan sweep, project-key split). WP-24 becomes the demonstration story 42 promises rather than asserts. The reason the seam moves rather than staying: a suite with one registered harness is a description, not a suite, so the seam and its first added harness have to ship together — and C18 settled which harness that is. Cost, stated plainly: issue #12 is labelled `ready-for-agent` and is substantially rewritten and re-scoped by this, not merely amended.
- **C20. Four phases, and C13's test tier is built in phase 1 as suite infrastructure.** (1) The `Harness` trait, `HarnessSpec` and the conformance suite over the fourteen checkpoints as generalized by C17, Claude Code the sole registered harness, green — with the token-free vendor tier landed here, because it belongs to the suite rather than to codex. (2) Codex registered: `CODEX_HOME` snapshot seeding, the brief via `model_instructions_file`, C7's sandbox trio, the FLEETOR provider (C9), per-worktree trust (C16), C21's feature overrides. (3) The gate: the `doctor` probe, the pickers, the per-harness cost line. (4) The integration tail: gauge, archive, orphan sweep, project-key split. Building the tier in phase 1 is what makes every checkpoint in phase 2 verified as it is written rather than after. Rejected: a standalone phase for the brief carrier on M21's reasoning (it is one config key and one rendered file, and the tier already asserts it on every run — a phase boundary there is ceremony), and folding the gate into the tail (story 11's refuse-to-start and story 14's stale-model rules are how the gate becomes the part that got rushed).
- **C21. FLEETOR disables four default-on codex features on worker seats — the inverse of M10, and the first exception M10's rule did not anticipate.** `codex doctor --all` reports **47 feature flags enabled by default**, four of which reach past the fence C7 actually enforces: `multi_agent` (a worker spawns its own subagent threads), `browser_use` and `computer_use` — with `browser_use_full_cdp_access` also on — and `in_app_local_automation`. The sandbox trio bounds the filesystem and the network; it does not bound a pane driving a browser or a desktop, and it does not bound a pane fanning out into threads the run manifest never sees. That last is the sharpest: WP-11's archive claims to be the evidence of what a run did, and D-029/D-030 deleted headless supervision deliberately, so a worker quietly re-growing it is a regression wearing a feature flag. The four are set false in the worker's seeded `CODEX_HOME`; the **orchestrator inherits the operator's flags untouched**, since it is their own pane. Every override gets an Activity feed line (story 19). Cost: "preferences are inherited" now carries two exceptions rather than one — C7's sandbox trio and this group.
- **C22. Codex accepts fleetor's delivery path, but it is the first harness that needs a real typing profile — measured, including the failure.** A bracketed paste followed by CR into a live `codex` pty, with fleetor's own `TERM`/`COLORTERM`, submitted the turn: the request reached a capture-server provider carrying a sentinel that appeared nowhere else. **The first attempt did not.** With a 6 s startup wait and a 0.6 s post-paste delay nothing was submitted; with 12 s and 1.5 s it was. The probe used fixed sleeps rather than readiness detection, so which of the two timings is load-bearing is not yet known — but that a timing is load-bearing is now measured, and codex ships a `disable_paste_burst` config key suggesting the paste path is special-cased. This is exactly the failure M6 exists to refuse — a green `accepted` for a message left sitting in an input box, the one lie D-034 was built to prevent — and where M26 found cursor's profile identical to Claude Code's and owed no tuning, codex owes tuning. Checkpoint 9 gets a real per-harness value and a phase-2 spike with readiness detection rather than sleeps.
- **C23. The gate offers a harness per role, and mixed fleets are the point — the operator's own restatement.** M15's shape is confirmed for three harnesses: one orchestrator row, one workers row with a disclosure that expands into four, harness + model on each, so the common case costs one selection and a mixed fleet is one disclosure away. Codex joins Claude Code on both the orchestrator and worker rows (C15); the judges are not offered a choice. What is **not** offered anywhere is a provider picker — C2-as-amended stands.
- **C24. WP-25's out-of-scope list.** Carried from issue #12 unchanged: mid-run harness switching; harness choice for the evaluator and the Critic; per-pane budgets; quota-exhaustion detection; transcript normalization; network egress policy as a security feature; hardening the CLI's identity and authorization model (M27, still unconditional — nothing here re-executes fleet commands out of band); a frontend test runner. Codex adds four of its own. **Per-seat provider choice** — the orchestrator inherits and displays, workers get FLEETOR's, and no picker exists (C2, C9). **Codex's newer `[permissions]` config generation** — C7 ships the legacy trio and C8's gate tripwire is what makes its retirement survivable; migrating is urgent only when the tripwire fires. **Codex subagents, browser use and computer use inside the fleet** — C21 turns all four off on worker seats, and making any of them work, including accounting for subagent threads in the run manifest, is its own arc rather than a compatibility detail smuggled in here. **Reconstructing gauge token usage** if codex does not persist it — C12's spike decides, and if the answer is no the rail says `unavailable`; an estimate synthesized from what FLEETOR sent is the number that looks right and is wrong, which D-054 and M22 already refuse.
- **C25. WP-25 is broken into 28 tickets, #14–#41, published in dependency order with blocking edges as real issue references.** Phase 1 (#14–#23) is sequenced expand–migrate–contract per the wide-refactor rule: one expand ticket defines all fourteen checkpoint fields up front, two conformance-suite tickets (checkpoints 1–7 and 8–14) become the safety net, five migrate batches move call sites in disjoint-file groups so CI stays green batch to batch, and one contract ticket deletes the literals behind a source-reading tripwire. Phase 2 (#24–#33) lands each codex checkpoint with codex **unregistered** and a direct test of its own, so the suite stays green with one harness ticket to ticket and no integration branch is needed; #33 registers codex and is the first moment the suite runs two passes. Phase 3 is #34–#37, phase 4 #38–#41. Three tickets are spikes producing version-stamped notes in `docs/notes/` rather than code (#24 `/clear` survival, #25 trust-key resolution, #38 persisted token usage — the last is the one ticket that spends money). Rejected: registering codex incrementally from its first checkpoint (turns the suite red between tickets, which is the "half-implemented harness compiles quietly" failure story 55 names), and a shared phase-2 integration branch (unnecessary once registration is deferred to #33).
- **C26. Checkpoint 9 carries the paste framing, the submit byte and the submit gap — and deliberately *not* a startup wait, because a per-harness startup wait is a Tier 1.4 violation wearing a constant's clothes.** C22 measured two numbers together and named both: a 6 s startup wait with a 0.6 s post-paste delay submitted nothing, 12 s with 1.5 s submitted. The second is the gap between closing the bracketed paste and writing `\r` — that is `pty::SUBMIT_GAP`, which already exists, is already 30 ms, and is documented at `pty.rs:64` as *the only delay anywhere between `fleet send` and a pty* (D-034). Making it per-harness is a widening of a value the invariant already sanctions, so `TypingProfile::submit_gap_ms` is in. The first is not the same kind of thing: "wait N seconds after spawn before typing" is a new delay on the message path, and Tier 1.4 forbids one — it has been argued and lost twice, and a `HarnessSpec` field is exactly how it would come back, this time as a table entry nobody re-litigates. C22 itself says which of the two timings is load-bearing is not yet known and defers to a phase-2 spike **with readiness detection rather than sleeps**, so encoding a sleep here would pre-commit to the thing that spike exists to replace. Checkpoint 9 is therefore `bracketed_paste`, `paste_start`, `paste_end`, `submit_bytes`, `submit_gap_ms`. Cost, stated: a harness that genuinely needs to be waited for has no field to put that in, and phase 2 will feel the absence as a missing hook — which is correct, because the answer is a readiness *mechanism* on the spawn path, not a number in a spec. What would reverse it: the phase-2 spike showing that readiness cannot be detected for some harness and that a bounded pre-first-turn wait is the only option — in which case it belongs on the pane's bring-up, before the pane is announced as live, where it delays nothing that `fleet send` can already reach. Rejected: adding `startup_wait_ms: 0` for Claude Code and letting phase 2 fill it in (zero is behaviour-preserving today and that is precisely what makes it a trap — the field would land unopposed).
- **C27. The conformance suite asserts checkpoint 5's scrub as *absence* on a fenced pane's command, not as *removal*, and says so in the test rather than reaching for the one thing that would prove it.** `place` returns a `CommandBuilder` whose environment is the application's own, snapshotted at construction, plus placement's overrides — and `env_remove` deletes the entry rather than marking it, so a name that was scrubbed and a name the machine never exported are the same observation. The only way to tell them apart is for the name to be in the process environment when the command is built, which means a test that sets one; `tests/placement.rs` makes exactly that exception once, deliberately, for `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, and the conformance suite makes it zero times because it runs one pass per harness over a growing list of scrubbed names and a suite that mutates the process environment per harness is a suite with an ordering dependency between its own tests. What the suite asserts instead — no scrubbed name has a value on a worker's command — still catches the failure that matters most, a harness handing a fenced pane the operator's own credential namespace, and was verified to catch it by mutation. What it does not catch is a missing `env_remove` on a machine that never exported the name, and that gap is written into the test's own doc comment so the next reader does not "fix" it by setting a variable. What would reverse it: a `Placed` that carried the scrub as data — the names placement decided to remove, returned rather than only applied — which would make removal caller-observable without any environment at all, and is a production change this ticket was scoped out of making. Rejected: setting the variables in-process (racy across the suite's own threads, and the prior art's single exception does not generalize to a per-harness loop), and re-executing the test binary as a child with a controlled environment (real, but it buys one bit at the cost of machinery no other checkpoint needs).
- **C28. Checkpoint 9 is asserted against a real pty driven through `place` with the stand-in pane program, not against `pty.rs`'s constants and not against the spec.** The typing profile is the one checkpoint whose subject is not on `Placed` at all: `place` returns a command, and the profile is the bytes written into that command's pty afterwards. `PASTE_START`, `PASTE_END` and `SUBMIT_GAP` are private constants in `pty.rs` with no public spelling, so the choice was between asserting the spec against itself — which the driver's own header calls worthless — reaching past the interface by making them `pub` for a test's convenience, or observing the bytes where they actually land. The third wins: the conformance driver places a worker exactly as every other checkpoint's worker is placed, with the single documented difference that the machine carries `Host::pane_program`, drives `PaneRegistry::write_paste` over a real pty, and reads back what the process on the far end received. That override is not a workaround — checkpoint 1's own doc comment sets it aside as a fact about the machine rather than about the harness, and `tests/panes.rs` has used it for five real ptys since WP-08. Because the stand-in only ever prints a line it was given as a *submitted* one, a single assertion covers the framing and the submit byte together, and the submit gap is asserted as a floor on how long the write took. Cost, stated: the driver now reads `PATH` off the process to describe the machine — the stand-in's `#!/usr/bin/env bash` has to find a real `bash` — which is a *read* where the suite still sets nothing, and it is the same exception `tests/panes.rs` documents in the same words. What would reverse it: `pty.rs` migrating onto `spec.typing`, at which point the constants become the spec and the round trip is asserting a value against its own source; the pty test is then the migrate batch's safety net rather than the checkpoint's only evidence. Rejected: asserting `spec.typing` against itself (the failure the suite exists to refuse), publishing the constants (a production change to make a test convenient), and a bespoke byte-recorder script with `stty raw -echo` (more machinery than the stand-in that already exists, for the same bytes).
- **C29. Checkpoint 12 asserts the join between the sweep's names and the launched program, and leaves the sweep's *safety* property unasserted rather than making `orphans` public.** The property that matters most about the orphan sweep is that it never searches by name: it reads pids FLEETOR itself recorded and uses the `comm` suffixes only to confirm a recorded pid is still the process it was, so the operator's own harness is never at risk and a missing name leaks rather than kills — cleanup more dangerous than the leak is the real failure. That property is not reachable from `src-tauri/tests/`: `orphans` is a private module and `sweep_at`, `sweep_at_named` and `reap_if_named` are private inside it, so a test would need two walls taken down to drive a pid it controls past a wrong name. It is already covered where it lives, by the crate-internal tests that spawn a real process and assert the sweep leaves it alone under the wrong name. What checkpoint 12 asserts instead is the join that actually drifts and that nothing else covers: **every name the sweep would confirm matches the program `place` really launches**, for every seat — which is exactly the live bug the roadmap names, one literal `"claude"` standing where a per-harness answer belongs, and which fails loudly the day a harness is registered whose program no suffix matches. Stated plainly so the next reader does not mistake the coverage: this catches the spec drifting from the *launcher*, and does not catch the spec drifting from the *sweeper* — `orphans.rs`'s own literal is invisible to it. What would reverse it: the migrate batch that makes `orphans` read `spec.orphans.comm_suffixes` instead of its literal, which both closes that gap and gives the module a public surface worth testing through. Rejected: publishing the module for the suite's benefit (a production change to assert a property that is already tested one level down), and asserting the suffixes against the spec (the same worthless shape C28 refuses).
- **C30. `Placed` carries checkpoint 5's scrub as data, closing C27's gap: `place` now reports the credential names it removed, not only the command it removed them from.** C27 recorded that the conformance suite can assert the scrub only as *absence* — `env_remove` deletes a `CommandBuilder` entry rather than marking it, so a scrubbed name and a name the machine never exported are the same observation — and named the production change that would reverse it. Making it here cost one field on `Placed`, one `spawn::Worker` return type and one `scrub` helper, because this is the batch that owns credential wiring and the removal was already in one place. The list comes back from the same call that performed the removal, so the field cannot claim a scrub the command did not get; it is empty for every attended seat, and that emptiness is the asymmetry (D-030, D-052, D-062) made caller-observable rather than a missing answer. Deliberately *not* the whole of what a placement removes: `CLAUDE_CODE_CHILD_SESSION` goes from every pane as terminal hygiene, belongs to no checkpoint, and is left off so the field means one thing. What the suite can now assert without touching the process environment: a fenced worker's `Placed::scrubbed` equals `spec.credentials.scrubbed_env`, and every attended seat's is empty. What would reverse it: the manifest (M24) wanting the full removal set rather than checkpoint 5's, at which point the field is a superset and its name has to change with it. Rejected: leaving the gap for the contract ticket (#23 deletes literals and adds no data, so it would have been nobody's), and having `place_worker` read `spec.credentials.scrubbed_env` itself beside the builder that removes them (two expressions that can silently disagree, which is the failure the field exists to prevent).
- **C31. The project-key split is carried on the pane, not resolved into a string: `TranscriptSource` holds the harness the pane runs, and both consumers ask it.** M23 records that `spawn::project_key` can no longer be one function, and its two consumers — the trust flag and the context gauge — had agreed on a cwd's spelling only because both named the same function. They now agree because both ask the same harness: `Harness::seed_config_dir` calls `self.project_key` for the record it writes, and `context_gauge::project_dir` calls `source.harness.project_key` for the directory it reads. The alternative was for `place` to resolve the key once and hand the gauge a `project_key: String` — smaller, and it keeps `dyn` out of a data struct — but it answers checkpoint 14 and nothing else, and the gauge's remaining two literals (`projects/` and the window constant, checkpoints 13 and 11) would each need the harness added back in #22. Carrying the harness costs one `Debug` supertrait on the trait and one field on a struct that is recorded from the same `Placed` the command comes off, so the key the gauge looks under and the key the pane's trust record was written under cannot come from different harnesses. What would reverse it: a harness whose transcripts are not keyed by project path at all (a database rather than a directory tree), which makes `TranscriptSource` the wrong shape rather than this field the wrong content. **Noted while migrating, not fixed here:** checkpoint 6's `config_env` has no production reader — the command sets checkpoint 4's `ConfigDir::env_var`, which is the same string, and `config_env` exists for a harness that splits them; it is read by the conformance suite alone, and that is honest rather than dead only for as long as the two stay equal. Also noted: the seed's *values* are not on the spec — `seed_keys` and `trust_keys` name keys, and "the affirmative answer is `true`" is Claude Code's, which is why the seeder is a `Harness` method reading spec key names rather than a shared function that would be general by accident.
- **C32. Checkpoint 7 moves the four names the spec has fields for; the settings document's *model* — JSON, a top-level `hooks` object, and the `{matcher, hooks:[{type, command}]}` entry — stays in the fleet's installer, unmoved and now named as the gap.** `guardrail::install` reads `settings_file`, `hook_event`, `tool_matcher` and `hook_file` off `GuardrailInstall` and nothing else, because nothing else is there: #14 wrote the checkpoint's doc comment as "the installer itself does not move… what varies is the settings file, the event name and the tool matcher", and the conformance suite asserts exactly those four. The ticket's prose also names the *entry shape*, and that genuinely did not move — it cannot, as data, without inventing a schema language for one vendor's JSON, and it cannot be designed honestly before codex's own hook configuration is measured (a phase-2 spike, #31). So the migrate batch moved what the spec can hold and left the document model where it is rather than adding a speculative fifth field. **Tier 1.7 is untouched by the split and deliberately so:** the roots stay the caller's, computed in `placement::guardrail_notices` from the pane's own cwd, and no field of `GuardrailInstall` can widen them — a harness supplies where its refusal is registered, never what it refuses. What would reverse it: #31 measuring codex's hook configuration and finding it is not JSON — which it is not, `config.toml` is TOML — at which point checkpoint 7's *installer* becomes a `Harness` method the way checkpoint 4's seeder did for exactly this reason (C16, C31: the document format is the vendor's, so it belongs to a method rather than to a shared function that would be general by accident), with the roots, the script, the interpreter, the hook command and the journal staying the fleet's free functions beneath it. Rejected: adding entry-shape fields to `GuardrailInstall` now (speculative design for an unmeasured harness, and the field would land unopposed — C26's trap), and moving the whole install behind a trait method in this batch (a Tier 2 reshape of the seam inside a batch whose promise is a byte-identical artifact). **Noted while migrating:** `tool_matcher` now names `guardrail::WRITE_TOOLS` rather than repeating its literal, following the convention harness.rs already states for `HOOK_FILE` and #19 used for `ENV_CC_SECURESTORAGE_DIR`; the value is Claude Code's tool names, so #23 moves the literal into the spec and deletes the constant.
- **C33. The two harness-*unaware* call sites — the orphan sweep and the transcript harvest — read the union over `registered()` rather than a per-pid or per-pane harness record.** Checkpoints 12 and 13 answer per harness, but neither call site has a harness in scope and neither can get one honestly. `orphans::sweep_at` reads a registry that is a list of pids and nothing else, written by a process that has since crashed and read by a launch that knows nothing about the fleet it is cleaning up after. `runs::harvest_transcripts` walks the *previous* run's pane-config directories, whose panes are gone. The alternative in both cases is to persist which harness each pid or pane ran — a registry with a schema that has to survive a crash mid-write, or a marker file written into a config dir purely so a later directory walk could read it back — which is durable state added for the benefit of a lookup. The union costs one `ps` suffix comparison per recorded pid and one failed `read_dir` per harness per pane, and it cannot widen what either does dangerously: the sweep never signals a pid that was not in the registry FLEETOR wrote (a name matching nothing leaks a crashed pane rather than killing a live one), and the harvest files what it finds under the *seat* it found it in, never under a harness name. The harvest's locations are deduplicated so two harnesses agreeing on a location cannot double the count a run manifest reports as its evidence. What would reverse it: a run manifest that already records each pane's harness for M24's reasons, at which point the harvest should read that rather than guess — the sweep would still not, because its registry is deliberately not a manifest. Rejected: making the sweep search `ps` by name so an unrecorded crashed pane could still be reaped, which trades the leak for exactly the danger C29 names.
- **C34. Codex trust is a two-candidate exact lookup, not a root walk: the canonicalized cwd *or* the git root the cwd resolves to — and for a linked worktree that root is the *main repository*.** C16 inferred by-root resolution from the operator's `[projects."/Users/bubblyducks/harness"]`, and that inference is **falsified**: `~/harness` is a plain directory that is not a git repository at all, `~/harness/fleetor` is the repository inside it, and a key for a plain parent trusts nothing below it — with or without git. The entry exists because codex was once run in `~/harness` itself. Measured over twelve arms against `codex-cli 0.153.4`, zero tokens, re-runnable as `python3 examples/codex-spike/trust_probe.py`; the evidence is `docs/notes/codex-trust-key-notes.md`. Three consequences bind issue #30. (1) A key for a **linked git worktree** trusts the worktree root and **not one directory below it**, while a key for the main repository trusts the worktree and everything under it — so a pane's seeded `CODEX_HOME` writes **both** keys, and the main-repository path is the parent of `--git-common-dir`, never `--show-toplevel`, which inside a worktree returns the worktree and is the wrong input. (2) The key must be `std::fs::canonicalize`d: a `/tmp/…` spelling is silently ignored where the cwd resolves to `/private/tmp/…`, exactly the macOS case `spawn::project_key` already handles — the canonicalization ports, only the key's shape differs (M23). (3) The gate reads the **persisted** `config.toml`, so `-c projects."…".trust_level="trusted"` parses, lands in the projects table, and still gates; trust cannot be passed on the command line, and neither `-s workspace-write -a never` nor `--dangerously-bypass-approvals-and-sandbox` opens it. With no entry the pane parks on the gate indefinitely — and that is observable without a human, which is what lets checkpoint 14 assert behaviour rather than an artifact: the literal string `Do you trust the contents of this directory` reaches the pty in about three seconds. What would reverse it: a codex build that resolves the key differently — the probe re-measures the whole matrix in one command and prints a drift note when the running build is not the recorded one.
- **C35. Checkpoints 9 and 10 are read off a `HarnessSpec` the *pane* carries, recorded by `PaneRegistry::spawn` from the same `Placed` the command came off — and a command's spelling is applied in `pty::write_command`, under the one entry lookup that already yields the writer.** The typing profile is the one checkpoint whose subject is not on `Placed` at all (C28): it is the bytes written into a command's pty afterwards, so "read it from the spec" first has to answer *which* spec, and the only two candidates were resolving Claude Code's profile once inside `pty.rs` (which reads from the spec and is still not per-harness — it answers checkpoint 9 for exactly as long as one harness is registered, and phase 2 would have had to do this plumbing anyway) or carrying the harness on the pane. Carrying it wins for C31's reason, sharpened: checkpoints 9 and 10 are *two* answers about one pane, and a pane that looked them up separately could type one harness's paste markers and another harness's `/compact`. The record is written at spawn from the placement that produced the command, so it cannot disagree with the process actually running, and a respawn replaces it. Cost, stated: `PaneRegistry::spawn` grows a third argument and seven call sites — six of them tests — say which harness they are bringing up; that is the price of the registry no longer knowing how to type by assumption, and it is paid once. The command spelling sits in `write_command` rather than in `deliver.rs` because `deliver` would have had to ask the registry for the harness and *then* ask it for the writer — two lookups, two locks, and two chances for a respawn in between to make the spelling and the pty disagree; `write_command` takes the same single lock `write_paste` always took, so the command path gained no lock, no branch and no delay. **A word with no row is typed as it arrived, never refused:** it was allowlisted at accept time and after acceptance nothing may delay, drop or alter it (D-034, D-045), so a second gate here would be exactly the post-accept refusal Tier 1.4 bans — a missing row is a conformance failure instead, caught before a harness can register. **Noted, not fixed here:** checkpoint 10's conformance assertion runs against `fleetor_core::Command::keystrokes()`, which is harness-free, so the suite now asserts the *canonical* spelling and not the production translation; it passes today only because Claude Code's table is the identity, and the gap is covered meanwhile by `pty.rs`'s own unit test against a fabricated table (a real table, deliberately not Claude Code's, so a `spell` that returned its argument would fail). What would reverse it — for the gap: `Pass` gaining a live-pty command write the way `echo_of_a_paste` gave checkpoint 9 one, which is a suite change #16 owns rather than a migrate batch's. What would reverse the whole shape: a harness whose typing profile depends on the pane's *state* rather than its identity, which makes a value recorded at spawn the wrong thing to record. Rejected: reading `claude_code().spec()` inside `pty.rs` (reads from the spec, is not per-harness, and hides the plumbing cost in phase 2), and a `PaneRegistry::record_harness` beside `spawn` (two calls where one would do, and the two can silently disagree — the failure C30's `scrubbed` field exists to prevent).
- **C36. Checkpoint 8 moved nothing, because there is no literal and no assumption in production to move — and saying so is the honest answer rather than writing a reader for a value that cannot vary yet.** `Outbound` is `sandboxed`, `socket_reachable` and `reachability_keys`, and Claude Code's answers are `false`, `true` and empty. The two candidate readers are both dead code: refusing to place, or warning, when `!socket_reachable` is unreachable because the conformance suite already refuses to let such a harness register (a fleet of mute panes is not a registration that may be left open), and applying `reachability_keys` at seed time is applying an empty list. That second one is the shape C26 named and C32 rejected again — a code path that is a no-op today lands unopposed and nobody re-litigates it when it stops being one. `reachability_keys` is also not alone: checkpoint 3's `sandbox_keys` and checkpoint 5's `provider_keys` are the same shape and #18 and #19 left them with the same absent reader, so writing one for reachability alone would make the three disagree about where a harness's config keys get written. What checkpoint 8 *is* — a declared answer plus an end-to-end assertion that every seat is given the fleet's own socket, that it is not inside the pane's private `HOME`, and that `fleet` resolves on the pane's `PATH` — landed in #14 and #16 and is unchanged here. What would reverse it: the phase-2 ticket that registers a harness with a seatbelt (codex, whose socket refusal and its one all-or-nothing lever are measured), at which point all three key lists get one reader together — `Harness::seed_config_dir`, which is already the method the vendor's document format belongs to (C16, C31, C32).
- **C37. The codex brief survives `/clear` — measured against the wire, on both spellings of the carrier, so C3's carrier decision stands unchanged and checkpoint 2 needs no rework.** C3 settled that `model_instructions_file` *replaces* the built-in prompt but explicitly left the context clear unmeasured, on the reasoning that it is a file the harness reads rather than a value snapshotted at boot — and `codex-spike-notes.md` says in terms that *expected is not measured*. It is measured now. A live codex TUI on a real pty was driven through a turn, a `/clear`, and another turn against a local capture server answering `data: [DONE]`; the post-clear request's `instructions` field carried the same 50-character sentinel brief as the pre-clear one, **byte-identical**, with `"You are Codex"` absent from the whole body, while the pre-clear message was gone from `input` — that last is asserted as its own arm, because without it a `/clear` that silently failed would produce the same reading and prove nothing. Both carriers were measured rather than one being inferred from the other: the key in the seeded `CODEX_HOME/config.toml` (C6's `seed_config_dir`, which is how a pane actually gets it) and `-c model_instructions_file=` on the command line (which is what `probe.py` measures). A negative control with the carrier removed returned the built-in **17,730**-character prompt and went red, which is the shape a "no" would have had and independently reproduces C3's number in the **interactive TUI** — C3 measured it through `codex exec`. **What would reverse it:** a vendor build in which the post-clear `instructions` reverts to the built-in prompt; the probe is the tripwire and re-running it is the whole check. A "no" would have been expensive rather than absorbable — C3 measured and rejected both alternative carriers — so it would have been escalated, not designed around. Recorded as suite infrastructure per C13: `examples/codex-spike/probe_clear.py`, five arms, zero tokens, ~40 s, a **sibling** of `probe.py` rather than a ninth arm in it, because `vendor_binary_tier.rs` gates that file's eight arms and exit code and this measurement needs a capture server that numbers every request. Cost, stated: the sibling is runnable but **not yet gated** — wiring it into the vendor-binary tier is a Rust change this spike did not make, so a regression in it would currently be silent. `docs/notes/codex-clear-notes.md` carries the evidence, and also **supports C26 from a second direction**: this probe needs no startup sleep at all, because the three things a sleep was standing in for are all *detectable* — a window size (`pty.fork()` leaves the terminal 0x0 and codex paints empty frames into it), five unanswered terminal capability queries (`CSI 6n`, `OSC 10;?`/`11;?`, `CSI ?u`, `CSI c`), and an animated first-run splash that ends on a keypress rather than a timer. **This does not amend C22:** which of the three explains its 6 s-vs-12 s split was not isolated, so its table stands as recorded. What is settled is that a `startup_wait` field would encode a guess where a readiness check exists, which is the case C26 already makes.
- **C38. The contract step deleted the four `pty` typing constants and `guardrail::WRITE_TOOLS`, and deliberately kept `CLAUDE_CODE_CHILD_SESSION`, `ENV_CC_SECURESTORAGE_DIR` and `WORKER_WINDOW_TOKENS` — the difference is whether a second harness would answer it differently *and* the spec has a field for it.** Both halves are needed. `PASTE_START`, `PASTE_END`, `SUBMIT_BYTES`, `SUBMIT_GAP_MS` and `WRITE_TOOLS` were vendor answers with a field waiting (`TypingProfile`, `GuardrailInstall::tool_matcher`), and each carried a doc comment naming #23 as the ticket that would move it, so they moved and the constants are gone — dead code implementing a pattern the design now bans is the trap `building.md` §6 is about. `ENV_CC_SECURESTORAGE_DIR` and `WORKER_WINDOW_TOKENS` are the opposite shape and stay: the spec *names the item* rather than repeating its string, so there is exactly one spelling and it cannot drift by editing one side, which is the convention `HOOK_FILE` established and the failure this arc exists to prevent pointed the other way round. `CLAUDE_CODE_CHILD_SESSION` is the honest gap: it is a real vendor literal, removed from **every** pane in `apply_terminal_env` as terminal hygiene, and no checkpoint owns it — it is not checkpoint 5, whose scrub is about credentials and is reported on `Placed::scrubbed`, as that field's own doc already records. Moving it would have meant either changing a caller-observable list in the batch whose job is to change nothing, or adding a fifteenth checkpoint in the step that deletes rather than defines; phase 2 answers it when a harness needs a different marker. Rejected: deleting `Posture::sandbox_keys`, `Credentials::provider_keys` and `Outbound::reachability_keys` for having no production reader — C36 already settled that all three share one absent reader in phase 2's `Harness::seed_config_dir`, and deleting them would force phase 2 to re-add them field by field. What would reverse the kept-literal half: a second harness with its own session marker, which turns `CLAUDE_CODE_CHILD_SESSION` into a fifteenth checkpoint rather than a constant. `src-tauri/tests/harness_literals.rs` is the tripwire that holds the deleted half, and its header names both of the two things it cannot catch — that literal, and `write_guardrail.py`'s second spelling of the tool names, which can now drift from `GuardrailInstall::tool_matcher` and would need the script handed its tools rather than baked with them.
- **C39. Seeding takes a `Seed` value rather than two paths, the three key lists' one reader is a dotted-path TOML writer whose value rule is codex's own, and the plugin *cache* is not carried in.** Four things settled together because they are one mechanism, all in `placement::codex` (#26; C6, C9, C31, C32, C36). **(a) `Harness::seed_config_dir(&self, seed: &Seed)`.** Checkpoint 6 for codex is a snapshot of the operator's `~/.codex`, so the seeder needs to know where that is — and `placement`'s one rule is that nothing in it reads the process, so the operator's `HOME` arrives on `Seed::operator_home` from `Host::operator_home` the way every other machine fact does. A struct rather than a third argument because #29 adds the fleet's endpoint and credential to the same call and #30 may add more, and each of those should be a field here rather than a fourth edit to the same four `place_*` arms. The default that lost: two positional paths, which was the phase-1 shape and cannot express a snapshot source at all. What would reverse it: nothing plausible — a harness needing *less* would ignore fields, which the Claude Code seeder already does. **(b) The value in a key list is parsed as TOML with a bare string as the fallback** — so `("sandbox_workspace_write.network_access", "true")` writes the boolean and `("sandbox_mode", "workspace-write")` writes the string. Not invented: it is quoted verbatim from `codex --help`'s own `-c key=value` documentation, which is the form every one of those keys was measured through in the spike, so #28, #29 and #30 write exactly what they would have typed after `-c`. Rejected: requiring the spec to spell TOML literals (`"\"workspace-write\""`, unreadable and wrong-by-default), and a `seed_values` field with a typed enum (C31's named wrong move, and it would make the fleet responsible for a schema the vendor owns). What would reverse it: a harness whose config is not TOML, which is a different `Harness::seed_config_dir` and does not touch this one. **(c) Checkpoint 4's `seed_keys` for codex are `sqlite_home` and `log_dir`, and they are checkpoint 6 wearing checkpoint 4's clothes.** Codex has *no* top-level onboarding flag — the vendor binary's own `ConfigToml` field list has nothing of the shape `hasCompletedOnboarding` has, and its first-run gates are the trust dialog (checkpoint 14) and an animated splash that ends on a keypress (#42's readiness question, not a key). So the honest non-empty answer is the two keys that keep an operator's shared `sqlite_home` from silently putting four panes' thread history in one file, which is the arrangement C6 rejected and the one that would make checkpoint 13's harvest a query instead of a read. **This is checkpoint 4 shaped around Claude Code and it should be said plainly:** `seed_keys` presumes a seed is a small set of affirmative top-level flags, and codex's seed is a *directory snapshot*; the field survives because the two isolation keys are a real answer, not because the question fit. What would reverse it: a codex build that adds a real first-run flag, or a decision to let `seed_keys` be empty for a harness whose seeding is a snapshot — which is a change to the conformance suite, not to this module. **(d) The plugin marketplace cache is not snapshotted, against the ticket's own acceptance criterion.** #26 says skills *and plugins* travel; measured, codex's plugins are an 88 MB marketplace cache under `~/.codex/.tmp/plugins` — a path the vendor named `.tmp` itself — beside a 275 MB `packages/` of downloaded release binaries. Four panes' worth is over a gigabyte per run of content the vendor re-fetches on demand, and pointing a pane's marketplace root at the operator's copy would mean a `codex plugin add` inside a worker writing into the operator's installation, which is exactly what "never written back" forbids. Plugin *configuration* travels with everything else in `config.toml`. What would reverse it: a measurement that a fenced pane cannot re-fetch, or a plugin directory that is preferences rather than cache. Cost, stated: a codex worker starts with a cold plugin cache. Snapshot contents are an **allowlist** (`models.json`, `skills/`, `prompts/`) rather than a denylist, because a name missing from an allowlist costs a preference and a name missing from a denylist copies the operator's credential into four fenced panes.
- **C40. The brief a codex pane runs on travels on the `Seed`, is written in the same atomic pass as the rest of the seed, and an absent one is refused rather than installed.** Checkpoint 2 for codex is `model_instructions_file`, a config key naming a file, so the brief is a *file inside the configuration directory* and the key naming it is a line in the same document the seeder already writes — which makes `Seed::brief` (set through `Seed::with_brief`, C39's builder rather than a fourth positional argument) the honest place for it, and `install_brief` a step inside `install` rather than a call after it. **The default that lost** was a second `Harness` method — `install_brief(config_dir, brief)` called after `seed_config_dir` — which reads better as a seam (checkpoint 2 gets its own method, the way checkpoint 4 has one) and is wrong in mechanism: it is a second read-modify-write of `config.toml` on every spawn, so a pane can reach its prompt with the trust row and the isolation keys installed and the carrier missing, and a half-configured codex pane is indistinguishable from a working one. Rejected for the same reason: writing the brief from the command builders, which are pure command construction and do no I/O. **An empty brief is an `Err`, not a shorter prompt** — a codex pane with no carrier key runs the vendor's built-in 17,730-character prompt, renders a prompt, accepts a paste and answers, having never been told it is a FLEETOR worker; the seeder is the last place that can see it, so it refuses there, exactly as `refuse_home_relative` does for the tilde trap. Cost, stated: `Seed::new` leaves the brief empty, so a harness whose carrier is a config key refuses a seed nobody briefed — which is loud by construction and is why the four `place_*` arms now render the brief *before* seeding rather than after. `place_orch` and `place_worker` already rendered it for the Loadout notice, so that is a move rather than a new render; the command builders still render their own copy of the same text, which is duplication that predates this ticket and is harmless while codex carries no brief in argv. **What would reverse it:** a harness whose brief must be written somewhere the config seed does not reach — a keychain entry, a socket handshake — at which point checkpoint 2 becomes a `Harness` method for the reason checkpoint 4 became one (C31, C32). **Tier 3, noted rather than argued:** the file is `fleetor-brief.md` inside `CODEX_HOME`, `fleetor-` prefixed because that directory is the vendor's and a bare `brief.md` there has no visible author. **One thing did not fit the recorded shape, and it is a measurement rather than a decision:** C3 and C37 both quote the request's top-level `instructions` field, but both drove `probe.py`'s *cloned catalog entry*; against the build's own default model there is no `instructions` field at all and the prompt travels as the first `developer` message in `input`. The replace-not-append property is identical either way — with the carrier, `input[1]` is the brief and `You are Codex` is absent from the whole body; without it, `input[1]` is the built-in prompt — so C3 stands unamended and the vendor-binary arm asserts on the body rather than on a field name. `docs/notes/codex-spike-notes.md` carries it (#27; C3, C37, C39, D-042, D-043).
- **C41. Codex's containment is two spec rows and one seat, the four feature overrides are the module's rather than the spec's, and `seed_config_dir` now returns Activity feed lines.** Four things settled together because they are one mechanism, all in `placement::codex` and `placement::harness` (#28; C5, C7, C21, C36, C39). **(a) Checkpoints 3 and 8 needed no new mechanism, only rows** — `sandbox_keys` is `sandbox_mode = "workspace-write"` and `approval_policy = "never"`, `reachability_keys` is `sandbox_workspace_write.network_access = true`, each written exactly as it would be typed after `-c` per C39(b). All three land on **every** codex pane including the orchestrator's, because that is what a spec key list means and because both FLEETOR-owned keys are load-bearing for messaging rather than for comfort: an operator who narrowed either would get a fleet whose panes cannot write or cannot talk. **(b) The four features are a module constant, not a spec field** (`WORKER_FEATURE_OVERRIDES`, five rows for four features — `browser_use` carries `browser_use_full_cdp_access`). They are vendor feature names read by the one method that writes them, which is `PROVIDER_CREDENTIAL_KEYS`'s shape and C31's rule; a spec field for "keys set on every seat but the operator's" is a field every other harness answers with an empty list. What *is* general is the seat, and that is `Seed::operators_own_seat` — **a boolean defaulting to `false`, not a `PaneId`**, because under C39's builder shape a `PaneId` has no safe default and the narrow answer must be the one a seat nobody thought about gets. Only `place_orch` says otherwise, in one line. **(c) `Harness::seed_config_dir` returns `Vec<(NoticeLevel, String)>`.** Story 23 wants every override announced, and the seeder is the only place that can tell an override from a default: the call site sees a directory, and only the seeder sees the document it replaced. Returned rather than emitted, which is `prompts.rs`'s rule and for its reason — a notice a store has to exist to observe is a notice no test asserts. Two shapes, deliberately: a **`Warn` per key whose operator value actually lost** (`prompts.rs`'s conditional shape, so a default machine is silent), and **one unconditional `Info` per codex pane** naming what FLEETOR narrowed — unconditional because codex's 47 feature flags are on by *default* rather than by a line in anyone's config, so a diff-based notice would say nothing at all about the most consequential thing the seeder does. Rejected: one line per overridden key, which is 20 identical lines a run and a feed nobody reads the tenth line of. Cost, stated: the seeder is not handed a `PaneId`, so its notices name the pane by the **configuration directory's last segment**, which is the pane's name by construction (`Layout::pane_config`); a second field answering the same question is two chances to disagree. **(d) The containment assertion needs two vendor instruments, and this is measured.** `codex sandbox` takes its sandbox from `-c` and **ignores `sandbox_mode` in `config.toml` entirely** — a file saying `danger-full-access` still runs read-only, and a trust row does not change it — so it proves what the trio's *values* do and nothing about the *file*. `codex doctor --json` is the other half: `checks["sandbox.helpers"]` names the posture resolved from the file (`restricted`/`Never` under FLEETOR's seed, `unrestricted`/`OnRequest` under an operator's) and `checks["config.load"].details["enabled feature flags"]` names the resolved feature list, which is C21's instrument. Both zero tokens, ~1.3 s. **The inside-the-worktree write is asserted alongside the outside one**, because the spike's arm 2 measured only the refusal and a read-only fallback passes that arm perfectly while shipping a worker that cannot work — the exact failure C7 rejected the newer permission-profile generation over. Also measured: `workspace-write`'s default writable roots include `$TMPDIR` and `/tmp`, so a scratch root under `temp_dir()` is *inside* the fence and the test hands the child a `TMPDIR` inside its own worktree to reproduce the real layout. **What would reverse (a):** a codex build where the legacy keys stop being honoured, which #37's gate tripwire catches. **What would reverse (b):** a second harness with per-seat feature flags, which turns the constant into a fifteenth checkpoint. **What would reverse (c):** nothing plausible — a harness with nothing to announce returns an empty vector, which is Claude Code's answer. **Not reopened:** the narrow socket lever (C5). `codex sandbox --allow-unix-socket <PATH>` was found while reading the subcommand's flags and is exactly the targeted allowance C5 wanted — but it is a flag on that subcommand with **no configuration-key equivalent**, and a pane is what the fleet runs. A build that exposes it as a config key is the measurement that would reopen it.

Checkpoint 5 for codex is an entry on every pane, a selection on every seat but the operator's, and a credential that never lands in a file — and the fleet's `responses`-wire endpoint is spelled a second time on purpose.** Four things settled together because they are one mechanism, all in `placement::codex` (#29; C2, C9, C27, C30, C36, C39, C41). **(a) The fleet's key travels in a variable, not in the document.** Codex's `ModelProviderInfo` offers two credential mechanisms and this is the choice between them: `experimental_bearer_token` would write the fleet's key in plaintext into `pane-config/worker-N/config.toml`, and `env_key` names a variable instead — `FLEETOR_CODEX_KEY`, set by `spawn::worker_command_with` on the same line, in the same function, that sets a Claude Code worker's `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN`. So `Credentials::token_env` is `Some` for a harness whose credential is "configured", which reads like a contradiction and is not: the *entry* is configuration and the *secret* is environment. **The default that lost** is the one #26's own spec comment predicted — "a `provider_keys` answer, not an environment one" — and it lost to a measurement plus a property. Measured on `codex-cli 0.153.4`: with `env_key` set, `codex doctor --json` reports `auth.credentials` as `auth is provided by the active model provider` and names the variable; with the variable absent it **fails rather than falling back, even when the operator's own `CODEX_API_KEY` is present in the environment**. So the fleet's provider entry cannot be satisfied by an operator's shell credential *by construction*, which makes `scrubbed_env` the second line of defence rather than the only one — and it also means no `Seed` field carrying a secret was needed, so nothing under `pane-config/` holds a credential at all, the fleet's included. **What would reverse it:** a codex build that drops `env_key` for custom providers, or one whose fallback order lets an ambient auth variable satisfy a named `env_key` — the vendor-tier arm's middle assertion is the tripwire and re-running it is the whole check. **(b) The entry is a spec key list and the selection is not.** `credentials.provider_keys` is the four rows of `[model_providers.fleetor]` and lands on **every** codex pane, which is C41(a)'s rule unchanged and is safe because a provider nothing selects is inert — measured: an unselected table whose `env_key` variable is absent resolves clean. `model_provider = "fleetor"` is `WORKER_PROVIDER_SELECTION`, a module constant applied only when `!Seed::operators_own_seat`, for `WORKER_FEATURE_OVERRIDES`'s reason (C31, C41(b)). **Putting the selection in the spec was the alternative and it is wrong rather than merely worse**: a spec key list lands on every pane, so it would have run the orchestrator on the fleet's credential and flatly contradicted C9's amendment to C2. **(c) `scrubbed_env` is `OPENAI_API_KEY` and `CODEX_API_KEY`, and both names are the vendor's own** — `doctor` reports them under `auth env vars present`, so an operator with either in a shell profile is an operator whose personal credential codex recognises. `env_key`'s refusal to fall back (a) means neither can reach a worker's active provider today; they are removed anyway, because a fenced pane holding the operator's credentials in its environment is the thing D-062 forbids regardless of this build's resolution order, and because `Placed::scrubbed` (C30) is what makes the removal assertable as data. **(d) The fleet's endpoint is spelled twice, and the second spelling is a literal.** `model_providers.fleetor.base_url` is `https://api.deepseek.com/` with `wire_api = "responses"`; `LaunchConfig::worker_base_url` is `https://api.deepseek.com/anthropic`. Same vendor, two wire protocols, two paths, and no launch key holds the `responses` one — so an operator who repoints `worker.base_url` in `launch.conf` repoints their Claude Code workers and **not** their codex ones. Stated rather than hidden, and rejected: deriving it by stripping `/anthropic` (inventing a rule the vendor does not document, over a value an operator can edit), and adding a second launch key (operator-visible configuration surface this ticket was not scoped to add, and C24 keeps per-seat provider choice out). **What would reverse it:** a fleet pointed at a vendor whose two wire endpoints are not both derivable from one another, which is the ticket that adds the second launch key. **Cost, stated:** a codex worker cannot spend an operator's subscription plan even when that is what they wanted, and a codex orchestrator's inherited provider survives the snapshot with its credential struck (C9) — which is #26's behaviour unchanged, not this ticket's, and is written up as the open question #33 inherits.
