The credential strike is seat-conditional, and the orchestrator's snapshot carries the operator's login.** #44, closing C42's cost line (C2, C6, C9, C14, C42; D-030, D-052, D-062). **What changed:** `install` copies `OPERATORS_OWN_ENTRIES` — `auth.json`, one name — and skips `strike_provider_credentials`, on the seat where `Seed::operators_own_seat` is `true` and on no other. Measured on `codex-cli 0.153.4`: an orchestrator seeded the old way reports `auth.credentials` = **`fail`, "no Codex credentials were found"** — a pane whose configuration loads, which starts, and which cannot authenticate. The narrow default was still right as a *default*; what was wrong is that only `place_orch` was allowed to opt out of the provider selection and nothing was allowed to opt out of the strike. **Copying in is not writing out** — C6's snapshot-not-symlink rule is untouched, the pane's `CODEX_HOME` is still never written back, and `the_operators_installation_is_never_written_to` now seeds an orchestrator alongside its four workers to say so. **All three of C14's auth shapes survive, each with its own vendor reading** (`the_operators_own_seat_authenticates_and_a_fenced_seat_holds_nothing_of_the_operators`, ~1.7 s, zero tokens, loopback only): a subscription plan and an API key are `auth.json` and read back as `stored auth mode = chatgpt` / `api_key`; a named custom provider is `config.toml`'s and reads back as `provider auth env var … (present)`. The third is the operator's own installation, so a copy handling only the first two would have passed on any machine but theirs. **The negative control is the load-bearing arm**: the same seeded orchestrator with the copied `auth.json` removed reproduces the failure above, so the assertion measures the copy rather than the machine. **Stated rather than hidden:** `doctor` reads a *bearer token* nowhere — a provider table with one and a table with none produce byte-identical `auth.credentials` — so that spelling of shape three is asserted off the document the vendor loaded and is labelled as the artifact assertion it is. Also stated: the copy is one-way in both directions, so a plan token the pane refreshes is not carried back, and a re-seed overwrites the pane's copy with the operator's again. **What would reverse it:** a codex build that reaches the operator's login without the file — a keychain, or a credential helper — which would make the orchestrator's seat free the way Claude Code's is and retire `OPERATORS_OWN_ENTRIES`; or a seat between the two, which is the ticket that turns the boolean into something with three answers. **Not reopened:** the fence. A worker's snapshot is unchanged, #29's byte scan over every file under the pane directory stays green unweakened, and this tier now scans a fenced pane for all three credential shapes as well.
- **C44. A codex pane is woken before it is announced, and the readiness answer is a second map rather than a check on the delivery path.** #42 (C6, C22, C26, C37; D-034, D-051, D-081; Tier 1.4, Tier 1.5). **Measured first, because #24 only measured the splash inside its own probe and a finding that reproduces only in the instrument is a finding about the instrument.** It reproduces against `PaneRegistry::spawn` itself: a `CODEX_HOME` written by codex's own `seed_config_dir` opens on an animated splash that paints every 72 ms, never ends on its own, and **discards** a bracketed paste delivered into it — the pane looks perfectly healthy and `fleet send` would answer `accepted` for a message that is gone. It is **not** an artifact of an empty directory: an empty `CODEX_HOME` and a snapshot of the operator's real `~/.codex` animate identically (196 KB and 195 KB over the same window). **What changed:** `HarnessSpec` gains `bring_up: BringUp`, read by `PaneRegistry::spawn` and by nothing else. `AtOnce` is Claude Code's and is the path every pane has always taken. `AfterWaking` is codex's: the pane gets its pty, its pump and its channels immediately — the operator watches it come up — but it is held in a second `waking` map until `wake` presses the key the splash ends on and observes the pane fall silent, and only then does it enter the map delivery reads. Measured cost: **ready in ~2 s after 2 presses**. **Why a second map rather than a readiness check in `writable`:** the two behave identically and only one of them is legal. A check in `writable` sits between `fleet send` and a live pty, which Tier 1.4 forbids and which `building.md` §9 records as argued and lost twice; holding an unready pane out of the map adds nothing to the message path at all, and `writable`, `write`, `write_paste`, `write_command` and `type_framed` are untouched — asserted by a source-reading tripwire, because a check inside `writable` would behave the same as this and no test that *runs* the code could tell them apart. The root workspace's **226** hub and CLI tests pass unchanged, which is the evidence. **Why this is not the field C26 refused:** C26 rejected a `startup_wait` number on checkpoint 9, including a zero-valued placeholder, on the grounds that a number encodes a guess where a readiness check exists and that a behaviour-preserving placeholder lands unopposed. `BringUp` carries no number, is not on checkpoint 9, and Claude Code's `AtOnce` is a measured statement rather than a value awaiting one; the *when* is a condition on what the pane paints, in `pty.rs`, where `QUIET_SAMPLE` is 400 ms against a measured worst-case splash gap of **152 ms** over 279 frames. C26's own reversal clause named this place — "on the pane's bring-up, before the pane is announced as live, where it delays nothing that `fleet send` can already reach". **Cost, stated, three ways.** A message aimed at a pane during its ~2 s bring-up is refused for want of a pane and comes back `Delivered { accepted: false }` carrying "… is not running" — the same answer a pane nobody has spawned gives, and the sentence the sending model reads on stderr. **`recorded` would have been the wrong word and the claim was corrected before this line was final:** D-051's `recorded` is the operator seat, the task board and a handoff — ops that type nowhere — and `deliver::outcome_of` maps a refused write to `rejected`, not to it. `accepted: false` is strictly better than today's green `accepted` for a swallowed message, and it is a real behaviour change for a codex pane's first seconds. A pane that never stops painting is never announced: unbounded on purpose, because the alternative is a deadline that announces a pane which is still swallowing, and waiting forever is D-034's honest failure. And `pty_spawn` now blocks for the length of a codex bring-up, which the UI's terminal component awaits. **What would reverse it:** a vendor build whose fresh `CODEX_HOME` reaches a composer that accepts input with no keypress — `src-tauri/tests/codex_bringup.rs` is the tripwire, and its control arm, which spawns the same pane under `AtOnce` and requires the message to be swallowed, goes red first. Rejected: writing the wake key once at spawn and letting pty ordering deliver it (**measured and false** — a `\r` written before the pane reads is discarded, still animating at 25 s); a readiness check in `writable` (Tier 1.4); a `startup_wait` field (C26); and answering the vendor's terminal capability queries from `pty.rs`, which `probe_clear.py` needed for its 0x0 pty and which measurement showed the wake does not — the pane settles and takes the paste with nothing answering `CSI 6n` at all.

Checkpoint 7's installer becomes a `Harness` method, `write_guardrail.py`'s second spelling of the tool names is closed, and codex's hook is installed correctly and *does not fire* — which is said out loud rather than shipped quietly.** #31, closing C32's named reversal condition and answering C4's named unknown (C4, C7, C21, C32, C36, C39, C41; D-065). **(a) The installer moved, exactly as C32 predicted it would have to.** `guardrail::install` hardcoded a JSON document — a top-level `hooks` object keyed by event, entries shaped `{matcher, hooks:[{type, command}]}` — and codex's settings file is `config.toml`. So checkpoint 7 followed checkpoint 4's precedent (C16, C31): `Harness::install_guardrail` is a fourth trait method, Claude Code's JSON merge moved into `harness.rs` beside the vendor whose format it always was, codex's TOML merge lives in `placement::codex`, and the script, the interpreter, the hook command, the temp-file install and the journal stayed free functions in `guardrail`. **Tier 1.7 did not move and cannot**: the roots arrive on `guardrail::GuardrailPlacement`, already computed by `placement::guardrail_notices` from the pane's own cwd, so no implementation of the method is handed anything it could widen them with. No `roots` field was added to the spec and none was wanted — the thing the ticket said to escalate on never came up. **(b) `tool_matcher` is deleted and `write_tools: &[&str]` replaces it, which is what closes #23's carry-forward.** A matcher string and a tool list are not one fact: a matcher is the settings document's syntax, and the tool names are what arrives in a payload. `harness_literals.rs` was already splitting the matcher on `|` to recover the list, which is the evidence the list was the real fact. Claude Code's installer now renders `"Bash|Write|…"` from it as *its own syntax for* that list, and the script is handed the same list on `--tool` — so `write_guardrail.py`'s baked `WRITE_TOOLS` set is gone and there is one spelling in the fleet. It reads the input's *shape* now and applies both readings to every call, which can only ever refuse more. **(c) The hook protocol ported for free, and the settings document was the only difference.** Measured against `codex-cli 0.153.4`'s own embedded schemas: codex's `PreToolUse` payload names `cwd`, `tool_name` and `tool_input` exactly as Claude Code's does, and both read `hookSpecificOutput.permissionDecision = "deny"`. A `hook_dialect` spec field was written and then deleted as unearned. Two codex rules are stricter and are now asserted rather than trusted: only `deny` is supported (`allow`/`ask`/`approve` are rejected by name, and this script never emits one — a permitted call is `{}`, an opinion withheld), and a denial with an empty reason is rejected outright, which would let the write through. **(d) Two spec rows were wrong in ways that would have shipped a silent guardrail, and both were measured rather than carried over.** The event is **`PreToolUse`**, not `pre_tool_use` — the snake_case spelling C4 recorded is real but is the one codex uses inside a *trust key*, while the `config.toml` table is PascalCase. And `write_tools` for codex is **Claude Code's tool names**, because codex *normalizes* its wire names in a hook payload: a call the wire calls `exec_command` arrives as `"tool_name": "Bash"` with `{"command": "…"}`. A list of codex's own wire names — which is what this ticket started with — would have matched nothing and refused nothing. `apply_patch` is carried alongside and is labelled as the one unmeasured name; an unrecognised name is permitted, so carrying one that never arrives changes no behaviour while omitting one that does is a write nobody refuses. `features.hooks = true` is written on every seat: it is default-*on* in this build, but the pane's configuration is a snapshot of the operator's (C6, C39), so an operator's `hooks = false` would otherwise produce five unguarded panes. It is the first override that turns a feature *on*, the inverse of C21's four. **(e) C4's unknown is answered and the answer is bad: an untrusted hook is skipped silently and the tool call proceeds.** The vendor says so itself — a real `codex` TUI on a real pty with one `PreToolUse` hook and no trust record renders `Hooks need review … 3. Continue without trusting (hooks won't run)`, and measured both ways against the same `CODEX_HOME` the hook script simply never runs, with no warning and nothing in the transcript. Answering that prompt makes codex write the record, and its **key** is now known exactly — `"<absolute config.toml path>:pre_tool_use:<group>:<hook>"`, zero-based, snake_case event where the table is PascalCase — reproduced byte for byte. What is **not** known is `trusted_hash`: a `sha256:` over something wider than the declaration, which did not reproduce as the command, the command plus a newline, the script's contents, or ~200 JSON and TOML renderings of the handler, including for a command as short as `/bin/true`. **What would settle it**, and the probe is written down rather than described: drive `codex app-server --listen stdio://`, send `initialize` then `{"method":"hooks/list","params":{"cwds":["<cwd>"]}}`, and read `currentHash` off the reply — but that is a live call to the vendor binary, and `placement`'s one rule is that nothing in it reads the process, so a per-pane hash cannot live on `Host` the way C8's gate probe does. **(f) So FLEETOR writes no trust record and refuses to pretend.** Three things were deliberately not done: `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust` was not reached for — it is Tier 1.7's to decide and not a builder's (`building.md` §9.2), and it is the wrong shape anyway, trusting *every* hook in the document including one an operator's snapshot carried in; a guessed hash was not written, because a wrong one reads back as `modified`, the vendor's word for tampered-with rather than unreviewed; and the install was not skipped, so the entry, the script, the feature and the roots are all correct and the day the hash is known this is one function. What ships instead is an **`Error` on the Activity feed on every codex pane** saying the guardrail is installed but will not fire, naming the exact `hooks.state` key an operator would have to fill in — which is this module's existing rule for the missing interpreter (*a guardrail that quietly does nothing is worse than no guardrail, because the operator believes in it*), and turns this arc's signature failure from a silent one into a loud one. **Not reopened:** the `sha2` dependency this would have needed was added and then removed with the record it was for; it was already in the lock via tauri, so it costs a line and no tree when the hash is known. **What would reverse (f):** the hash, by any route — at which point checkpoint 7 for codex is complete and #33 can register it. **#33 blocks on this**, which is the correct outcome rather than a failure: a harness whose guardrail does not fire is not one the registry may hold, since `checkpoint_7_every_seat_gets_the_write_guardrail_with_roots_no_wider_than_its_own` would pass on the artifact while the pane ran unguarded. **Verified:** src-tauri **305** green (209 lib, up from 204 — five new codex checkpoint-7 tests including the decision driven through the installed command with a real `sh` and a codex-shaped payload) and root workspace **226** unchanged; clippy adds nothing beyond the two pre-existing warnings; the registry still holds one harness and `codex_is_implemented_and_still_not_registered` still pins it; the eight existing write-guardrail tests pass **unchanged**, which is what says the Claude Code path is byte-compatible after the refactor. Zero tokens: every codex measurement was `doctor`, a pty that was killed before anything was typed into it, or a loopback provider.
- **C45 addendum — the two things measured after C45 was written, one of which is worse than anything in it.** **(a) `apply_patch` is not normalized.** Only `exec_command` is renamed, and it is renamed to `Bash`; `apply_patch` arrives verbatim with the raw patch text under `command`, and `view_image` arrives as `view_image`. So codex's normalization is *partial*, and Claude Code's five names alone would have let every codex file edit through — carrying `apply_patch` on speculation turned out to be load-bearing rather than cheap insurance. `matcher` is measured too: it filters on the normalized `tool_name` and alternation works (`"Bash|apply_patch"` fired for both, `"apply_patch"` for one), so the decision to omit a matcher and filter in the script is now a choice rather than an avoidance. **(b) The cmux PATH shim silently erases a `config.toml` hook, which is a second, independent way this guardrail does not fire — and it has nothing to do with trust.** `codex` on `PATH` on this machine is a wrapper (recorded in `docs/notes/codex-trust-key-notes.md`) that injects `-c hooks.PreToolUse=[{…cmux's own script…}]`, and a `-c` override **replaces the whole array rather than appending to it**. Measured, same `CODEX_HOME`, only the binary differing: `/opt/homebrew/bin/codex` fired `PreToolUse` twice (`Bash` and `apply_patch`); the shim fired it **zero** times, running only cmux's own `SessionStart` and `UserPromptSubmit`. So the shim's `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust` was a red herring throughout — through it the hook does not run because it is *no longer in the configuration*, not because it is untrusted. **This is exactly the failure the ticket predicted in advance**: a naive test through `PATH` would have passed for the wrong reason, and testing both is what caught it. **What it implies, stated and not decided here:** a guardrail registered in `config.toml` cannot survive a pane launched through a wrapper that overrides the same key, so checkpoint 1's `program.bin` (`"codex"`, resolved on `PATH`) and checkpoint 7 are coupled in a way no checkpoint currently records. The two candidate fixes — resolve the vendor binary absolutely rather than by name, or inject the guardrail as its own `-c` and clobber cmux in turn — are both changes to how a pane is *launched*, which is #33's and #42's territory rather than this ticket's. Filed rather than fixed.
- **C46. Codex's write guardrail ships installed-but-not-firing, loudly, and `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust` is refused — the fix is gated on a measurement (#45).** #31 measured two independent failures: against the real binary an *untrusted* `PreToolUse` hook is **silently skipped** and the tool call proceeds (the TUI's own prompt, `Hooks need review … Continue without trusting (hooks won't run)`, is the evidence, and a fleet-spawned pane has no human to answer it); and the cmux PATH shim injects `-c hooks.PreToolUse=[…]`, where **`-c` replaces the array rather than appending**, erasing a `config.toml` guardrail outright. The trust *key* is known byte-for-byte (`"<abs config.toml path>:pre_tool_use:<group>:<hook>"`) but the *hash* did not reproduce across ~200 renderings, and the one reliable source — codex's own `hooks/list` — is a live vendor call, which `placement`'s "nothing reads the process" rule forbids. **The bypass flag was refused on two grounds**: it is a Tier 1.7 decision rather than a builder's, and decisively it *does not fix the problem* — the shim-clobber is independent of trust, so it would buy a fence that works on one machine's binary and not another's, which is worse than a known gap. **PARTLY FALSIFIED by C48 (#45): there is no shim-clobber.** `-c` does not replace a `config.toml` hook — the two sources compose; what replaces is one `-c` by a *later* `-c` for the same key. So the decisive half of this objection is void. C48 also measured that a `-c`-delivered hook does **not** fire untrusted either — trust is a property of the hook, not of how it arrived — so the composition #45 planned was unnecessary as well as insufficient. **The one surviving objection to the flag is that it trusts every hook in the document rather than exactly ours**; see C49 for how that is answered. What ships instead: no trust record (a wrong hash reads back as `modified` — "tampered with" — which is worse than absent) and an **Error on every codex pane's Activity feed** naming the exact key an operator must fill in. **Why this blocks #33 rather than being an emergency:** C7's sandbox is the primary fence and #28 measured it against the real seatbelt (a write outside the worktree refused with no file created, a write inside succeeding), so what is degraded is *defence in depth*, not containment — but a harness whose guardrail does not fire is still not one the registry may hold, because conformance would pass on the artifact while the pane ran unguarded. Reversal: #45 measures whether a `-c`-delivered hook fires *untrusted*; if it does, our hook composes into a single `-c hooks.PreToolUse=[…]` **alongside** the wrapper's rather than replacing it, closing the trust gap and the shim-clobber together with no `dangerously` flag. Two spec rows were also wrong in ways that would have shipped a silent guardrail and are now fixed: the event is `PreToolUse`, not the `pre_tool_use` C4 recorded (that spelling is real, but only inside the trust key), and codex normalizes tool names only **partially** — `exec_command` arrives as `Bash` while `apply_patch` arrives verbatim, so Claude Code's five names alone would have refused nothing.

Every vendor measurement taken through the PATH `codex` was taken through a wrapper that injects flags, and at least one verdict differs absolutely.** The `codex` on this machine is a cmux shim in front of `/opt/homebrew/bin/codex` that adds `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust` and `-c hooks.PreToolUse=[…]`. #25 ran its whole trust matrix both ways and got identical verdicts on every arm, which is why this went unnoticed; #31 found the case where they differ absolutely — our hook fires zero times under the shim and twice under the real binary. **Rule going forward: any arm measuring hooks, flags or config precedence resolves the vendor binary absolutely and reports both readings.** **C48 contradicts this entry's own evidence and the contradiction is unresolved:** #31 read *zero* firings under the shim, #45 measured the hook *firing* there. The likeliest reconciliation is that #31's run fell through the wrapper's `IN_CMUX`/socket passthrough and injected nothing — not verified, not reproducible. The rule stands regardless, and this is why. Arms measuring the seatbelt, the wire or the config document are unaffected, because the shim's injections do not reach them. Reversal: a machine whose `codex` is the real binary, where the distinction costs nothing and the second reading can be dropped.
- **C48. A `-c`-delivered codex hook does not fire untrusted either — so #45's fix does not exist, the shim-clobber never did, and what remains is one Tier 1.7 decision about trust.** C46 gated the guardrail fix on one measurement: *does a hook delivered via `-c hooks.PreToolUse=[…]` fire without a trust record?* Measured against `/opt/homebrew/bin/codex` per C47, with two positive controls proving the instrument: **no.** `config-toml-untrusted-is-skipped` and `c-flag-untrusted-is-skipped-too` differ only in delivery channel, and both are skipped silently while the out-of-worktree write lands; the same two hooks under `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust` both fire and both deny. **Hook trust in this build is a property of the hook, not of how the hook arrived.** The hint that suggested otherwise is explained and the attribution was simply wrong: cmux's hooks run because the same shim injects **`--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust`** alongside its six `-c hooks.*` flags — read off the shim's own arg stream (`cmux hooks codex inject-args`) rather than argued. **C46's second cause is falsified as stated: `-c` does not clobber a `config.toml` hook.** Ours in `config.toml` plus a decoy in `-c` and *both* fire — the two sources compose; what replaces is one `-c` by a **later `-c` for the same key**, which is the behaviour #31 saw and generalised one step too far. So the shim erased nothing, the guardrail was skipped for exactly one reason, and **`install_guardrail`'s existing placement needs no change** — the composition #45 planned was unnecessary as well as insufficient. **Also established, and it is what makes the remaining gap precisely one thing:** the shipped `write_guardrail.py`, driven from a real codex `exec_command` call through the exact command line `install_guardrail` composes, refuses the write and files the Activity-feed journal line (`pane="worker-1"`, `tool="Bash"`). Everything except trust works. **So #45 stops here by its own instruction** — the ways to close it are all Tier 1.7 and get decided with the measurement in hand: the bypass flag (whose *decisive* objection in C46, that it does not fix the shim-clobber, is now itself void — but whose first objection stands untouched: it trusts **every** hook in the document, including any an operator or a wrapper injected, which widens what a pane may execute); its config twin `-c bypass_hook_trust=true`; a correct `trusted_hash`, not reopened because #31 already paid for that and it bought nothing; or `hooks/list`, which `placement` may not call. **One unverified lead, named rather than chased:** the binary carries a **fourth trust state `managed`** (the enum reads `managed|untrusted|trusted|modified`) beside an `allowManagedHooksOnly` managed-config field, which plausibly trusts a hook by *provenance* rather than by hash — string-level evidence only, enforcement not read, and the probe that would settle it is a hook placed in a managed-config layer observed without a trust record. **Honest gap:** C47 records #31 reading our hook as firing *zero* times under the shim; this measured it *firing* there, because the shim injects the bypass. The likeliest reconciliation is that #31's shim run fell through the wrapper's `IN_CMUX`/socket passthrough and so injected nothing — not verified, and not reproducible from here. `examples/codex-spike/hook_probe.py` is the instrument (eleven arms, zero tokens, one command); `docs/notes/codex-hook-trust-notes.md` is the record. The bypass flag's only **executable** use in the repository is that probe's positive control — elsewhere it appears solely as prose explaining why it was not reached for — and proving an instrument with a flag is the opposite of shipping one. #31's Activity-feed Error stays: the guardrail still does not fire, and its text still names the right key. Reversal: a Tier 1.7 decision on trust, or a codex build that gates hooks by document rather than by entry.
- **C49. The remaining hook-trust gap is answered by FLEETOR owning a worker's `hooks` array outright, not by trusting more.** C48 removed C46's decisive objection (there is no shim-clobber) and closed every delivery-shaped route (`-c` and `config.toml` behave identically; trust is a property of the hook). What is left is a genuine Tier 1.7 question: `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust`, and its config twin `bypass_hook_trust`, trust **every hook in the document**, and `install_guardrail` currently *merges* the operator's own hooks into a pane's config — so bypassing would execute the operator's hooks unverified inside a fenced worker. That is the objection, stated precisely, and it is about *whose* hooks run rather than about the flag's name. **The answer is the asymmetry this arc already applies everywhere else: on a worker seat FLEETOR owns the `hooks` array, exactly as it owns the sandbox keys (C7), the four features (C21), the provider selection (C9) and the credential strike (C43).** A worker then executes only hooks the fleet authored, so trusting them is not a widening — it is trusting our own artifact, which is what D-065's guardrail already is. Tier 1.7 is satisfied on its own terms: auto-approve still never exceeds the worker's worktree, and the pane's executable surface **narrows**, because an operator hook that would have been merged in now is not. **The orchestrator is the exception, as always:** it inherits the operator's hooks untouched, gets no bypass, and keeps its own trust decisions — it is their own pane and there is a human there to answer the prompt. Cost, stated: an operator's own `PreToolUse` hook does not run on worker seats; that is a real loss of a real feature and it is the price of the fence. Order of attack in #46: spend **one bounded probe** on the binary's unverified fourth trust state `managed` (beside `allowManagedHooksOnly`), which would trust *exactly our hook* by provenance rather than trusting a document — strictly better if it works, and #45 found it as string-level evidence only. Fall back to own-the-array-plus-`bypass_hook_trust` if it does not. **Both carriers named here fell to C50 (#46), while the reasoning above stood.** `managed` is settled *negative*: it is named by `hooks.managed_dir`, a **requirements**-layer field, and every requirements layer this build reads is machine-global and not writable by a fleet placing panes as the operator — disqualifying before measurement, since the guardrail's command line embeds *that pane's* roots and one global dir cannot serve five workers with five root sets. And `bypass_hook_trust` is **accepted in four spellings and ignored in all of them** — it is an app-server newThread override, not a key the TUI reads, so C48's attribution of it was wrong. The carrier is therefore the flag; the decision it serves is unchanged, because C49 was never about the carrier. Reversal: a build where hook trust can be established for a single hook without a live vendor call — a documented hash, or `managed` proving out — at which point FLEETOR need not own the array and the operator's hooks can come back.
- **C50. C49 ships, and its carrier is the CLI flag rather than the config twin — because the config twin does not exist in this build.** #46, implementing C49 (C7, C9, C21, C43, C46, C47, C48; D-065, Tier 1.7). **The `managed` probe is settled and negative.** `managed` is reachable only through `hooks.managed_dir`, and that field lives in a **requirements** layer — `/etc/codex/requirements.toml` (absent on this machine, root-owned), macOS MDM, or cloud. All three are machine-global and none is writable by a fleet that places panes as the operator, so even a working `managed` could not carry five workers' five different root sets; the guardrail's command line embeds the pane id and that pane's roots. Measured as well as reasoned: a hooks dir named from a `CODEX_HOME/requirements.toml`, with `hooks.json`, `hooks.toml` and `config.toml` spellings inside it, left the hook unrun and the out-of-worktree write on disk. **The config twin is falsified as a shipping mechanism.** C48 read `bypass_hook_trust` out of the binary and inferred `-c bypass_hook_trust=true`; four spellings were driven through `hook_probe.py` — the key in `config.toml`, the same key as `-c`, and the nested `hooks.bypass_hook_trust` and `features.bypass_hook_trust` — and **all four are accepted without error and ignored**, the failure mode this arc keeps meeting. The literal is real and the attribution was wrong: the binary's own string is `` `bypass_hook_trust` override must be a boolean `` and it sits in `app-server/`, so it is a **newThread override for the app-server protocol**, not a config key the TUI a fleet pane runs reads. Two document-resident trust routes fell in the same run: `hooks.state."<key>" = { state = "managed" }` and the same with `"trusted"` and no hash. The bypass flag was the positive control in that run and fired, which is what makes those four readings measurements rather than a broken instrument. **So the bypass ships in argv**, from `Harness::command_args` on a seat FLEETOR drives and never on the operator's own. **What makes that narrow is that it runs second:** `install_guardrail` replaces a fenced pane's **whole `hooks` table** — every event, not just `PreToolUse`, since the bypass does not distinguish between events, and the inherited `hooks.state` record with it — leaving exactly one entry, the fleet's write guardrail. The vendor's own help for the flag reads *"Intended only for automation that already vets hook sources"*; vetting the sources is precisely what owning the table does. **The surface narrows and it is asserted rather than assumed:** the same seed is measured before and after and a fenced pane comes out able to execute strictly fewer hooks than the snapshot carried. **The boolean is `Seed::operators_own_seat` and no new seat concept was introduced** — it is added to `GuardrailPlacement` and to `command_args`, handed down from the four `place_*` arms. It is deliberately **not** derived from `permission_mode`: the evaluator and the Critic carry a posture *and* are seats FLEETOR drives, so inferring one from the other would have handed the orchestrator's answer to two panes that must not have it. **Measured at the vendor, which is the whole ticket:** a worker seeded through `Harness::seed_config_dir`, guarded through `Harness::install_guardrail`, and run with the argv `Harness::command_args` composed, is **refused an out-of-worktree write under both binaries** (C47) — `/opt/homebrew/bin/codex` and the PATH `codex` — with two witnesses, the absent file and the journal line naming `pane="worker-1"`. Zero tokens: `examples/codex-spike/hook_probe.py` is the model, a loopback provider answering with a canned `exec_command`, and the seeded `config.toml` is used **byte for byte** with the provider redirect and the sandbox relaxation delivered as `-c` session flags instead of edits. **The negative control is the load-bearing arm and it reproduced C47 in passing:** with the bypass removed from the argv and nothing else changed, the same write **lands** under the vendor binary — so the refusal is attributable — and does **not** land under the cmux shim, because the shim injects the flag itself (C48). The arm therefore requires the control on the resolved vendor binary and reports it as inconclusive on a wrapper, rather than pretending both readings say the same thing. **#31's Activity-feed Error is removed on fenced seats only, and only because the guardrail fires**; the orchestrator keeps it, keeps the merge, keeps its own hooks and gets no bypass, because there is a human there to answer codex's prompt and because trusting that document would trust something FLEETOR did not write. **Cost, stated rather than hidden** (C21's rule): an operator's own `PreToolUse` hook does not run on a worker, and they get an Activity feed line naming the events and counts that were dropped. **Not reopened:** Tier 1.7 — no root moves, the roots still arrive already computed on `GuardrailPlacement` and no `roots` field went on the spec. Codex is still unregistered; `REGISTERED` still holds one harness (#33, C25). Reversal: a build that gates hooks by document rather than by entry, a documented hash, or a per-pane managed layer — any of which retires both the flag and FLEETOR's ownership of the array, and C49's reversal clause applies unchanged.

- **C51. Checkpoint 14 for codex is two computed trust keys, discovered on `Seed` and written through the override announcer.** #30, implementing C34 (C6, C16, C17, C21, C31, C34, C39, C47; Tier 1.7, D-034). **The rule, as measured and now as implemented:** codex resolves trust by a two-candidate exact lookup, so a pane's seeded `CODEX_HOME` carries `[projects."<canonicalized cwd>"]` *and* `[projects."<canonicalized main repository>"]`, deduplicated where they are the same path. `trust_candidates` is the computation and it lives in `placement::codex` rather than on `HarnessSpec`, because the number of candidates is a fact about the pane's cwd and not one of the fourteen answers — `exact_path_match` stays `true` and C16's open question is closed rather than re-opened. **The second key arrives as a value, on `Seed::main_repository`, filled by `Seed::new` from `placement::main_repository` — the parent of `git rev-parse --path-format=absolute --git-common-dir`.** The default that lost was computing it at the four `place_*` arms, which C39 already ruled against, and the alternative that lost was `--show-toplevel`: inside a linked worktree it returns the worktree, and a key for the worktree trusts the worktree root and *not one directory below it* (C34, rows 10–12), so the obvious computation produces the key that gates every worker that `cd`s into a subdirectory. `Seed` therefore loses `Copy` and gains one owned `PathBuf`, and `Seed::new` shells to git — which is not a *process read* in `placement`'s sense, since it derives a fact from the path it was handed and a test pointed at a scratch cwd can no more reach the operator's repository than their `~/.codex`. `Seed::in_repository` is the fabricated half, so the two-key branch is unit-testable without building a worktree. **The trust row is written with `set_owned` rather than `set_path`, which is the second half of the ticket:** an operator's explicit `trust_level = "untrusted"` for the target was being overwritten in silence, and it now gets a `Warn` naming the key, the value they set and the value it became, following `prompts.rs`'s conditional shape so a machine with no opinion stays quiet. The override itself stands and that is a judgement rather than a bug — a pane with no trust record parks on a modal dialog nothing can answer from this side of the pty while every `fleet send` reports `accepted` (L1, D-034) — so C21's rule applies: overriding loudly and overriding silently are different things. **Not a widening of Tier 1.7:** the record lives in the pane's own disposable `CODEX_HOME`, `~/.codex` is never written, and what a trusted project may do is still the seatbelt's answer. **Measured behaviourally, under both binaries (C47):** `tests/codex_trust_gate.rs` boots a real pane in a *subdirectory* of a real linked worktree and it reaches its composer, while the control — the same seed with `--show-toplevel`'s answer alone — gates; three states, with "neither marker" a failure. Those panes are spawned under `BringUp::AtOnce` deliberately, because #42's wake presses `\r` and `1. Yes, continue` is the gate's pre-selected option, so a woken pane would answer the dialog and destroy the control. **Unmeasured, and named rather than assumed:** what that keypress does to a *production* gated codex pane — whether it dismisses the gate, and what it writes where — is untried; the probe that would settle it is `trust_probe.py` with a wake in front of it. Codex is still unregistered; `REGISTERED` still holds one harness (#33, C25). Reversal: a build that resolves trust by an ancestor walk after all, which retires the second key and `main_repository` with it.
- **C52. Codex's typing profile is Claude Code's, measured rather than inherited — and checkpoint 10 now asserts the production translation instead of the canonical spelling.** #32, implementing C22 and closing C35's named gap (C10, C26, C28, C35, C37, C44, C47; D-034, D-081; Tier 1.4, Tier 1.5). **The gap is 30 ms and there is no codex number to encode.** C22 measured 6 s startup + 0.6 s post-paste submitting nothing and 12 s + 1.5 s submitting, and #24 withdrew any claim about which of the two was load-bearing (C37). A multi-line message delivered into a pane #42 woke is submitted at the profile's **30 ms** gap — *twenty times shorter than the gap in C22's failing row* — so the post-paste gap was never the discriminator, and `submit_gap_ms` stays at 30 because that is what was measured to work rather than because it was the value already there. This is a reading of C22, not a re-run of it: the 6 s arm was not reproduced. **The framing claim is sharper than "a paste arrives":** the message is *three lines*, and every line arrives in **one** request body — which is what `bracketed_paste` is a field to buy, since unbracketed the first newline submits and one message becomes three turns, three requests, the first carrying one line. **`submit_bytes` is load-bearing and that is a control rather than an assumption:** codex ships `disable_paste_burst`, so a TUI that submitted on `paste_end` by itself would read identically under any submit byte including none — and the same paste framed with `submit_bytes: &[]` **paints into the composer and never reaches the wire**, which is exactly the lie the seam refuses (Tier 1.5): bytes delivered, `write_paste` returning `Ok`, and nothing submitted. `tests/codex_typing.rs`, two arms, ~7.6 s, zero tokens against `probe.py`'s loopback provider; the pane, the capture server and the paint reader moved to a shared `tests/codex_pane/mod.rs` so #42's instrument and this one cannot drift into two, because one file's control vouching for another file's arm through a second copy is the failure a shared instrument prevents. **C47 was settled by measurement rather than by argument:** both arms pass identically through the cmux shim and through `/opt/homebrew/bin/codex` resolved absolutely, in the same 7.6 s — a typing arm measures the TUI's input handling and not a flag, so `on_path` stays and a machine that installs codex elsewhere still runs it. **Checkpoint 10's end-to-end half had the wrong subject and now has the right one.** It compared `fleetor_core::Command::keystrokes()` — *harness-free*, the canonical word the fleet allowlisted — against the harness's spelling, so it asserted what the fleet decided and not what the harness types, and held only because Claude Code's table is the identity map (C35). `Pass` gains `echo_of_a_command`, the sibling `echo_of_a_paste` already was (C28): `keystrokes()`, the exact string `hub.rs` hands `deliver`, goes into `PaneRegistry::write_command` over a real pty with the stand-in pane program on the far end, and the far end must read **this harness's spelling**, argument intact, slash in column 0, as one submitted line. **Falsified before it was trusted:** with Claude Code's table mutated to `/clear`→`/reset` the old assertion fails and the new one passes, and with `spell` additionally returning its argument the new one goes red naming both spellings — so it tests the translation and not the table. **Nothing was added between `fleet send` and a pty.** `writable`, `write`, `write_paste`, `write_command` and `type_framed` are untouched, the root workspace's **226** hub and CLI tests pass unchanged, and `submit_gap_ms` is still D-034's only delay. **No startup-wait field exists, placeholder or otherwise:** `TypingProfile` is still `bracketed_paste`, `paste_start`, `paste_end`, `submit_bytes`, `submit_gap_ms` (C26), and readiness is still `BringUp` on the spawn path (C44). Codex is still unregistered; `REGISTERED` still holds one harness (#33, C25). **Cost, stated:** two more real interactive panes on a machine with `codex` installed, and checkpoint 10 now spawns one stand-in pane per allowlisted command instead of asserting a string. **Not measured, and named rather than assumed:** whether a gap *shorter* than 30 ms also submits — nobody needs that number, and looking for it would be tuning a value the invariant already sanctions. **Reversal:** a vendor build whose composer submits on `paste_end` alone, which retires the control arm and makes `submit_bytes` unfalsifiable — `tests/codex_typing.rs`'s second arm goes red first and says so.
- **C53. Registering codex found seven things, six of them reshapes that landed and one of them a gate that did not — so the reshapes ship and the flip is a follow-up on #39.** #33 (C15, C20, C25, C34, C36, C39, C41, C49, M25; D-030, D-052, D-054, D-062; Tier 1.4, Tier 1.7). **The flip was made, the suite ran two passes, and the flip was reverted.** `REGISTERED` still holds one harness and `codex_is_implemented_and_still_not_registered` still says so, because a known-red test in the tree makes every later agent's `cargo test` gate meaningless — they cannot tell their failure from a standing one. What the second pass produced is the ticket's actual product and all of it is here. **Six checkpoint assertions were shaped around the one harness they were written against, and two of the six were not diagnosed in advance — the second pass was the only way to find them.** (1) **The three key lists** (`Posture::sandbox_keys`, `Credentials::provider_keys`, `Outbound::reachability_keys`, C36) were asserted by searching the config dir for the **whole dotted key** as a substring, which is only ever true of a document that renders a path flat; a seeder writes nesting as structure, so codex's `model_providers.fleetor.base_url` arrives as a `[model_providers.fleetor]` header and a `base_url` leaf and the dotted key is nowhere in the file. Replaced by `conformance::config_states`, which is **strictly stronger than what it replaced and deliberately not "match the last segment"**: every segment of the path present, the leaf and the value on one line, and both in the *same file* — the old form called `files_containing` twice and never compared the answers, so a key in one file and its value in another satisfied it. (2) **Checkpoint 6 refused `seeds_from_operator: true` outright**, with a `panic!` telling the reader to give the driver an operator directory first; the driver placed against a machine with nothing, which is Claude Code's own answer (fresh directory, login through the keychain). Replaced by a second placement against a fabricated operator `HOME` under the pass root, asserting the two properties the seam can see: configuration is still relocated **into the layout** (a snapshot is a copy, never a pane pointed at the operator's tree), and **the operator's tree is byte-identical before and after** — the snapshot is a read. Named rather than assumed: *which* subdirectory of a `HOME` a harness reads is not one of the fourteen, so the suite cannot plant a marker there; that half is `placement::codex`'s own tests, the discipline checkpoints 11 and 13 already apply to their gaps. (3) **Checkpoint 14 parsed the trust file with `serde_json::from_str`** — but `trust_file` names a *file* and its format is the vendor's, so a correct harness whose document is TOML failed on the first line. It now reads JSON or TOML into one `serde_json::Value`, so `record_under` and `is_affirmative` stay one implementation and the encoding stops being a property of the seam. (4) **Checkpoint 14's `exact_path_match` negative was false for a harness with two trust candidates.** It asserted no record under the repository root, reasoning that such a row is one the harness never reads; codex resolves a directory against the canonicalized cwd *and* the git root it resolves to, and for a linked worktree that root is the main repository, which it does read (C34, row 11). Replaced by the containment property it was reaching for: every directory the seed recorded a trust answer for is inside the layout or the target — which catches a harness trusting the operator's home or `/`, and the old form did not. (5) **Checkpoint 13 required `subdir` non-empty**, which is Claude Code's `projects/`; the empty string is a real answer meaning the configuration directory itself, and what a Critic reading a run cold actually needs is `file_ext` and `format`, which still may not be empty. (6) **The driver's `echo_of` waited for the stand-in's reply from byte zero**, so a harness whose bring-up types into the pane before it is announced (`BringUp::AfterWaking`, C44) had already produced one: checkpoints 9 and 10 would have returned on the *wake's* echo, before the write under test produced anything. `wait_until` takes a floor immediately before the write. **`Harness::command_args` grew a `Seat<'_>` rather than a fourth argument**, following C39's rule for `Seed` — checkpoint 3 declares two model channels and only `Posture::model_env` had a call site, so `model_flag` was unwired and a harness naming its model in argv could not have passed checkpoint 3 at all. Claude Code reads `Seat::model` and produces nothing, which is the honest answer for a harness whose model travels beside the command. **`PaneSpec` carries the harness on `Orch { harness }` and `Worker { slot, harness }`, and that makes C15 structural rather than checked**: the evaluator and the Critic have no field to hold one, so a later ticket that offers a judge a harness must change the type to do it — stated plainly because "the judges are not offered codex" being a *shape* rather than a policy is the whole value of it. `fleet.rs` names `claude_code()` at the one call site rather than defaulting inside `PaneSpec`, so #35's picker is one line and the fleet cannot acquire a second harness by accident on the way there. **M25 is asserted rather than relied on**: the roster is checked on the **serialized** answer for every registered harness's name *and* program, so a field added to `PaneEntry` fails it where a test written against the fields it knows about would not. **The one that is not a reshape, and the reason the flip is deferred: checkpoint 13 refuses a harness whose transcript is a live database, and it is right to.** `harvest_transcripts` takes transcripts with a plain rename; codex's is WAL-mode sqlite, which is `db` + `-wal` + `-shm`, so renaming the `.sqlite` alone leaves committed transactions behind — rotation running before any pane exists makes such a copy untorn, not complete. **#39 owns the mechanism** (a backup API or `VACUUM INTO`, and a `rusqlite` dependency `src-tauri` does not yet have), and the reshaped assertion is what will gate it. Reading the flag to *skip* such a harness was rejected in `runs.rs` before this ticket and stays rejected: it archives nothing while the run looks ordinary. **Registration is therefore a two-line follow-up — `harness::REGISTERED` and the pin — gated on #39, and is not forgotten.** **Cost, stated:** three assertion paths ship exercised by nothing until the flip — `config_states` (Claude Code's three key lists are empty), checkpoint 6's operator-installation arm, and checkpoint 14's TOML branch — which is the same deliberate shape C36 named for the three key lists themselves, and the alternative is writing them at the flip with no measurement behind them. **Not established:** whether codex passes checkpoint 13 once #39 lands; the other thirteen were observed green over both harnesses and this one never ran to completion for codex. **Reversal:** #39 landing and checkpoint 13 still refusing codex — which would mean the harvest's mechanism does not cover this transcript, and the flip stays deferred with a second reason.
